The author of al-Takhmīr says: “The lām (لام (ل)) is for indication itself. Sometimes the indication points to an individual already familiar to the addressee, and sometimes to a class. Its meaning is one in every case. Understand this: people's errors concerning it are considerable, and it is a point of doctrinal grammatical importance.”
I say: it consequently requires something previously available to be indicated. Whenever the discourse contains something that can serve as such a referent in any way, that is selected. Al-Bazdawī says: “The defining lām (لام (ل)) refers back to what is known: a thing is mentioned and then returned to, the second being the first. An example is our scholars' ruling about someone who acknowledges a thousand with a particular qualification, then acknowledges it again with the same qualification: the second is the first. If each expression is indefinite, there is disagreement over whether unity of the sitting is a condition for the second to be identical with the first. According to Abū Ḥanīfa it is; according to Abū Yūsuf it is not.”
The author of al-Muṭliʿ transmits from al-Farrāʾ that when the Arabs mention an indefinite noun and then repeat an indefinite noun like it, they become two: “When you earn a dirham, spend a dirham.” The second differs from the first. If they repeat it in definite form, it is the same. Al-Zajjāj states something similar.
Al-Sayyid says in al-Amālī: “Hardship is definite and ease indefinite because, when a noun is repeated indefinitely, the second differs from the first: ‘A man came to me, and I said to a man such-and-such.’ The same applies when the first is definite and the second indefinite: ‘The man arrived, and I said to a man such-and-such.’ If the first is indefinite and the second definite, the second is the first; likewise when a definite noun follows a definite noun: ‘The man arrived, and I honored the man.’ Hence Ibn ʿAbbās said, ‘One hardship will not overcome two eases.’”



al-Sharḥ · Verses 7–8: continuing in worship

K IV:770–772 · T XVI:492–503

Q 94:7–8

فَإِذَا فَرَغْتَ فَٱنصَبْ ﴿7﴾ وَإِلَىٰ رَبِّكَ فَٱرْغَب ﴿8﴾

7. So when thou art relieved, still toil 8. And strive to please thy Lord

K094.06 · Verses 7–8: continuing in worship



“So when you have finished, exert yourself; and direct your longing toward your Lord.”
If you ask how “so when you have finished, exert yourself” connects with what precedes, I answer: after enumerating His earlier blessings and promising those to come, He urged him to gratitude, diligence in worship, and exertion in it: to join one act of worship to another, pursue them successively, and take care that none of his times be empty of them. Whenever he finishes one act of worship, he follows it with another.
Ibn ʿAbbās said: when you finish your prayer, exert yourself in supplication. Al-Ḥasan said: when you finish warfare, exert yourself in worship. Mujāhid said: when you finish your worldly business, exert yourself in prayer. Al-Shaʿbī saw a man lifting a stone and said: “This is not what someone with time to spare has been commanded to do.” Sitting idle without occupation, or occupying oneself with what benefits neither one's religion nor one's worldly affairs, is evidence of foolish judgment, feeble understanding, and heedlessness taking control. ʿUmar said: “I dislike seeing any of you idle and aimless, engaged neither in work for this world nor in work for the next.”
Abū al-Sammāl read fariġta, with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) on the rāʾ (راء (ر)); this is not an eloquent usage. Among novel interpretations is the report that one of the Rāfiḍa read fa-nṣib (فانْصِب), with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) on the ṣād (صاد (ص)), meaning: appoint ʿAlī to the imamate. Were this valid for the Rāfiḍī, it would equally be valid for a Nāṣibī to read it in this fashion and make it a command to naṣb (نصب), which is hatred and hostility toward ʿAlī.
“And direct your longing toward your Lord”: make Him exclusively the object of your longing; ask only for His bounty, relying upon Him. Fa-raġġib is also read: encourage people to seek what is with Him.
The Prophet is reported to have said: “Whoever recites ‘Have We not expanded,’ it is as though he came to me while I was grieving and relieved my grief.”



T094.11 · The question about indefiniteness; successive acts of devotion (501–502)

فما معنى هذا التنكير؟ 

On “then what does this indefiniteness mean?”: the fāʾ (فاء (ف)) indicates an objection. If the two eases mean what you have explained in either of the two ways, they ought to be definite; why, then, are they indefinite?
On “when you finish your prayer, exert yourself in supplication”: this is coordinated with “when he finishes one act of worship, he follows it with another.” Both “you have finished” and “exert yourself” are unrestricted. They may remain unrestricted: when you finish one act of worship, follow it with another. Or they may be specified as prayer and supplication, because prayer is the best of the acts of worship and supplication is its marrow; or as warfare and worship, as in the saying, “We have returned from the lesser struggle to the greater struggle”; or as worldly business and prayer, because ‘having free time’ is most often used of worldly affairs. An example is the report, “[Take advantage of] your free time before you become occupied.” This transmission from Mujāhid is mentioned in Sharḥ (شرح) al-Sunna.
On “idle and aimless” (fāriġan (فارغًا) sabahlalan (سبهللًا)): al-Nihāya says: “In ʿUmar's report, ‘I dislike seeing any of you aimless, engaged neither in work for this world nor in work for the next,’ the indefiniteness in ‘world’ and ‘next world’ goes back to the noun annexed to them, namely ‘work’: neither in any work belonging to this world's affairs nor in any work belonging to the next world's affairs. One says jāʾa yamshī (جاء يمشي) sabahlalan (سبهللًا) when someone comes and goes idly, engaged in nothing.”



T094.12 · Exclusive longing and the unusual conjunction (503)

واجعل رغبتك إليع خصوصا

On “make Him exclusively the object of your longing”: particularization is conveyed by placing the prepositional phrase before the verb. Al-Sayyid says in al-Amālī: “The fāʾ (فاء (ف)) occurs together with the wāw (واو (و)), and ilā is governed by what comes after the fāʾ (فاء (ف)). Similarly, in ‘and your garments—purify them’ (74:4), what precedes the fāʾ (فاء (ف)) is made accusative by what follows it. This is among the remarkable features of their speech: ordinarily fāʾ (فاء (ف)) coordinates, introduces a response, or introduces something resembling a response, such as the predicate of an incomplete noun—that is, a relative pronoun whose relative clause is verbal. Here it falls outside its assigned function.”
The sūrah is complete, by God's praise and aid. God suffices us; how excellent a trustee He is.



al-Tīn · Heading and verses

K IV:773–774 · T XVI:504–508

Q 95:1–8

بِّسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ وَٱلتِّينِ وَٱلزَّيْتُونِ ﴿1﴾ وَطُورِ سِينِينَ ﴿2﴾ وَهَـٰذَا ٱلْبَلَدِ ٱلْأَمِينِ ﴿3﴾ لَقَدْ خَلَقْنَا ٱلْإِنسَـٰنَ فِىٓ أَحْسَنِ تَقْوِيمٍ ﴿4﴾ ثُمَّ رَدَدْنَـٰهُ أَسْفَلَ سَـٰفِلِينَ ﴿5﴾ إِلَّا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّـٰلِحَـٰتِ فَلَهُمْ أَجْرٌ غَيْرُ مَمْنُونٍ ﴿6﴾ فَمَا يُكَذِّبُكَ بَعْدُ بِٱلدِّينِ ﴿7﴾ أَلَيْسَ ٱللَّهُ بِأَحْكَمِ ٱلْحَـٰكِمِينَ ﴿8﴾

1. By the fig and the olive 2. By Mount Sinai 3. And by this land made safe 4. Surely We created man of the best stature 5. Then we reduced him to the lowest of the low 6. Save those who believe and do good works, and theirs is a reward unfailing 7. So who henceforth will give the lie to thee about the judgment 8. Is not Allah the most conclusive of all judges

K095.01 · Heading and verses [IV:773]



Meccan; eight verses. [The DKA heading additionally places its revelation after al-Burūj.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

By the fig and the olive, (1) and Mount Sīnīn (سينين), (2) and this secure city: (3) We have indeed created the human being in the finest proportion; (4) then We returned him to the lowest of the low, (5) except those who believe and do righteous deeds: theirs is a reward unceasing. (6) So what makes you deny the requital after this? (7) Is God not the wisest of judges? (8)



al-Tīn · The fig and olive

K IV:773–774 · T XVI:504–508

Q 95:1

بِّسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ وَٱلتِّينِ وَٱلزَّيْتُونِ ﴿1﴾

1. By the fig and the olive

K095.02 · The fig and olive [IV:773]



He swears by these two because they are remarkable among the varieties of fruit-bearing trees. It is related that a dish of figs was presented to God’s Messenger. He ate some and said to his companions: “Eat! If I were to say that a fruit had descended from Paradise, I would say it was this one, because the fruit of Paradise has no stones. So eat them: they stop haemorrhoids and help against gout.”

Muʿādh ibn Jabal passed an olive tree, took a twig from it, and cleaned his teeth with it. He said: “I heard God’s Messenger say, ‘What an excellent tooth-stick is the olive, from the blessed tree! It sweetens the mouth and removes decay.’ And I heard him say, ‘It is my tooth-stick and the tooth-stick of the prophets before me.’” From Ibn ʿAbbās: “It is this fig of yours and this olive of yours.”

It is also said that they are two mountains of the Holy Land called in Syriac Ṭūr (طور) Tīnā and Ṭūr (طور) Zaytā, because they are places where figs and olives grow. Another view makes “the fig” the mountains between Ḥulwān and Hamadhān, and “the olive” the mountains of Syria, because these fruits grow there: as though He said, “By the places where figs and olives grow.”



T095.01 · Fruit-stones and the properties of figs and olives [XVI:504–505]

بلا عجم

Meccan; eight verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

On “without stones”: it is transmitted both with a quiescent jīm (جيم (ج)), ʿajm (عَجْم), and with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)), ʿajam (عَجَم). Dīwān al-Adab has: “ʿAjam (عَجَم), with the middle consonant vowelled, means fruit-stones”; it does not list ʿajm (عَجْم) in this sense. Al-Jawharī says: “Ordinary people say ʿajm (عَجْم), with the jīm (جيم (ج)) quiescent.”

On “they stop haemorrhoids,” the Qāḍī [al-Bayḍāwī] says: “The fig is a pleasant fruit with no waste. As food it is light and quickly digested, and as a medicine it has many benefits: it loosens the bowels, dissolves phlegm, cleanses the kidneys, removes gravel from the bladder, opens obstructions of the liver and spleen, and fattens the body. The olive is a fruit, a condiment, and a medicine. Its oil is light, very beneficial, and pleasant; yet it sometimes grows where it contains no oil, as in the mountains.”



al-Tīn · Sīnīn, the secure city, and the blessed places

K IV:773–774 · T XVI:504–508

Q 95:2–3

وَطُورِ سِينِينَ ﴿2﴾ وَهَـٰذَا ٱلْبَلَدِ ٱلْأَمِينِ ﴿3﴾

2. By Mount Sinai 3. And by this land made safe

K095.03 · Sīnīn, the secure city, and the blessed places [IV:773–774]



Al-ṭūr, “the mountain,” is put in a genitive construction with Sīnīn (سينين), which is the locality. Sīnūn (سينون) resembles Yabrūn (يبرون) in permitting declension with wāw (واو (و)) and yāʾ (ياء (ي)), or retention of the yāʾ (ياء (ي)) while the nūn (نون (ن)) takes the case vowels.

“The city” is Mecca—may God protect it. Amīn (أمين) derives from amuna al-rajulu amānatan (أمانة) fa-huwa amīn (أمين), “the man proved trustworthy, and so is trustworthy.” Ammān (أمّان) is also said, just as karrām (كرّام) is used for karīm (كريم). Its trustworthiness consists in protecting whoever enters it, as a trustworthy person protects what is entrusted to him. It may also be a faʿīl (فعيل) form with a passive sense, from aminahu, since the city is secure from calamities. Thus it is described as secure in His saying, “a secure sanctuary” [28:57], meaning one possessing security.

The purpose of swearing by these things is to disclose the honour of the blessed places, and the good and blessing manifested there through the residence of prophets and righteous people. The place where figs and olives grow is Abraham’s place of migration and Jesus’ birthplace and place of upbringing. The Mount is the place from which Moses was called. Mecca is the location of the House that is guidance for all peoples, and God’s Messenger’s birthplace and the place of his commissioning.



T095.02 · Tooth decay and trustworthy places [XVI:505]

ويذهب بالحفرة

On “removes decay”: one says ḥafirat asnānuhu ḥafaran (حَفِرَت أسنانه حَفَرًا) when the sockets of his teeth—that is, their roots—have deteriorated. One also says ḥafarat ḥafran (حَفَرَت حَفْرًا); ḥafra (حَفْرة) denotes a single occurrence.

On “and so is amīn (أمين); ammān (أمّان) is also said”: that is, they say, “in a trustworthy place.”



al-Tīn · Finest proportion and the lowest condition

K IV:773–774 · T XVI:504–508

Q 95:4–5

لَقَدْ خَلَقْنَا ٱلْإِنسَـٰنَ فِىٓ أَحْسَنِ تَقْوِيمٍ ﴿4﴾ ثُمَّ رَدَدْنَـٰهُ أَسْفَلَ سَـٰفِلِينَ ﴿5﴾

4. Surely We created man of the best stature 5. Then we reduced him to the lowest of the low

K095.04 · Finest proportion and the lowest condition [IV:774]



“In the finest proportion”: in the finest adjustment of his shape and form, and the most harmonious arrangement of his limbs. Then, when he failed to give thanks for the blessing of that beautiful, upright, well-proportioned creation, his final outcome was that We returned him lower than those low in constitution and composition: uglier in form than the ugly, and most deformed in creation. These are the inhabitants of the Fire. Or: lower than the lowest inhabitants of its descending levels.

Or: after that proportioning and beautification, We returned him lower than those lowest in beauty of form and shape, reversing his constitution. His back, once straight, became bent; his hair, once black, turned white; his skin, once tender, dried and wrinkled; his hearing and sight, once sharp, grew dull. Everything about him changed: his gait became shuffling, his voice faint, his strength weakness, and his mental alertness senility.

ʿAbd Allāh (الله) [ibn Masʿūd] read asfala al-sāfilīn (السافلين), with the definite article: “the lowest of the low.”



T095.03 · The vocabulary of ageing [XVI:506]

تشنن

On “dried and wrinkled,” al-Asās says: “Tashannana jilduhu min al-haram (تشنّن جلده من الهرم) means his skin shrivelled and dried from old age. One says, ‘an old man like a worn-out waterskin.’”

On “tender,” baḍḍ (بَضّ), with the single-dotted bāʾ (باء (ب)) and dotted ḍād (ضاد (ض)): al-Asās says: “Al-Aṣmaʿī said: abyaḍ (أبيض) baḍḍ (بَضّ) means intensely white. Al-Mubarrad said: it means a person with fine skin on which everything leaves an impression. One says imraʾa (امرأة) ghaḍḍa (غَضّة) baḍḍa (بَضّة), a fresh, tender-skinned woman.”

On “his gait became shuffling”: dalīf (دليف) is slow walking. Al-Asās says: “The old man and the fettered man dalafa dalīfan (دليفًا) wa-dulūfan (ودلوفًا); this is a little more than creeping.”

On “senility”: kharaf, with the middle consonant vowelled, means deterioration of the mind.



al-Tīn · The exception

K IV:773–774 · T XVI:504–508

Q 95:6

إِلَّا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّـٰلِحَـٰتِ فَلَهُمْ أَجْرٌ غَيْرُ مَمْنُونٍ ﴿6﴾

6. Save those who believe and do good works, and theirs is a reward unfailing

K095.05 · The exception [IV:774]



If you ask how the exception works on the two interpretations, I answer: on the first it is a connected exception, and its connection is plain. On the second it is disconnected: “But those among the aged who were righteous have an abiding, uninterrupted reward”—for their obedience, their patience under God’s trial of old age and decrepitude, their endurance of hardships, and their continuing to worship despite their faltering ability to rise.



T095.04 · The two interpretations and the exception [XVI:506–507]

فكيف الاستثناء على المذهبين

On “how does the exception work on the two interpretations?”: someone has taken this to mean the Ḥijāzī and Tamīmī grammatical schools. That is not correct. It means the two interpretations just mentioned, as the answer and the introductory fāʾ (فاء (ف)) in the question show.

On “on the first it is a connected exception”: that is, when being returned to “the lowest of the low” means being returned lower than those low in constitution and composition, namely the inhabitants of the Fire, or lower than the lowest inhabitants of its descending levels. Al-Wāḥidī reports from Mujāhid: “‘Then We returned him’ to the Fire. The Fire is the lowest of the low because Jahannam has levels one beneath another. He then makes the exception, ‘except those who believe and do righteous deeds’: these are not returned to the Fire.”

On “on the second it is disconnected”: that is, when “the lowest of the low” means being returned lower than those lowest in beauty of form and shape. Hence he says: “But those among the aged who were righteous have an abiding reward.”



al-Tīn · Who is addressed, and what makes him a denier?

K IV:773–774 · T XVI:504–508

Q 95:7–8

فَمَا يُكَذِّبُكَ بَعْدُ بِٱلدِّينِ ﴿7﴾ أَلَيْسَ ٱللَّهُ بِأَحْكَمِ ٱلْحَـٰكِمِينَ ﴿8﴾

7. So who henceforth will give the lie to thee about the judgment 8. Is not Allah the most conclusive of all judges

K095.06 · Who is addressed, and what makes him a denier? [IV:774–775]



If you ask who is addressed by “So what makes you deny?”, I answer: the human being is addressed through a shift of person. That is: what makes you a liar on account of the requital and your denial of it after this proof? You lie when you deny recompense, because everyone who denies the truth is a liar. What, then, compels you to become a liar through denying recompense? The bāʾ (باء (ب)) is like that in His saying, “those who take him as their patron and those who, because of him, associate others [with God]” [16:100].

The meaning is that you can see no clearer proof of the Creator’s power than the human being’s creation from a drop, his proportioning as a well-formed human, his gradual progress through stages of growth until he reaches maturity and full stature, and then his reversal until he reaches the most abject age. Whoever has power to do all this with the human being is not incapable of bringing him back. So what, human being, is the cause of your denying recompense after this decisive proof?

It is also said that the address is to God’s Messenger.

“Is God not the wisest of judges?” is a threat to the unbelievers: He will judge them as they deserve. It is related from the Prophet that, when he recited it, he said: “Indeed; and I am among those who bear witness to that.”



T095.05 · The causal bāʾ [XVI:507]

{والذين هم به مشركون

On “‘those who, because of him, associate others’ [16:100]”: that is, because of Satan they associate others with God. The bāʾ (باء (ب)) in bihi (به) is not the complement of mushrikūn (مشركون), “associating others”; its proper complement is omitted.



T095.06 · The alternative addressee and the force of the question [XVI:508]

وقيل: الخطاب لرسول الله? 

On “it is also said that the address is to God’s Messenger”: this is coordinated with “the human being is addressed.” On this interpretation there is no shift of person, and mā (ما) has the sense of man, “who”: who, then, calls you a liar, truthful and confirmed Messenger, regarding the true religion you have brought—or on account of religion—after the appearance of these proofs indicating your prophethood? “Is God not the wisest of judges?” He will judge between you and those who deny you.

If the address is taken to concern the human being, one should adopt the interpretation involving a shift of person, because of the preceding “We have indeed created the human being in the finest proportion,” and take the bāʾ (باء (ب)) as causal, since the human being is himself the denier. The meaning is: “Human being, what drives you to be a liar through denying recompense?” The expression both conveys wonder and arouses it. Having established that He created the human being in the finest proportion and then returned him to the most abject age, God has indicated the perfection of His power to originate and restore. He then asks why the human being denies recompense, because the cause of something wondered at is obscure. This is, as you see, manifest and clear. It is indicated by his saying, “So what, human being, is the cause of your denying recompense after this decisive proof?” On this interpretation, “Is God not the wisest of judges?” threatens the unbelievers, declaring that He judges them as they deserve.



T095.07 · The response to the final verse [XVI:508]

قال: "بلى وأنا على ذلك من الشاهدين"

On “he said, ‘Indeed; and I am among those who bear witness to that’”: the report is transmitted by al-Tirmidhī and Abū Dāwūd from Abū Hurayra, who said that God’s Messenger said: “Whoever among you recites ‘By the fig and the olive,’ and reaches ‘Is God not the wisest of judges?’, should say: ‘Indeed; and I am among those who bear witness to that.’”

End of the sūrah.



al-Tīn · Closing report

K IV:773–774 · T XVI:504–508

Q 95:1–8

بِّسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ وَٱلتِّينِ وَٱلزَّيْتُونِ ﴿1﴾ وَطُورِ سِينِينَ ﴿2﴾ وَهَـٰذَا ٱلْبَلَدِ ٱلْأَمِينِ ﴿3﴾ لَقَدْ خَلَقْنَا ٱلْإِنسَـٰنَ فِىٓ أَحْسَنِ تَقْوِيمٍ ﴿4﴾ ثُمَّ رَدَدْنَـٰهُ أَسْفَلَ سَـٰفِلِينَ ﴿5﴾ إِلَّا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّـٰلِحَـٰتِ فَلَهُمْ أَجْرٌ غَيْرُ مَمْنُونٍ ﴿6﴾ فَمَا يُكَذِّبُكَ بَعْدُ بِٱلدِّينِ ﴿7﴾ أَلَيْسَ ٱللَّهُ بِأَحْكَمِ ٱلْحَـٰكِمِينَ ﴿8﴾

1. By the fig and the olive 2. By Mount Sinai 3. And by this land made safe 4. Surely We created man of the best stature 5. Then we reduced him to the lowest of the low 6. Save those who believe and do good works, and theirs is a reward unfailing 7. So who henceforth will give the lie to thee about the judgment 8. Is not Allah the most conclusive of all judges

K095.07 · Closing report [IV:775]



From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites Sūrat ‘By the fig,’ God gives him two qualities: well-being and certainty as long as he remains in this world; and when he dies, God gives him a reward according to the number of those who have recited this sūrah.”



al-Qadr · Heading and verses

K IV:780–780 · T XVI:522–525

Q 97:1–5

بِّسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِنَّآ أَنزَلْنَـٰهُ فِى لَيْلَةِ ٱلْقَدْرِ ﴿1﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا لَيْلَةُ ٱلْقَدْرِ ﴿2﴾ لَيْلَةُ ٱلْقَدْرِ خَيْرٌ مِّنْ أَلْفِ شَهْرٍ ﴿3﴾ تَنَزَّلُ ٱلْمَلَـٰٓئِكَةُ وَٱلرُّوحُ فِيهَا بِإِذْنِ رَبِّهِم مِّن كُلِّ أَمْرٍ ﴿4﴾ سَلَـٰمٌ هِىَ حَتَّىٰ مَطْلَعِ ٱلْفَجْرِ ﴿5﴾

1. Lo! We revealed it on the Night of Predestination 2. Ah, what will convey unto thee what the Night of Power is 3. The Night of Power is better than a thousand months 4. The angels and the Spirit descend therein, by the permission of their Lord, with all decrees 5. (The night is) Peace until the rising of the dawn

K097.01 · Heading and verses [IV:780]



Meccan; also said to be Medinan. Five verses. [The DKA heading additionally places its revelation after ʿAbasa.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

Surely We sent it down on the Night of Decree. (1) And what will make you know what the Night of Decree is? (2) The Night of Decree is better than a thousand months. (3) The angels and the Spirit descend in it, by their Lord’s permission, for every matter. (4) Peace it is, until the rising of dawn. (5)



al-Qadr · The Qurʾān’s greatness and its descent

K IV:780–780 · T XVI:522–525

Q 97:1

بِّسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِنَّآ أَنزَلْنَـٰهُ فِى لَيْلَةِ ٱلْقَدْرِ ﴿1﴾

1. Lo! We revealed it on the Night of Predestination

K097.02 · The Qurʾān’s greatness and its descent [IV:780]



He magnifies the Qurʾān in three ways. First, He attributes its sending down to Himself and makes it His exclusive act, to the exclusion of anyone else. Second, He uses its pronoun instead of its explicit name, attesting its prominence and its having no need for attention to be called to it. Third, He elevates the rank of the time in which it was sent down.

It is related that it was sent down all at once on the Night of Decree, from the Preserved Tablet to the lowest heaven. Gabriel dictated it to the scribes; thereafter he brought it down to God’s Messenger in instalments over twenty-three years. From al-Shaʿbī: the meaning is, “We began sending it down on the Night of Decree.”



T097.01 · Exclusive agency and the night’s reflected honour [XVI:522–523]

وجعله مختصا به

Meccan; five verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful. [The embedded Kashshāf heading instead records disagreement about its place of revelation.]

On “and makes it His exclusive act”: he means that the construction belongs to the category of placing the semantic agent first, as in “I took care of your concern,” and “I fulfilled your need.” The choice of the plural form conveys a magnification beneath which every other magnification falls.

On “elevates the rank of the time in which it was sent down”: there is a subtle point here. He first says, “He magnifies the Qurʾān in three ways,” then says, “elevates the rank of the time.” The expected expression would be “elevates its rank by sending it down on this night.” He departs from that expression to indicate that the night was honoured by its descent within it, becoming possessed of importance and distinction. The Qurʾān’s own distinction and importance therefore follow all the more. He then rises further in elevating the night’s rank by saying, “And what will make you know what the Night of Decree is?”; higher still with “The Night of Decree is better than a thousand months”; and higher yet with “The angels and the Spirit descend in it.”



T097.02 · How “sending down” applies to speech [XVI:523]

روي أنه أنزل جملة واحدة

On “it is related that it was sent down all at once”: if you ask, “In your explanation of the preface you said that sending down means moving a thing from above to below, and that this belongs to bodies and is not literally realized in speech. Speech is therefore described by an attribute of its bearer because of its connection with him. This figurative use works when Gabriel brings the Qurʾān down to the Prophet, but how does it work for its descent from the Tablet to heaven, since that is without an intermediary?”—I answer: in that case sending down is borrowed from bodies and applied to meanings. The transfer of the Qurʾān from the Tablet to heaven, and its being established there, is likened to a body’s descent from a higher place to a lower, and it is said: “Surely We sent it down on the Night of Decree.”

On this basis, its manifestation in the visible world—that is, the Tablet—from the unseen world, which is the higher world, can also be explained as a descent. On the first interpretation it is a transferred expression without comparison, a majāz (مجاز) mursal; on the second it is a figurative expression preceded by a comparison.



al-Qadr · When the night occurs and why it is concealed

K IV:780–780 · T XVI:522–525

Q 97:1

بِّسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِنَّآ أَنزَلْنَـٰهُ فِى لَيْلَةِ ٱلْقَدْرِ ﴿1﴾

1. Lo! We revealed it on the Night of Predestination

K097.03 · When the night occurs and why it is concealed [IV:780]



They differ about its time. Most hold that it falls in Ramaḍān, on one of the odd nights of the final ten; the most frequent view is that it is the seventh of those nights. Perhaps the reason for concealing it is that the person seeking it will keep many nights alive in worship, hoping to coincide with it, so that his worship increases and his reward is multiplied; and that, if it were made known, people should not rely on attaining its excellence and then neglect the other nights.



T097.03 · Ubayy’s testimony concerning Ramaḍān [XVI:523–524]

على أنها في شهر رمضان

On “that it falls in Ramaḍān”: we have transmitted from Muslim, al-Tirmidhī, and Abū Dāwūd, from Zirr ibn Ḥubaysh, who said: “I heard Ubayy ibn Kaʿb being told, ‘ʿAbd Allāh (الله) ibn Masʿūd says: whoever stands in prayer throughout the year will encounter the Night of Decree.’ Ubayy replied, ‘By God, besides whom there is no god, it is certainly in Ramaḍān’—he swore and made no exception—‘and, by God, I know which night it is. It is the night on which God’s Messenger commanded us to stand in prayer: the twenty-seventh night.’” The report continues.



al-Qadr · Decree, distinction, and a thousand months

K IV:780–780 · T XVI:522–525

Q 97:1–3

بِّسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِنَّآ أَنزَلْنَـٰهُ فِى لَيْلَةِ ٱلْقَدْرِ ﴿1﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا لَيْلَةُ ٱلْقَدْرِ ﴿2﴾ لَيْلَةُ ٱلْقَدْرِ خَيْرٌ مِّنْ أَلْفِ شَهْرٍ ﴿3﴾

1. Lo! We revealed it on the Night of Predestination 2. Ah, what will convey unto thee what the Night of Power is 3. The Night of Power is better than a thousand months

K097.04 · Decree, distinction, and a thousand months [IV:780–781]



The meaning of “the Night of Decree” is the night on which affairs are determined and decided, from His saying, “In it every wise affair is distinguished” [44:4]. It is also said to have been named thus because of its importance and distinction above all other nights.

“And what will make you know what the Night of Decree is?” means that your knowledge has not reached the utmost of its excellence or the limit of its exalted rank. He then explains this by saying that it is “better than a thousand months.” Its excellence reaches this height because of the religious benefits He mentions as occurring in it: the descent of the angels and the Spirit, and the determination of every wise affair.

Concerning the specification of this period, it is mentioned that God’s Messenger spoke of a man of the Children of Israel who bore arms in God’s path for a thousand months. The believers marvelled at this and their own deeds seemed paltry to them; so they were given a night better than that warrior’s entire period. It is also said that, in former times, a man was not called a worshipper until he had worshipped God for a thousand months. They were therefore given a night which, if they kept it alive in worship, would entitle them to be called worshippers more than those worshippers of old.



T097.04 · Eternal determination and the meaning of qadr [XVI:524]

ليلة تقدير الأمور

On “the night on which affairs are determined”: the Imām [Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī] transmits from al-Wāḥidī that qadr (قدر) in the language means taqdīr (تقدير): making something according to the measure of another, without increase or diminution. He says: “It is named thus because it is the night for the determination of affairs and judgements. From Ibn ʿAbbās: God Most High determined in it everything that would occur in that year—rain, provision, bringing to life, and causing to die—until the following year, as in His saying, ‘In it every wise affair is distinguished’ [44:4]. The meaning is not that God’s determination comes into being only on that night. He determined all measures in pre-eternity, before creating the heavens and earth. Rather, what is meant is making those determinations manifest to the angels.”

On “it is also said to have been named thus because of its importance”: the Imām transmits from al-Zuhrī: “The Night of Qadr (قدر) is the night of greatness and distinction, from the expression ‘So-and-so has qadr (قدر) with so-and-so,’ meaning standing and honour. His saying, ‘The Night of Decree is better than a thousand months,’ supports this. It may mean that whoever performs acts of obedience becomes possessed of rank and distinction, or that acts of obedience themselves possess additional worth and distinction on that night.” From Abū Bakr al-Warrāq: “It is called the Night of Qadr (قدر) because in it a book possessing qadr (قدر) descended, through the tongue of an angel possessing qadr (قدر), upon a community possessing qadr (قدر).”



al-Qadr · The descent and the peace of the night

K IV:780–780 · T XVI:522–525

Q 97:4–5

تَنَزَّلُ ٱلْمَلَـٰٓئِكَةُ وَٱلرُّوحُ فِيهَا بِإِذْنِ رَبِّهِم مِّن كُلِّ أَمْرٍ ﴿4﴾ سَلَـٰمٌ هِىَ حَتَّىٰ مَطْلَعِ ٱلْفَجْرِ ﴿5﴾

4. The angels and the Spirit descend therein, by the permission of their Lord, with all decrees 5. (The night is) Peace until the rising of the dawn

K097.05 · The descent and the peace of the night [IV:781]



“Descend”: to the lowest heaven; or, according to another view, to the earth. “The Spirit” is Gabriel. It is also said to be a kind of angelic creature which the angels see only on that night.

“For every matter”: they descend on account of every matter God has decreed for that year until the next. The reading min kulli imriʾ (امرئ), “for every person,” is also transmitted: on account of every human being. It is said that, on that night, they encounter no believing man or woman without greeting them with peace.

“Peace it is”: it is nothing but safety; that is, God decrees in it only safety and good, whereas on other nights He decrees both affliction and safety. Or it is nothing but greetings of peace, because of the many greetings they address to believers.

Maṭlaʿ (مَطْلَع) is read both with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) and with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) on the lām (لام (ل)): maṭlaʿ (مَطْلَع) and maṭliʿ (مَطْلِع).



T097.05 · The syntax of “Peace it is” [XVI:525]

ما هي إلا سلامة

On “it is nothing but safety”: he means that hiya (هي), “it,” is the subject and salām (سلام), “peace,” the predicate. The predicate is placed first, and the night is made peace itself, to convey exclusivity.

The author of al-Kashf says: “Hiya (هي) is a subject and salām (سلام) a preceding predicate. Salām (سلام) has the sense of an active participle: it brings peace. This interpretation is necessary if ḥattā (حتى), ‘until,’ is to depend on it. If salām (سلام) were taken as a verbal noun, ḥattā (حتى) could not depend on it, because one may not separate a governing expression from its complement. It may instead depend on ‘the angels descend.’ Hiya (هي) cannot be the subject with the phrase beginning ḥattā (حتى) as its predicate, for that would convey no useful information: every night has this characteristic [of lasting until dawn].”



T097.06 · The two readings of maṭlaʿ [XVI:525]

وقرئ: {مطلع}

On “maṭlaʿ (مَطْلَع) is read”: al-Kisāʾī reads maṭliʿ (مَطْلِع), with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) on the lām (لام (ل)); the others read it with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)). Al-Zajjāj says: “With fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) it is the verbal noun, meaning rising: one says ṭalaʿa al-fajru ṭulūʿan wa-maṭlaʿan (طلع الفجر طلوعًا ومطلعًا), ‘dawn rose.’ With kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) it is a noun for the time of rising.” According to some, it cannot here mean the place of rising. God knows best.

The sūrah is complete, praise be to God Most High.



al-Qadr · Closing report

K IV:780–780 · T XVI:522–525

Q 97:1–5

بِّسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِنَّآ أَنزَلْنَـٰهُ فِى لَيْلَةِ ٱلْقَدْرِ ﴿1﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا لَيْلَةُ ٱلْقَدْرِ ﴿2﴾ لَيْلَةُ ٱلْقَدْرِ خَيْرٌ مِّنْ أَلْفِ شَهْرٍ ﴿3﴾ تَنَزَّلُ ٱلْمَلَـٰٓئِكَةُ وَٱلرُّوحُ فِيهَا بِإِذْنِ رَبِّهِم مِّن كُلِّ أَمْرٍ ﴿4﴾ سَلَـٰمٌ هِىَ حَتَّىٰ مَطْلَعِ ٱلْفَجْرِ ﴿5﴾

1. Lo! We revealed it on the Night of Predestination 2. Ah, what will convey unto thee what the Night of Power is 3. The Night of Power is better than a thousand months 4. The angels and the Spirit descend therein, by the permission of their Lord, with all decrees 5. (The night is) Peace until the rising of the dawn

K097.06 · Closing report [IV:781]



From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites Sūrat al-Qadr is given a reward like that of someone who fasted Ramaḍān and kept the Night of Decree alive in worship.”



al-Zalzalah · Heading and verses

K IV:783–785 · T XVI:536–545

Q 99:1–8

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِذَا زُلْزِلَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ زِلْزَالَهَا ﴿1﴾ وَأَخْرَجَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ أَثْقَالَهَا ﴿2﴾ وَقَالَ ٱلْإِنسَـٰنُ مَا لَهَا ﴿3﴾ يَوْمَئِذٍ تُحَدِّثُ أَخْبَارَهَا ﴿4﴾ بِأَنَّ رَبَّكَ أَوْحَىٰ لَهَا ﴿5﴾ يَوْمَئِذٍ يَصْدُرُ ٱلنَّاسُ أَشْتَاتًا لِّيُرَوْا۟ أَعْمَـٰلَهُمْ ﴿6﴾ فَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ خَيْرًا يَرَهُۥ ﴿7﴾ وَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ شَرًّا يَرَهُۥ ﴿8﴾

1. When Earth is shaken with her (final) earthquake 2. And Earth yieldeth up her burdens 3. And man saith: What aileth her 4. That day she will relate her chronicles 5. Because thy Lord inspireth her 6. That day mankind will issue forth in scattered groups to be shown their deeds 7. And whoso doeth good an atom's weight will see it then 8. And whoso doeth ill an atom's weight will see it then

K099.01 · Heading and verses [IV:783]



Medinan; also said to be Meccan. Eight verses. [The DKA heading additionally places its revelation after al-Nisāʾ.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

When the earth is shaken with its shaking, (1) and the earth brings out its burdens, (2) and the human being says, “What is the matter with it?”—(3) on that day it will tell its tidings, (4) because your Lord has inspired it. (5) On that day people will come forth separately, to be shown their deeds. (6) Whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it, (7) and whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it. (8)



al-Zalzalah · The shaking and the burdens

K IV:783–785 · T XVI:536–545

Q 99:1–2

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِذَا زُلْزِلَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ زِلْزَالَهَا ﴿1﴾ وَأَخْرَجَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ أَثْقَالَهَا ﴿2﴾

1. When Earth is shaken with her (final) earthquake 2. And Earth yieldeth up her burdens

K099.02 · The shaking and the burdens [IV:783]



Zilzālahā (زِلْزالَها) is read with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) or fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) on the zāy (زاي (ز)). With kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) it is a verbal noun; with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)), a noun. Among word-patterns, faʿlāl (فَعْلال) with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) occurs only in a reduplicated form.

If you ask what “its shaking” means in the possessive construction, I answer: the shaking that befits it in accordance with wisdom and God’s will—the violent shaking beyond which there is none. Compare: “Honour the pious man with his honour, and abase the impious man with his abasement,” meaning the honour and abasement they respectively deserve. Or it means its entire shaking, every degree of shaking possible for it.

Athqāl (أثقال) is the plural of thiql, household goods, as in “They carry your burdens” [16:7]. He makes the things buried within the earth its burdens.



T099.01 · The word-pattern faʿlāl [XVI:536]

وليس في الأبنية فعلال بالفتح إلا في المضاعف

Medinan; nine verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful. [The embedded Kashshāf heading instead records disagreement about the place of revelation; both headings here give nine verses.]

On “among word-patterns, faʿlāl (فَعْلال) with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) occurs only in a reduplicated form,” al-Kawāshī has: “Nāqa (ناقة) jizʿāl (جِزْعال) occurs, meaning a she-camel which [limps; the printed text here reads ‘looks out/climbs’], as does qasṭāl (قَسْطال), a word for dust; neither is reduplicated. It is said that Bahrām and Shahrām are foreign.” As for qahqār (قهقار), it is a weak linguistic form. Al-Ṣiḥāḥ has: “Qahqarr (قهقرّ), with doubled rāʾ (راء (ر)), is hard stone. Aḥmad ibn Yaḥyā alone used to say qahqār (قهقار).”



T099.02 · A shaking beyond which there is none [XVI:537]

الذي ليس بعده

On “beyond which there is none”: no shaking comes beyond it—that is, none surpasses it or is stronger. Al-Mughrib says: “The expression wa-in kāna laysa bi-lladhī lā (لا) baʿda (بعد) lahu means ‘although it is not the ultimate in excellence.’ It derives from their saying, ‘This is something beyond which there is no further extreme of excellence or badness.’ Sometimes they abbreviate it and say laysa baʿdahu (بعده), ‘there is none beyond it’; then the lā (لا) that negates an entire class is introduced, and [baʿd (بعد)] is used like a fully declinable noun.”

On “or its entire shaking”: the measure appropriate to it and attributed to it. The difference from the preceding interpretation is that the first refers to the agent and the requirement of His will. Hence he says, “the shaking that befits it in accordance with wisdom.” The second, although indicating comprehensiveness, is less intense than the first. His phrase “befits it in accordance with wisdom” alludes to his doctrinal position.

The Imām says: “That is, the shaking prescribed for it, if it is conceived as a living being. It is related that it shakes from the intensity of Isrāfīl’s voice.” This works only if it is conceived as alive and frightened, just as it is made speaking in “it tells its tidings.”

On “He makes the things buried within it its burdens,” al-Rāghib says: “Its burdens are said to be its treasures, or the human bodies it contains at the resurrection. ‘They carry your burdens’ [16:7] means your heavy loads.”



al-Zalzalah · The human being’s question and the earth’s speech

K IV:783–785 · T XVI:536–545

Q 99:3–4

وَقَالَ ٱلْإِنسَـٰنُ مَا لَهَا ﴿3﴾ يَوْمَئِذٍ تُحَدِّثُ أَخْبَارَهَا ﴿4﴾

3. And man saith: What aileth her 4. That day she will relate her chronicles

K099.03 · The human being’s question and the earth’s speech [IV:783–784]



“And the human being says, ‘What is the matter with it?’”—that it has shaken so violently and cast out what was in its belly. This occurs at the second blast, when it shakes and casts out its dead alive. They say this because of the dreadful event that overwhelms them, just as they say, “Who has raised us from our sleeping-place?” [36:52]. It is also said that this is the unbeliever’s speech, because he did not believe in resurrection; the believer says, “This is what the All-Merciful promised, and the messengers spoke truly” [36:52].

If you ask what the earth’s telling and its being inspired mean, I answer: this is figurative language for God’s bringing about in it states that take the place of telling by the tongue. Someone who asks, “What is the matter with it?”, looks at those states and knows why it shook and why it cast out the dead, and that this is what the prophets used to forewarn of and caution against.

It is also said that God literally makes it speak and it reports the good and evil done upon it. It is related from God’s Messenger: “It bears witness against everyone concerning what he did upon its surface.”



T099.03 · The implied circumstance and the earth’s testimony [XVI:538]

{ما لها

On “‘What is the matter with it?’—that it has shaken”: it is said that this indicates an omission in the expression. The omitted clause is circumstantial, referring to the pronoun governed by the preposition, because it functions as an object: what has happened to it in this state? Compare His saying, “What is the matter with them, turning away from the reminder?” [74:49].

On “it bears witness against everyone concerning what he did upon its surface”: Imām Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal and al-Tirmidhī transmit from Abū Hurayra that God’s Messenger recited “On that day it will tell its tidings” and asked, “Do you know what its tidings are?” They said, “God and His Messenger know best.” He said: “Its tidings are that it bears witness against every male or female servant concerning what they did on its surface. It says, ‘On such-and-such a day, he did such-and-such.’ These are its tidings.”



al-Zalzalah · Adverbials and the objects of “tell”

K IV:783–785 · T XVI:536–545

Q 99:1–4

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِذَا زُلْزِلَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ زِلْزَالَهَا ﴿1﴾ وَأَخْرَجَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ أَثْقَالَهَا ﴿2﴾ وَقَالَ ٱلْإِنسَـٰنُ مَا لَهَا ﴿3﴾ يَوْمَئِذٍ تُحَدِّثُ أَخْبَارَهَا ﴿4﴾

1. When Earth is shaken with her (final) earthquake 2. And Earth yieldeth up her burdens 3. And man saith: What aileth her 4. That day she will relate her chronicles

K099.04 · Adverbials and the objects of “tell” [IV:784]



If you ask what governs the accusative of idhā (إذا), “when,” and yawmaʾidhin (يومئذ), “on that day,” I answer: yawmaʾidhin (يومئذ) substitutes for idhā (إذا), and tuḥaddithu (تُحَدِّث), “it tells,” governs both. Alternatively, idhā (إذا) may be governed by an omitted verb, and yawmaʾidhin (يومئذ) by tuḥaddithu (تُحَدِّث).

If you ask where the two objects of tuḥaddithu (تُحَدِّث) are, I answer: the first is omitted and the second is “its tidings.” The underlying expression is “it tells created beings its tidings.” But the aim is to mention its telling the tidings, not to mention created beings, in order to magnify the day.



T099.04 · Does “tell” take one object, two, or three? [XVI:539–540]

أين مفعولا {تحدث}؟ 

On “where are the two objects of ‘it tells’?”: it is said that both question and answer are open to examination. Ḥaddatha (حدّث) does not take two objects; it takes one. The omitted term explicitly supplied here is the direct object; the expressed term, “its tidings,” is an absolute object, a verbal-noun complement. These are not called “two objects” in the grammarians’ technical usage.

True, when the particular content of the verbal noun is mentioned in this construction, it is made accusative. Some grammarians then call its components a second and third object, as in “I told Zayd that ʿAmr was standing,” and call the verb transitive to three objects. It has been stated and established in its proper place that this is not so: the verb takes one object, while “Zayd standing” is accusative because it occupies the place of the verbal noun. But when the verbal noun is explicitly expressed, as in “I told him a telling and a report,” no one says that the verb takes two objects.

The proof is that, after explaining that “Zayd standing” is accusative in such a construction because it occupies the place of a verbal noun, rather than being a second and third object, Ibn al-Ḥājib says: “It remains to ask how something that is not an action in meaning—the supposed second and third objects—can occupy the position of a verbal noun.” He answers: “It was not a verbal noun insofar as it was ‘Zayd standing,’ but insofar as it was a particular report. The aspect that permits it to be predicated of ‘the report’ when you say, ‘Zayd told me that ʿAmr was departing,’ is the same aspect that permits it to occupy the place of a verbal noun.”

I say: one may answer that ḥaddatha (حدّث) and its sister verbs truly take one object, and that making them take three or two involves figurative extension or incorporation of another verb’s meaning. Al-Mufaṣṣal says: “Ḥaddathtu is treated like aʿlamtu (أعلمت), ‘I informed,’ because it agrees with it in meaning, and is therefore made transitive in the same way.” The author of al-Iqlīd says: “Anbaʾa (أنبأ), nabbaʾa (نبّأ), akhbara, and khabbara originally take one object: anbaʾtu (أنبأت) Zaydan bi-kadhā (بكذا), ‘I informed Zayd of such-and-such.’ The preposition is then omitted and one says anbaʾtuhu (أنبأته) kadhā (كذا). In revelation, ‘Who informed you of this?’ [66:3] means bi-hādhā (بهذا), ‘of this’; and ‘Inform My servants that I am the Forgiving, the Merciful’ [15:49]. When these verbs take three objects, this is only because they are treated like aʿlamtu (أعلمت).”

It is therefore clear that the author’s question rests on this analysis. His answer indicates it when he explicitly says: “As though it were said, ‘On that day it tells its tidings: that your Lord has inspired it,’ because one says both ‘I told him such-and-such’ and ‘I told him of such-and-such.’”



T099.05 · Why the hearers are omitted [XVI:540]

إلا أن المقصود ذكر تحديثها الأخبار

On “the aim is to mention its telling the tidings”: the purpose in the verse is the second object, not the first, because the sūrah is directed toward the terror of the Resurrection. That is: a tremendous day on which inanimate things speak.



al-Zalzalah · “Because your Lord has inspired it”

K IV:783–785 · T XVI:536–545

Q 99:5

بِأَنَّ رَبَّكَ أَوْحَىٰ لَهَا ﴿5﴾

5. Because thy Lord inspireth her

K099.05 · “Because your Lord has inspired it” [IV:784]



If you ask what the bāʾ (باء (ب)) in “because your Lord” depends on, I answer: on “it tells.” The meaning is: it tells its tidings because your Lord inspired it and commanded it to tell.

The meaning may also be: on that day, by telling that your Lord has inspired it, it tells its tidings—on the understanding that its telling that your Lord has inspired it is itself the telling of its tidings. Compare your saying, “You have given me every counsel by counselling me about religion.”

“Because your Lord” may also substitute for “its tidings,” as though it were said, “On that day it tells of its tidings: that your Lord has inspired it,” because you can say both ḥaddathtuhu (حدّثته) kadhā (كذا) and ḥaddathtuhu (حدّثته) bi-kadhā (بكذا), “I told him such-and-such.”

Awḥā lahā (أوحى لها) means awḥā ilayhā (أوحى إليها), “inspired it.” It is figurative, like “that We say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is” [cf. 16:40; 36:82]. The poet says:
“He inspired it to be still, and it became still.”

Ibn Masʿūd read tunabbiʾu akhbārahā (تُنَبِّئُ أخبارها), “it reports its tidings”; Saʿīd ibn Jubayr read tunbiʾu (تُنْبِئُ), without doubling the consonant.



T099.06 · Abstraction and the inspiration of the earth [XVI:540–541]

يومئذ تحدث بتحديث أن ربك أوحى لها أخبارها

On “on that day, by telling that your Lord has inspired it, it tells its tidings”: apparently the bāʾ (باء (ب)) here resembles that in “If you meet so-and-so, you will meet in him a man of consummate goodness.” The meaning is: by telling that your Lord has inspired it, the earth tells its tidings in their utmost degree. This therefore belongs to abstraction, tajrīd (تجريد). Hence he says: “On the understanding that its telling that your Lord has inspired it is itself the telling of its tidings.”

Under His words, “When We took from the prophets their covenant,” down to “and We took from them a solemn covenant” [33:7], he says: “By the second He intends precisely the first: by that covenant We took from them a solemn covenant.” The same applies to the example “You gave me every counsel by counselling me about religion”: from counsel concerning religion he abstracts perfect counsel. So too the poet says:
“She granted me all my wishes with a visit,
snatched in secret like the darting of a bird.
Had I been able, I would have bestowed upon the darkness
the blackness of my pupil, to lengthen our night.”

On “it is figurative”: that is, an extended metaphor, as previously under “Be, and it is.” The will to manifest the states within the earth is likened to an instruction conveyed to someone commanded, to display the swiftness with which what is wanted of him is obeyed.



al-Zalzalah · Separate departures and the order of the verses

K IV:783–785 · T XVI:536–545

Q 99:6

يَوْمَئِذٍ يَصْدُرُ ٱلنَّاسُ أَشْتَاتًا لِّيُرَوْا۟ أَعْمَـٰلَهُمْ ﴿6﴾

6. That day mankind will issue forth in scattered groups to be shown their deeds

K099.06 · Separate departures and the order of the verses [IV:784–785]



They come forth from the exits of their graves to the standing-place “separately”: some white-faced and secure, others black-faced and frightened. Or they leave the standing-place separately, divided between the paths of Paradise and the Fire, to be shown the recompense of their deeds.

In the Prophet’s reading, li-yaraw has fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)): “so that they may see.” Ibn ʿAbbās and Zayd ibn ʿAlī read yurahu with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)): “he will be shown it.” It is related that a Bedouin put the words “good, he will see it” later [than the corresponding clause about evil]. When told, “You have put one part before and another after,” he replied:
“Take the hollow of Harshā or its back:
either side of Harshā is a road for them.”



T099.07 · Harshā and the priority of good [XVI:542]

خذا بطن هرشى

On the line “Take the hollow of Harshā”: Harshā is a pass on the road to Mecca, near al-Juḥfa, with two routes. He addresses his two companions: travel through the hollow of this pass or along its back, since both sides are roads for the camels. This is a proverb for a course equally easy from either side.

It is said that the Bedouin imagined rearrangement here to be permissible. This is wrong: he overlooked the Qurʾān’s subtleties. There is no point in adducing the line here, and it would have been better to omit it, because primary concern attaches to good, while evil is incidental. Under “Whoever disbelieves, his unbelief is against him; and whoever acts righteously, they prepare for themselves—so that He may reward those who believe and do righteous deeds from His bounty; He does not love the unbelievers” [30:44–45], the Qāḍī says: “‘So that He may reward’ gives the purpose of ‘they prepare.’ Limiting the mention to the believer’s reward indicates that this is what is intended in itself.”



al-Zalzalah · The atom’s weight and the two groups

K IV:783–785 · T XVI:536–545

Q 99:7–8

فَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ خَيْرًا يَرَهُۥ ﴿7﴾ وَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ شَرًّا يَرَهُۥ ﴿8﴾

7. And whoso doeth good an atom's weight will see it then 8. And whoso doeth ill an atom's weight will see it then

K099.07 · The atom’s weight and the two groups [IV:785]



A dharra is a small ant. It is also said that dharr is the dust visible in a beam of sunlight.

If you ask, “The unbeliever’s good deeds are nullified by unbelief, and the believer’s evil deeds are pardoned through avoiding major sins: what, then, is the meaning of recompense for atom-weights of good and evil?”—I answer: the meaning is “whoever does an atom’s weight of good” from the company of the felicitous, and “whoever does an atom’s weight of evil” from the company of the wretched, because this follows “people will come forth separately.”



T099.08 · The restricted reading and objections to its premises [XVI:542–543]

لأنه جاء بعد قوله: {يومئذ يصدر الناس أشتاتا

On “because this follows ‘On that day people will come forth separately’”: he means that “Whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it, and whoever does an atom’s weight of evil will see it” sets out the division of people into two groups, the felicitous and the wretched. That is, the verse is restricted in scope.

Al-Intiṣāf says: “His question rests on two principles. The first is that the unbeliever’s good deeds are nullified by unbelief. This needs examination. If it means that he receives no reward for them, it is correct. But if it means that they do not lessen punishment, that is not conceded. Reports have come concerning Ḥātim, that God lightens his punishment for his generosity, and concerning Abū Ṭālib and others. Such deeds therefore have an effect in lessening punishment.

“The second is that avoiding major sins necessarily expiates minor ones. This conflicts with the doctrine of the People of the Sunna. Minor sins are expiated by one of two things: repentance, or God’s willing forgiveness. This question therefore falls away according to us.”

The Imām says: “One may say that, although the unbeliever’s good deeds are nullified by his unbelief, the weighing of deeds is recognized according to your own position. To the extent of those good deeds, punishment for his unbelief is reduced. The same applies on the other side. This therefore does not impair the verse’s generality.”



T099.09 · Two possible scopes and the author’s doctrinal intention [XVI:543–544]



I say: the verse admits two meanings. One paired clause may intend the felicitous and the other the wretched, since the relative expression is repeated. Or each clause may intend generality: whoever among believers and unbelievers does an atom’s weight of good will see it, and whoever among believers and unbelievers does an atom’s weight of evil will see it.

The author’s words follow the first interpretation, as does what Muḥyī al-Sunna [al-Baghawī] and the Imām transmit from Muḥammad ibn Kaʿb al-Quraẓī: whoever does an atom’s weight of good while an unbeliever sees its reward in this world—in himself, his family, and his property—until he meets the hereafter with no good awaiting him there. Whoever does an atom’s weight of evil while a believer has it expiated in this world—in himself, his family, and his property—until he reaches the hereafter with no evil awaiting him there.

But the author’s intention is to include the person committing a major sin among the unbelievers and the wretched, because that person’s good deeds are nullified by it and he therefore sees only evil; just as the minor sins of one avoiding major sins are expiated through that avoidance and he sees only good. This is known from his question.

On the second interpretation comes what al-Wāḥidī transmits from Muqātil: whoever does an atom’s weight of good in this world will see it on the Day of Resurrection and rejoice in it. Likewise with evil: he sees it in his record, and it grieves him. Muḥyī al-Sunna and the Imām transmit from Ibn ʿAbbās: no believer or unbeliever does anything, good or evil, without God showing it to him. The believer’s evil deeds are forgiven and he is rewarded for his good deeds; the unbeliever’s good deeds are rejected and he is punished for his evil deeds.



T099.10 · Wording, meaning, and style favour generality [XVI:544–545]



This latter possibility is supported by the wording, the meaning, and the style.

As for the wording: “Whoever does,” as already stated, elaborates the preceding “People will come forth separately, to be shown their deeds,” so the two must agree. “Their deeds” is a plural in a possessive construction and conveys comprehensiveness and inclusion of all. “People will come forth” is qualified by “separately,” indicating that they take diverse routes to settle in their stations in Paradise and the Fire according to their differing deeds. Hence Paradise has ascending degrees and the Fire descending levels.

As for the meaning: the passage explains the exhaustive display of deeds and their recompense, as in His saying, “We shall set up the just scales for the Day of Resurrection, and no soul will be wronged in anything. Even if it is the weight of a mustard seed, We shall bring it; and We suffice as reckoners” [21:47].

As for the style: this is one of the comprehensive statements containing the benefits of religion, both its foundations and its branches. We have transmitted from al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Abū Hurayra, that God’s Messenger was asked about donkeys and said, “Nothing has been revealed to me concerning them except this comprehensive, unique verse,” and recited it. “About donkeys” means about alms on donkeys. Fādhdha (فاذّة), “unique,” means singular in its meaning: a man fadhdha from his companions when he separated from them.

Imām Aḥmad transmits from Ṣaʿṣaʿa ibn Muʿāwiya, al-Farazdaq’s uncle, that he came to the Prophet, who recited the verse. He said, “That is enough for me; I do not mind hearing nothing else.” In al-Ḥaqāʾiq: a sage was asked, “Admonish us,” and recited the verse. The questioner said, “Then the admonition has reached its utmost.”



al-Zalzalah · Closing report

K IV:783–785 · T XVI:536–545

Q 99:1–8

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِذَا زُلْزِلَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ زِلْزَالَهَا ﴿1﴾ وَأَخْرَجَتِ ٱلْأَرْضُ أَثْقَالَهَا ﴿2﴾ وَقَالَ ٱلْإِنسَـٰنُ مَا لَهَا ﴿3﴾ يَوْمَئِذٍ تُحَدِّثُ أَخْبَارَهَا ﴿4﴾ بِأَنَّ رَبَّكَ أَوْحَىٰ لَهَا ﴿5﴾ يَوْمَئِذٍ يَصْدُرُ ٱلنَّاسُ أَشْتَاتًا لِّيُرَوْا۟ أَعْمَـٰلَهُمْ ﴿6﴾ فَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ خَيْرًا يَرَهُۥ ﴿7﴾ وَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ شَرًّا يَرَهُۥ ﴿8﴾

1. When Earth is shaken with her (final) earthquake 2. And Earth yieldeth up her burdens 3. And man saith: What aileth her 4. That day she will relate her chronicles 5. Because thy Lord inspireth her 6. That day mankind will issue forth in scattered groups to be shown their deeds 7. And whoso doeth good an atom's weight will see it then 8. And whoso doeth ill an atom's weight will see it then

K099.08 · Closing report [IV:785]



From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites Sūrat ‘When the earth is shaken’ four times is like someone who recites the whole Qurʾān.”



T099.11 · The closing report and another transmitted measure [XVI:545]

من قرأ [سورة] {إذا زلزلت

On “whoever recites Sūrat ‘When the earth is shaken’ four times”: we have transmitted from al-Tirmidhī, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said that God’s Messenger said: “Whoever recites ‘When the earth is shaken,’ it is reckoned for him as half the Qurʾān.”

End of the sūrah.



al-Qāriʿah · Heading and verses

K IV:789–790 · T XVI:554–557

Q 101:1–11

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ ٱلْقَارِعَةُ ﴿1﴾ مَا ٱلْقَارِعَةُ ﴿2﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا ٱلْقَارِعَةُ ﴿3﴾ يَوْمَ يَكُونُ ٱلنَّاسُ كَٱلْفَرَاشِ ٱلْمَبْثُوثِ ﴿4﴾ وَتَكُونُ ٱلْجِبَالُ كَٱلْعِهْنِ ٱلْمَنفُوشِ ﴿5﴾ فَأَمَّا مَن ثَقُلَتْ مَوَٰزِينُهُۥ ﴿6﴾ فَهُوَ فِى عِيشَةٍ رَّاضِيَةٍ ﴿7﴾ وَأَمَّا مَنْ خَفَّتْ مَوَٰزِينُهُۥ ﴿8﴾ فَأُمُّهُۥ هَاوِيَةٌ ﴿9﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا هِيَهْ ﴿10﴾ نَارٌ حَامِيَةٌۢ ﴿11﴾

1. The Calamity 2. What is the Calamity 3. Ah, what will convey unto thee what the Calamity is 4. A day wherein mankind will be as thickly-scattered moths 5. And the mountains will become as carded wool 6. Then, as for him whose scales are heavy (with good works) 7. He will live a pleasant life 8. But as for him whose scales are light 9. A bereft and Hungry One will be his mother 10. Ah, what will convey unto thee what she is 11. Raging Fire

K101.01 · Heading and verses [IV:789]



Meccan; eleven verses. [The DKA heading additionally places its revelation after Quraysh.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

The striking calamity! (1) What is the striking calamity? (2) And what will make you know what the striking calamity is? (3) On the day when people will be like scattered moths, (4) and the mountains like carded wool. (5) As for one whose scales are heavy, (6) he will be in a life of contentment. (7) But as for one whose scales are light, (8) his mother will be an abyss. (9) And what will make you know what it is? (10) A blazing fire. (11)



al-Qāriʿah · The day, the moths, and the mountains

K IV:789–790 · T XVI:554–557

Q 101:1–5

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ ٱلْقَارِعَةُ ﴿1﴾ مَا ٱلْقَارِعَةُ ﴿2﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا ٱلْقَارِعَةُ ﴿3﴾ يَوْمَ يَكُونُ ٱلنَّاسُ كَٱلْفَرَاشِ ٱلْمَبْثُوثِ ﴿4﴾ وَتَكُونُ ٱلْجِبَالُ كَٱلْعِهْنِ ٱلْمَنفُوشِ ﴿5﴾

1. The Calamity 2. What is the Calamity 3. Ah, what will convey unto thee what the Calamity is 4. A day wherein mankind will be as thickly-scattered moths 5. And the mountains will become as carded wool

K101.02 · The day, the moths, and the mountains [IV:789–790]



The adverbial “on the day” is accusative through an omitted verb indicated by al-qāriʿa (القارعة): “It strikes on the day when people will be like scattered moths.” He likens them to moths in their multitude, dispersal, weakness, abasement, and flight toward the summoner from every direction, as moths fly toward a fire. Jarīr says:
“Al-Farazdaq, so far as I have known, and his people
are like moths that swarm the fire of one warming himself.”

Among their proverbs are “weaker than a moth,” “more abased,” and “more ignorant.” They are called farāsh (فراش) because they spread out and disperse. He likens the mountains to ʿihn (عِهْن), wool dyed in different colours, because the mountains are variously coloured; and to carded wool because their parts separate. Ibn Masʿūd read ka-l-ṣūf (كالصوف), “like wool.”



T101.01 · Jarīr’s comparison [XVI:554–555]

إن الفرزدق

Meccan; ten verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful. [The source’s count is retained; the verse text above uses the eleven-verse numbering.]

On the line beginning “Al-Farazdaq”: mā (ما) ʿalimtu (علمت) means “what I have known,” and is parenthetical. He satirizes al-Farazdaq and his people: they are weak, abased, and ignorant, like moths which ghashīna (غشين)—that is, came in the covering darkness of night—to the fire at which the poet, Jarīr, warms himself. It is also said that ghashīna (غشين) means “plunged into.” Another explanation takes mā (ما) in mā (ما) ʿalimtu (علمت) as introducing a verbal noun, with a period of time understood: throughout the duration of my knowledge of them, al-Farazdaq and his people have been weak.



al-Qāriʿah · Heavy and light scales

K IV:789–790 · T XVI:554–557

Q 101:6–8

فَأَمَّا مَن ثَقُلَتْ مَوَٰزِينُهُۥ ﴿6﴾ فَهُوَ فِى عِيشَةٍ رَّاضِيَةٍ ﴿7﴾ وَأَمَّا مَنْ خَفَّتْ مَوَٰزِينُهُۥ ﴿8﴾

6. Then, as for him whose scales are heavy (with good works) 7. He will live a pleasant life 8. But as for him whose scales are light

K101.03 · Heavy and light scales [IV:790]



Mawāzīn (موازين) is the plural of mawzūn (موزون), something weighed: the deed that possesses weight and importance with God. Or it is the plural of mīzān (ميزان), a scale; their heaviness is their outweighing.

An example is Abū Bakr’s report in his testament to ʿUmar, God be pleased with them both: “The scales of those whose scales are heavy on the Day of Resurrection are heavy only because they followed the truth and because of their weight in this world. A scale into which nothing but good deeds is placed rightly becomes heavy. The scales of those whose scales are light are light only because they followed falsehood and because of their lightness in this world. A scale into which nothing but evil deeds is placed rightly becomes light.”



al-Qāriʿah · “His mother will be an abyss”

K IV:789–790 · T XVI:554–557

Q 101:8–9

وَأَمَّا مَنْ خَفَّتْ مَوَٰزِينُهُۥ ﴿8﴾ فَأُمُّهُۥ هَاوِيَةٌ ﴿9﴾

8. But as for him whose scales are light 9. A bereft and Hungry One will be his mother

K101.04 · “His mother will be an abyss” [IV:790]



“His mother will be an abyss” derives from their expression when invoking destruction upon a man: hawat ummuhu (هوت أمّه), “May his mother fall!” For when he hawā (هوى)—falls and perishes—his mother falls through bereavement and grief. The poet says:
“May his mother fall! What a man the morning rouses to set out,
and what a man the night brings back when he returns!”

It is thus as though He said: “As for one whose scales are light, he has perished.”

It is also said that Hāwiya (هاوية) is one of the names of the Fire. It is as though it were the deep Fire, so called because its people fall within it to a great depth, as in the report, “He falls in it for seventy autumns.” That is, his abode is the Fire. An abode is metaphorically called a mother because the mother is the child’s refuge and place of recourse.

From Qatāda: “His mother will be an abyss” means that the crown of his head will fall into the bottom of Jahannam, because he is cast into it headfirst.



T101.02 · The testament and the elegy [XVI:555–556]

ومنه حديث أبي بكر رضي الله عنه

On “an example is Abū Bakr’s report”: the author of Jāmiʿ al-Uṣūl (الأصول) transmits the report from Razīn al-ʿAbdarī. We have given it in full under al-Aʿrāf.

On the line “May his mother fall!”: its author is Kaʿb ibn Saʿd al-Ghanawī, lamenting his brother. Yabʿathu (يبعث) means rouses from sleep; al-ghādī (الغادي) is one who sets out in the morning and is circumstantial. Hawat ummuhu (هوت أمّه) is an imprecation whose occurrence is not intended; rather, it expresses wonder and praise. That is: what a man the morning sends forth from him when he sets out, and what a man the night returns from him when he comes back! “From him” is omitted in both places because the discourse indicates it, just as it is omitted in “The clarified butter: two minas for a dirham.” This involves abstraction, tajrīd (تجريد): the morning sends forth from him a raider, and the night a man returning with booty.



T101.03 · Seventy autumns [XVI:556]

سبعين خريفا

On “seventy autumns”: according to some, autumn stands for the year because fruits and crops develop in this season. The final portion of a period is used to designate the whole.



T101.04 · Destruction, life, and the parallel expressions [XVI:556]

في التفسير الأول

On “on the first interpretation”: that is, if “his mother will be an abyss” is explained as an imprecation, from their saying “May his mother fall!” The pronoun is made to refer to calamity because, when a person falls and perishes and his mother becomes bereaved and disgraced, a calamity has befallen him. On the second interpretation, “his mother” means his abode, and Hāwiya (هاوية) is one of the names of the Fire.

The first interpretation is the more evident, because “his mother will be an abyss” stands opposite “he will be in a life of contentment.” Destruction corresponds more aptly to ʿaysh (عيش), since this is the life peculiar to animate creatures. Just as the second paired clause is intensified by what follows it, the first is intensified by figurative predication.

Al-Rāghib says: “ʿAysh (عيش) is life peculiar to animate creatures, and is narrower than ḥayāt (حياة), ‘life,’ because ḥayāt (حياة) is said of animate creatures, the Creator Most High, and the angel. From it is derived maʿīsha (معيشة), that by which one lives. God Most High says, ‘We have apportioned among them their livelihood in the life of this world’ [43:32]. Of the people of Paradise He says, ‘He will be in a life of contentment.’ And the Prophet says in the report, ‘There is no life but the life of the hereafter.’”



al-Qāriʿah · The pronoun and the pausal hāʾ

K IV:789–790 · T XVI:554–557

Q 101:10–11

وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا هِيَهْ ﴿10﴾ نَارٌ حَامِيَةٌۢ ﴿11﴾

10. Ah, what will convey unto thee what she is 11. Raging Fire

K101.05 · The pronoun and the pausal hāʾ [IV:790–791]



In hiya-h (هِيَهْ), the pronoun refers to the calamity indicated by “his mother will be an abyss” on the first interpretation; or it refers to Hāwiya (هاوية). The final hāʾ (هاء (ه)) is for pausing. When the reciter connects the reading, he omits it. It is also said that one ought not to connect here, lest connection cause its omission, since it is written in the codex. Retaining it in connected recitation has also been permitted.



T101.05 · The stopping point [XVI:557]

والهاء للسكت، وإذا وصل القارئ حذفها

On “the hāʾ (هاء (ه)) is for pausing; when the reciter connects, he omits it”: al-Murshid says that mā (ما) hiya-h (هِيَهْ) is a sufficient stopping point. Abū Ḥātim calls it a good stopping point. He then explains it with “a blazing fire.” God knows best.

End of the sūrah.



al-Qāriʿah · Closing report

K IV:789–790 · T XVI:554–557

Q 101:1–11

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ ٱلْقَارِعَةُ ﴿1﴾ مَا ٱلْقَارِعَةُ ﴿2﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا ٱلْقَارِعَةُ ﴿3﴾ يَوْمَ يَكُونُ ٱلنَّاسُ كَٱلْفَرَاشِ ٱلْمَبْثُوثِ ﴿4﴾ وَتَكُونُ ٱلْجِبَالُ كَٱلْعِهْنِ ٱلْمَنفُوشِ ﴿5﴾ فَأَمَّا مَن ثَقُلَتْ مَوَٰزِينُهُۥ ﴿6﴾ فَهُوَ فِى عِيشَةٍ رَّاضِيَةٍ ﴿7﴾ وَأَمَّا مَنْ خَفَّتْ مَوَٰزِينُهُۥ ﴿8﴾ فَأُمُّهُۥ هَاوِيَةٌ ﴿9﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا هِيَهْ ﴿10﴾ نَارٌ حَامِيَةٌۢ ﴿11﴾

1. The Calamity 2. What is the Calamity 3. Ah, what will convey unto thee what the Calamity is 4. A day wherein mankind will be as thickly-scattered moths 5. And the mountains will become as carded wool 6. Then, as for him whose scales are heavy (with good works) 7. He will live a pleasant life 8. But as for him whose scales are light 9. A bereft and Hungry One will be his mother 10. Ah, what will convey unto thee what she is 11. Raging Fire

K101.06 · Closing report [IV:791]



From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites Sūrat al-Qāriʿah, God will make his scale heavy through it on the Day of Resurrection.”



al-Takāthur · Heading and verses

K IV:791–792 · T XVI:558–564

Q 102:1–8

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ أَلْهَىٰكُمُ ٱلتَّكَاثُرُ ﴿1﴾ حَتَّىٰ زُرْتُمُ ٱلْمَقَابِرَ ﴿2﴾ كَلَّا سَوْفَ تَعْلَمُونَ ﴿3﴾ ثُمَّ كَلَّا سَوْفَ تَعْلَمُونَ ﴿4﴾ كَلَّا لَوْ تَعْلَمُونَ عِلْمَ ٱلْيَقِينِ ﴿5﴾ لَتَرَوُنَّ ٱلْجَحِيمَ ﴿6﴾ ثُمَّ لَتَرَوُنَّهَا عَيْنَ ٱلْيَقِينِ ﴿7﴾ ثُمَّ لَتُسْـَٔلُنَّ يَوْمَئِذٍ عَنِ ٱلنَّعِيمِ ﴿8﴾

1. Rivalry in worldly increase distracteth you 2. Until ye come to the graves 3. Nay, but ye will come to know 4. Nay, but ye will come to know 5. Nay, would that ye knew (now) with a sure knowledge 6. For ye will behold hell-fire 7. Aye, ye will behold it with sure vision 8. Then, on that day, ye will be asked concerning pleasure

K102.01 · Heading and verses



Meccan; eight verses. [K-DKA adds: revealed after al-Kawthar (الكوثر).]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.
“Rivalry in increase has distracted you, until you visited the graves. No indeed! You will come to know. Again, no indeed! You will come to know. No indeed! If only you knew with knowledge of certainty! You will surely see the blazing Fire. Then you will surely see it with the very certainty of sight. Then that day you will surely be questioned about the pleasures you enjoyed.”



al-Takāthur · Rivalry and the graves

K IV:791–792 · T XVI:558–564

Q 102:1–2

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ أَلْهَىٰكُمُ ٱلتَّكَاثُرُ ﴿1﴾ حَتَّىٰ زُرْتُمُ ٱلْمَقَابِرَ ﴿2﴾

1. Rivalry in worldly increase distracteth you 2. Until ye come to the graves

K102.02 · Rivalry and the graves



Alhāhu (ألهاه) ʿan (عن) kadhā (كذا) and aqhāhu (أقهاه) mean that something occupied him and distracted him from something else. Takāthur (تكاثر) is competing in abundance and boasting of it: one group says, “We are more numerous,” and the other says, “We are more numerous.” It is reported that Banū ʿAbd Manāf and Banū Sahm boasted to one another about which was greater in number. Banū ʿAbd Manāf outnumbered them. Banū Sahm said: “Aggression destroyed us in the age of ignorance; count against us both the living and the dead.” Banū Sahm then outnumbered them.
The meaning is: you competed over the number of the living, and once you exhausted their number, you turned to the graves and competed over the dead. Their proceeding to mention the dead is expressed as visiting the graves, in mockery of them. It is also said that they actually visited the graves, saying, “This is so-and-so's grave; this is so-and-so's grave,” while boasting to one another.
The meaning is that this distracted you—something that does not concern you and yields you no benefit in this world or the next—from what does concern you: the affairs of religion, which are more important and more deserving of attention than anything else.
Or He means: rivalry in wealth and children distracted you until you died and were buried. You spent your lives seeking this world, racing toward it and throwing yourselves upon it, until death came while you cared for nothing else. It distracted you from what had a greater claim upon you: striving for your final outcome and working for your hereafter. Visiting the graves is an expression for death. A poet said:
“This year no intimate who has tasted a shared attachment
will secure ten [nights of exclusive companionship], unless he visits the grave.”
Another said:
“Abū Mālik (مَالِك) visited the graves,
and became the meanest of their visitors.”
Ibn ʿAbbās read a-alhākum (أألهاكم), as a question whose purpose is to elicit acknowledgment.



T102.01 · Outnumbering; the irony of visiting graves (XVI, 558–559)

فكثرتهم بنوسهم

On “Banū Sahm outnumbered them”: they defeated them through greater numbers, from the expression kāthartuhu fa-kathartuhu (كاثرته فكثرته), “I competed with him in numbers and outnumbered him.” Takāthur (تكاثر) is striving to increase one's wealth and numbers.
On “you turned to the graves and competed over the dead”: according to this, “the graves” is an indirect expression for moving from mentioning the living to mentioning the dead in mutual boasting. It is mockery because visiting graves was prescribed to recall death, reject love of this world, and abandon ostentation and boasting. These people reversed this: they made visiting graves a cause of greater hardness of heart, immersion in worldly love, and boasting of greater numbers.
We have transmitted from Muslim, Abū Dāwūd, and al-Nasāʾī, from Burayda, that God's Messenger said: “I forbade you to visit graves; now visit them.” Abū Dāwūd's version reads: “Visit them, for they remind you of the hereafter.”
On “or He means: rivalry in wealth and children distracted you until you died”: the three interpretations thus reduce to ‘visiting’ meaning either the transition from mentioning one group to mentioning another, actual visiting, or death. “Spending” is a circumstantial expression attached to “distracted you”; “from what had a greater claim upon you” is also governed by “distracted you.”



T102.02 · The verse about a shared attachment (559–560)

لن يخلص العام

On “This year no intimate…”: al-Fāʾiq says: “A woman's ḍamd (ضمد) is her joining together and taking two lovers.” Abū Dhuʾayb said:
“You wish to join me and Khālid as your lovers—
can two swords, woe to you, be joined in one sheath?”
The author of the quoted verse is Miqdād ibn Ḥassān al-Zubayrī. It is preceded by:
“I have found a shared attachment a grievous thing.”
In the age of ignorance a woman would take a lover besides her husband; that is ḍamd (ضمد).
On “ten” (ʿashran (عَشْرًا)): ten nights. A reading with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) on the ʿayn (عين (ع)), ʿishran (عِشْرًا), is also transmitted, meaning companionship. Muʿāshara (معاشرة) means association, as does taʿāshur (تعاشر); the corresponding noun is ʿishra (عِشْرة). Khalīl (خليل) means husband. The meaning is that a husband will not secure exclusive companionship with a woman for ten nights unless he dies. “Who has tasted a shared attachment” describes the intimate. [The compressed verse and its explanation remain difficult; the alternatives are retained rather than conflated.]



al-Takāthur · Repeated warning

K IV:791–792 · T XVI:558–564

Q 102:3–5

كَلَّا سَوْفَ تَعْلَمُونَ ﴿3﴾ ثُمَّ كَلَّا سَوْفَ تَعْلَمُونَ ﴿4﴾ كَلَّا لَوْ تَعْلَمُونَ عِلْمَ ٱلْيَقِينِ ﴿5﴾

3. Nay, but ye will come to know 4. Nay, but ye will come to know 5. Nay, would that ye knew (now) with a sure knowledge

K102.03 · Repeated warning



Kallā (كلّا), “no indeed,” rebukes and calls attention to the fact that someone concerned for his own welfare ought not to make this world his entire concern while neglecting his religion. “You will come to know” warns them so that they may fear and awaken from heedlessness. Repetition emphasizes the rebuke and warning directed at them. Thumma, “again/then,” indicates that the second warning is more emphatic and severe than the first, as when you tell someone you are advising, “I tell you, and then I tell you again: do not do it.”
The meaning is: you will know the error of your present course when you see before you the terror of meeting God. This admonition is counsel and mercy for you. He then repeats the admonition and says, “If only you knew,” with the consequence left unstated: if you knew what lies before you with knowledge of something certain—as you know those matters of which you are assured and to whose knowledge you have devoted your attention—you would do something beyond description or comprehension. But you are astray and ignorant.



T102.03 · Kallā, stopping points, and the two senses (560–561)

{كلا

On “kallā (كلّا) rebukes and calls attention”: it rejects the preceding discourse and calls attention to what the following discourse indicates. He thus takes both its meanings into consideration. The Imām [Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī] says: “Kallā (كلّا) is connected with what precedes by rejection and denial: the matter is not as these people imagine, that true happiness lies in abundant numbers, wealth, and children. It is connected with what follows in the sense, ‘Truly, you will come to know’—but at a time when the transgressor becomes repentant, the unbeliever becomes a Muslim, and the covetous person becomes an ascetic.” The author's words intimate both meanings.
Al-Kawāshī says: “The stop at ‘the graves’ is complete if kallā (كلّا) calls attention. If it rebukes, the stop is at kallā (كلّا).”
If you ask whether the author's position entails using a shared expression simultaneously in both its different meanings, I answer: it does not. The intention is that, if one begins with kallā (كلّا), a new statement begins there. An implied question is supplied: what is the recompense of these heedless people, and what is said of them?
