The answer is: truly, they will know the outcome of their state when they see the blazing Fire. The discourse thus conveys rebuke by its meaning.
If one stops at kallā (كلّا), the implied question occurs after it: what will be done to these rejected people who have been rebuked? The answer is: they will know what will be done to them when they see the blazing Fire. The discourse thus entails calling attention by its meaning. The author of al-Murshid says: “‘Until you visited the graves’ is a complete stop. Begin with kallā (كلّا) in the sense of threat and warning.”
On “if you knew what lies before you”: it is said that ‘knowledge’ here means knowing the thing itself, not knowing it by its description.



al-Takāthur · Seeing the Fire; readings; certainty

K IV:791–792 · T XVI:558–564

Q 102:6–7

لَتَرَوُنَّ ٱلْجَحِيمَ ﴿6﴾ ثُمَّ لَتَرَوُنَّهَا عَيْنَ ٱلْيَقِينِ ﴿7﴾

6. For ye will behold hell-fire 7. Aye, ye will behold it with sure vision

K102.04 · Seeing the Fire; readings; certainty



He then says, “You will surely see the blazing Fire,” explaining what He had warned them against and threatened them with. The magnifying effect of clarifying something after leaving it unspecified has already been discussed. This is the response to an omitted oath. The oath emphasizes the threat and establishes that what they are threatened with admits no doubt. He repeats it, coordinating it with thumma, to intensify the threat and increase the terror.
La-tarʾunna (لَتَرَؤُنَّ) is also read, with a hamza (همزة (ء)) [in place of the wāw (واو (و))]; it is considered objectionable. If you ask why, when changing a wāw (واو (و)) bearing ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) into hamza (همزة (ء)) is a regular analogy, I answer: that applies to a wāw (واو (و)) whose ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) is permanent. Here it is incidental, introduced to avoid the meeting of two vowelless letters. La-turawunna (لَتُرَوُنَّ) and la-turawunnahā (لَتُرَوُنَّها) are also read as passives: “you will surely be made to see,” and “you will surely be made to see it.”
“With the very certainty of sight” (ʿayna al-yaqīn (عين اليقين)): a seeing that is certainty itself and its pure essence. Seeing may mean knowledge and visual perception.



T102.04 · The hamza reading and the canonical readers (562)

ذاك في الواو التي ضمتها لازمة

On “that applies to a wāw (واو (و)) whose ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) is permanent”: al-Zajjāj says: “The reading is la-tarawunna (لَتَرَوُنَّ), with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) on the wāw (واو (و)) and without hamza (همزة (ء)). The wāw (واو (و)) receives ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) because it and the nūn (نون (ن)) are both vowelless. Some have pronounced it with hamza (همزة (ء)), but the grammarians dislike this because its ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) is not permanent: it was given a vowel to avoid two vowelless letters meeting. They change a wāw (واو (و)) with permanent ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) into hamza (همزة (ء)), as in adʾur (أدؤر), the plural of dār (دار), ‘house’; adwur is also permissible.”
On “la-turawunna (لَتُرَوُنَّ) is also read”: Ibn ʿĀmir and al-Kisāʾī read with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) on the tāʾ (تاء (ت)); the others with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)). Among the seven readers there is no disagreement that la-tarawunnahā (لَتَرَوُنَّها) has fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) on the tāʾ (تاء (ت)).



T102.05 · Knowledge, sight, and the degrees of certainty (562–563)

{عين اليقين

On “‘the very certainty of sight’: a seeing that is certainty itself”: it is said that ʿayna al-yaqīn (عين اليقين) is accusative as a verbal-noun expression. ʿAyn (عين (ع)) here means the thing itself, as when you say, “Zayd himself came”—nafsuhu or ʿaynuhu (عينه). The correct position is that seeing here means visual perception rather than knowledge.
I say: this is what he intended by “seeing may mean knowledge and visual perception,” treating the coordination as explanatory. The Qāḍī says: “‘ʿAyn (عين (ع)) al-yaqīn’ means a seeing that is certainty itself, for knowledge through direct witnessing is the highest degree of certainty.”
Our shaykh, Shaykh al-Islām—may his inner being be sanctified—says in al-ʿAwārif: “ʿIlm al-yaqīn (علم اليقين), knowledge of certainty, is reached through reflection and inference. ʿAyn (عين (ع)) al-yaqīn, the eye of certainty, is reached through unveilings and gifts. Ḥaqq al-yaqīn, the reality of certainty, comes through truly separating oneself from the stain of clay when the herald of union arrives.” Al-Junayd says: “The reality of certainty is that the servant becomes fully established in it: he witnesses unseen things just as he witnesses visible things, with direct vision.”



al-Takāthur · The enjoyment about which people are questioned

K IV:791–792 · T XVI:558–564

Q 102:8

ثُمَّ لَتُسْـَٔلُنَّ يَوْمَئِذٍ عَنِ ٱلنَّعِيمِ ﴿8﴾

8. Then, on that day, ye will be asked concerning pleasure

K102.05 · The enjoyment about which people are questioned



“About the pleasures you enjoyed”: about the amusement and comfortable enjoyment whose pleasures distracted you from religion and its obligations.
If you ask what enjoyment a person is questioned and reproached about, since everyone has some enjoyment, I answer: it is the enjoyment of someone who devotes all his concern to exhausting the pleasures of life, living only to eat fine food, wear soft clothes, and pass his time in amusement and entertainment. He pays no heed to knowledge and action and does not undertake their difficulties.
But someone who enjoys God's blessings and provisions—which He created only for His servants—and draws strength from them to study knowledge and perform good works, while rising to the duty of gratitude, is far removed from this. God's Messenger indicated this in the report that he and his Companions ate dates and drank water afterward, and he said: “Praise belongs to God, who fed us, gave us drink, and made us Muslims.”
God's Messenger is reported to have said: “Whoever recites ‘Rivalry in increase has distracted you,’ God will not call him to account for the enjoyment He granted him in the abode of this world, and he will be given a reward as though he had recited a thousand verses.”



T102.06 · Who is questioned about enjoyment? (563–564)

هو نعيم من عكف همته على استيفاء اللذات

On “the enjoyment of someone who devotes all his concern to exhausting pleasures”: the Qāḍī says: “The address, ‘Then that day you will surely be questioned about the pleasures you enjoyed,’ concerns specifically anyone whose worldly life distracted him from his religion, not the believers, because of the contextual indication and the many texts such as ‘Say: Who has forbidden God's adornment, which He has brought forth for His servants, and the good things of provision?’ (7:32), and ‘Eat of the good things’ (23:51). It is also said to concern unbelievers specifically; or to be general, since everyone is questioned about his gratitude.”
I say: the latter is supported by what we have transmitted from Muslim, al-Tirmidhī, and Ibn Mājah, from Abū Hurayra. God's Messenger went out and encountered Abū Bakr and ʿUmar. He asked: “What brought you out of your homes?” They said: “Hunger.” He said: “By Him in whose hand is my soul, the same thing brought me out.” They came to the house of a man of the Anṣār, who brought them a cluster containing unripe, dried, and fresh dates, and slaughtered an animal for them. They ate from the sheep and the date-cluster and drank. When they had eaten and drunk their fill, God's Messenger said to them: “By Him in whose hand is my soul, you will surely be questioned about this enjoyment on the Day of Resurrection.” The report is abbreviated here.
Al-Wāḥidī transmits from Muqātil: “He means the unbelievers of Mecca. In this world they lived amid goodness and blessings. On the Day of Resurrection they will be questioned about gratitude for what they enjoyed. They did not thank the Lord of blessings, since they worshipped others and associated partners with Him; then they will be punished. This is al-Ḥasan's position.”
I say: this is supported by the fact that the address from the beginning of the sūrah is to those competing and boasting in abundance, who, as explained above, are unbelievers. Since preoccupation with worldly enjoyment is a characteristic of the heedless, and a believer must avoid base qualities, God's Messenger spoke sternly when he said, “You will be questioned about this enjoyment on the Day of Resurrection.” This does not mean that he was interpreting the verse by what he said.
Complete.



al-ʿAṣr · Heading and verses

K IV:793–793 · T XVI:565–567

Q 103:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ وَٱلْعَصْرِ ﴿1﴾ إِنَّ ٱلْإِنسَـٰنَ لَفِى خُسْرٍ ﴿2﴾ إِلَّا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّـٰلِحَـٰتِ وَتَوَاصَوْا۟ بِٱلْحَقِّ وَتَوَاصَوْا۟ بِٱلصَّبْرِ ﴿3﴾

1. By the declining day 2. Lo! man is a state of loss 3. Save those who believe and do good works, and exhort one another to truth and exhort one another to endurance

K103.01 · Heading and verses [IV:793]



Meccan; three verses. [The DKA edition’s heading additionally places its revelation after al-Sharḥ.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

By time! (1) Surely the human being is in loss, (2) except those who believe, do righteous deeds, counsel one another to the truth, and counsel one another to patience. (3)



al-ʿAṣr · The oath and the exception

K IV:793–793 · T XVI:565–567

Q 103:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ وَٱلْعَصْرِ ﴿1﴾ إِنَّ ٱلْإِنسَـٰنَ لَفِى خُسْرٍ ﴿2﴾ إِلَّا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّـٰلِحَـٰتِ وَتَوَاصَوْا۟ بِٱلْحَقِّ وَتَوَاصَوْا۟ بِٱلصَّبْرِ ﴿3﴾

1. By the declining day 2. Lo! man is a state of loss 3. Save those who believe and do good works, and exhort one another to truth and exhort one another to endurance

K103.02 · The oath and the exception [IV:793–794]



He swears by the afternoon prayer because of its excellence. Evidence is His saying, “and the middle prayer—the afternoon prayer,” in Ḥafṣa’s codex [cf. 2:238], and the Prophet’s saying, “Whoever misses the afternoon prayer is as though he had been deprived of his family and property.” Moreover, the obligation to perform it is more demanding because people rush into their trading and earning toward the end of the day and are occupied with their livelihoods.

Or He swears by the late afternoon, just as He swears by the morning brightness, because both contain signs of divine power. Or He swears by time because of the many kinds of wonders in its passage.

“The human being” denotes the species. Khusr (خسر) means khusrān (خسران), loss, just as kufr is used for kufrān (كفران). The meaning is that people lose in their trading, except the righteous alone: they purchase the hereafter with this world, and so profit and attain felicity. Everyone else trades in the opposite way and falls into loss and wretchedness.

“And counsel one another to the truth”: to the established reality that cannot rightfully be denied. It encompasses all good: affirming God’s oneness and obeying Him, following His books and messengers, renouncing attachment to this world, and desiring the hereafter.

“And counsel one another to patience”: in refraining from acts of disobedience, performing acts of obedience, and bearing the trials with which God tests His servants.



T103.01 · “As though deprived of his family and property” [XVI:565]

فكأنما وتر أهله وماله

Meccan; three verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

On “as though he had been deprived of his family and property,” al-Nihāya says: “Wutira (وُتِرَ) means diminished. You say watartuhu when you diminish someone, as though making him solitary after he had been many. It is also said to derive from watr, an injury: the person who misses the afternoon prayer is likened to someone whose intimate companion has been killed or whose family and property have been taken from him.

“The report is transmitted with ‘family’ both accusative and nominative. Those who put it in the accusative make it the second object of wutira (وُتِرَ), and understand within the verb an unexpressed passive subject referring to the person who missed the prayer. Those who put it in the nominative posit no such pronoun, but make ‘family’ the passive subject, since they are the ones struck by the loss and taken away. Whoever attributes the deprivation to the man puts both nouns in the accusative; whoever attributes it to family and property puts them in the nominative.”



T103.02 · Rushing, time, and mutual counsel [XVI:566]

لتهافت

On “because they rush” (li-tahāfut (لتهافت)): it means falling down piece by piece. Tahāfut al-farāsh fī l-nār (تهافت الفراش في النار) means the moths’ falling into the fire.

On “or He swears by time,” al-Zajjāj says: “Al-ʿaṣr (العصر) means time; al-ʿaṣr (العصر) also means day, and al-ʿaṣr (العصر) means night.” Ḥumayd ibn Thawr says:
“The two times—day and night—do not delay,
when they seek something, in overtaking what they aim at.”

On “‘and counsel one another to the truth’: to the established reality,” and what follows, al-Rāghib says: “Waṣiyya (وصية) is giving another person advance instructions about something to be done, accompanied by admonition and sincere advice. It derives from their expression arḍ wāṣiya (أرض واصية): land whose vegetation is continuous. One says awṣāhu (أوصاه) and waṣṣāhu (وصّاه); a people tawāṣaw (تواصوا) when they counsel one another.” One says qaddamtu ilayhi bi-kadhā (بكذا) when one commands someone before the time comes for the action to be needed.



T103.03 · The four requirements of deliverance [XVI:567]



The Imām [Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī] says: “The verse contains a severe threat, for it judges all people to be in loss except whoever possesses faith, righteous action, mutual counsel to the truth, and mutual counsel to patience. This indicates that deliverance depends on these things together. Just as the morally responsible person must acquire what concerns his own self, he must also, regarding others, call to religion, give sincere advice, command right and forbid wrong, and love for another what he loves for himself. Mutual counsel is then repeated so that the first includes calling to God and the second remaining steadfast in it.”

End of the sūrah.



al-ʿAṣr · Closing report

K IV:793–793 · T XVI:565–567

Q 103:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ وَٱلْعَصْرِ ﴿1﴾ إِنَّ ٱلْإِنسَـٰنَ لَفِى خُسْرٍ ﴿2﴾ إِلَّا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّـٰلِحَـٰتِ وَتَوَاصَوْا۟ بِٱلْحَقِّ وَتَوَاصَوْا۟ بِٱلصَّبْرِ ﴿3﴾

1. By the declining day 2. Lo! man is a state of loss 3. Save those who believe and do good works, and exhort one another to truth and exhort one another to endurance

K103.03 · Closing report [IV:794]



From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites the sūrah ‘By time,’ God forgives him, and he is among those who counsel one another to the truth and counsel one another to patience.”



al-Humazah · Heading and verses

K IV:794–796 · T XVI:568–576

Q 104:1–9

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ وَيْلٌ لِّكُلِّ هُمَزَةٍ لُّمَزَةٍ ﴿1﴾ ٱلَّذِى جَمَعَ مَالًا وَعَدَّدَهُۥ ﴿2﴾ يَحْسَبُ أَنَّ مَالَهُۥٓ أَخْلَدَهُۥ ﴿3﴾ كَلَّا ۖ لَيُنۢبَذَنَّ فِى ٱلْحُطَمَةِ ﴿4﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا ٱلْحُطَمَةُ ﴿5﴾ نَارُ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمُوقَدَةُ ﴿6﴾ ٱلَّتِى تَطَّلِعُ عَلَى ٱلْأَفْـِٔدَةِ ﴿7﴾ إِنَّهَا عَلَيْهِم مُّؤْصَدَةٌ ﴿8﴾ فِى عَمَدٍ مُّمَدَّدَةٍۭ ﴿9﴾

1. Woe unto every slandering traducer 2. Who hath gathered wealth (of this world) and arranged it 3. He thinketh that his wealth will render him immortal 4. Nay, but verily he will be flung to the Consuming One 5. Ah, what will convey unto thee what the Consuming One is 6. (It is) the fire of Allah, kindled 7. Which leapeth up over the hearts (of men) 8. Lo! it is closed in on them 9. In outstretched columns

K104.01 · Heading and verses [IV:794]



Meccan; nine verses. [The DKA heading additionally places its revelation after al-Qiyāmah.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

Woe to every backbiter and slanderer, (1) who amasses wealth and counts it over, (2) thinking that his wealth has made him immortal. (3) No indeed! He will surely be cast into the Crusher. (4) And what will make you know what the Crusher is? (5) God’s kindled fire, (6) which rises over the hearts. (7) It is closed over them, (8) amid extended pillars. (9)



al-Humazah · Backbiting, ridicule, and a general warning

K IV:794–796 · T XVI:568–576

Q 104:1

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ وَيْلٌ لِّكُلِّ هُمَزَةٍ لُّمَزَةٍ ﴿1﴾

1. Woe unto every slandering traducer

K104.02 · Backbiting, ridicule, and a general warning [IV:794–795]



Hamz (همز) means breaking, like hazm (هزم); lamz (لمز) means piercing. One says lamazahu (لمزه) and lahazahu (لهزه), “he stabbed him.” What is intended is breaking people’s reputations, diminishing them, backbiting them, and attacking them. The pattern fuʿala (فُعَلة) indicates that this is a habit to which the person has become addicted. Comparable forms are luʿana (لُعَنة), a habitual curser, and ḍuḥaka (ضُحَكة), a habitual laugher. The poet says:
“And when I am absent, you are the backbiter, the slanderer.”

The reading waylun li-l-humazati l-lumaza, “woe to the backbiter, the slanderer,” is also transmitted. Another reading has humza lumza with the mīm (ميم (م)) quiescent: this is the laughing-stock who produces outlandish acts and comical displays, and is laughed at and abused.

It is said to have been revealed concerning al-Akhnas ibn Sharīq, whose habit was backbiting and defamation; or concerning Umayya ibn Khalaf; or al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra and his backbiting and disparaging God’s Messenger. The occasion may be particular while the threat is general, so as to include everyone who undertakes this ugly conduct, and to operate as an indirect allusion to the person concerning whom it was revealed. That is more restraining and more wounding to him.



T104.01 · Breaking reputations and the correspondence of punishment [XVI:568–569]

الهمز: الكسر

Meccan; nine verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

On “hamz (همز) means breaking”: according to some, hamz (همز) is like squeezing with the hand. One says, “I squeezed the thing in my palm”; from this comes hamz (همز) in letters. Hamz (همز) of a person is backbiting him. One says hāmiz (هامز), hammāz (همّاز), and humaza of a man.

On “diminishing them,” al-Jawharī says: “Ghaḍḍa (غَضّة) minhu yaghuḍḍu (يغضّ), with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) in the imperfect, means that he lowered and diminished his standing.” Others relate to it lowering the gaze and voice, and restraining blame.

On “the pattern fuʿala (فُعَلة) indicates that this is a habit,” al-Intiṣāf says: “How fine is the correspondence of humaza and lumaza with ḥuṭama (حُطَمة)! Having marked him with this description and with a form indicating rootedness and firmness, He threatens him with this corresponding attribute, so that deed and recompense balance one another.”

I say: there is another subtle correspondence. Hamz (همز) includes breaking reputations; ḥaṭm (حطم) includes breaking ribs. Nabdh, casting away, conveys contempt and belittlement, since the man imagined himself to belong among the honoured. Under His saying, ‘We seized him and his troops and cast them into the sea’ [28:40], he says: “To express contempt for them and belittle their number, He likens them to little pebbles which someone takes in his palm and throws into the sea.” Al-Wāḥidī transmits from Muqātil: “It crushes bones and consumes flesh until it assails the hearts.”



T104.02 · The poetic example and strange utterances [XVI:569–570]

وإن أغيب فأنت الهامز اللمزة

On “And when I am absent, you are the backbiter, the slanderer”: it is said that the opening is:
“You proffer affection for me, falsely, when you meet me.”

Al-Zajjāj cites these words of Ziyād al-Aʿjam:
“When I meet you, you bare your teeth in a smile despite your resentment;
when I am absent, you are the backbiter, the slanderer.”

Ibn al-Sikkīt says: “Kashr means smiling. Kashara al-rajulu, iftarra, and ibtasama all mean that his teeth become visible.”

On “outlandish acts,” al-Asās says: “Among figurative uses is ‘So-and-so is devoted to awābid (أوابد) of speech,’ meaning its strange expressions, and to awābid (أوابد) of poetry, those poems whose excellence is unmatched.”



T104.03 · General language and indirect rebuke [XVI:570–571]

ويجوز أن يكون السبب خاصا والوعيد عاما

On “the occasion may be particular while the threat is general”: the Imām transmits from al-Farrāʾ: “An expression’s being general does not contradict a particular person’s being intended. For instance, someone says to you, ‘I have never visited you,’ and you say, ‘Everyone who has not visited me, I will not visit.’ In legal theory this is called restricting a general expression by a contextual indication of customary usage.”

On “to operate as an indirect allusion to the person concerning whom it was revealed”: if that person is al-Akhnas, Umayya, or al-Walīd, and the wording is made general as an indirect rebuke, this is more restraining and wounding. His name has not been explicitly stated so that he would put on the leopard’s hide against the person confronting him. Instead it prompts him to reflect on his own state: has he entered this general category before everyone else by backbiting the best of creation and diminishing his due? Al-Asās says: “Nakaytu fī (في) l-ʿaduwwi nikāyatan means I inflicted many wounds upon the enemy. One says, ‘So-and-so inflicts little hurt but complains at length.’”



al-Humazah · Accumulation and counting

K IV:794–796 · T XVI:568–576

Q 104:2

ٱلَّذِى جَمَعَ مَالًا وَعَدَّدَهُۥ ﴿2﴾

2. Who hath gathered wealth (of this world) and arranged it

K104.03 · Accumulation and counting [IV:795]



“Who” is a substitute for “every,” or accusative in a construction of blame. Jammaʿa (جَمَّعَ) is also read with the consonant doubled, matching ʿaddadahu (عدّده), “counts it over.” Another explanation takes ʿaddadahu (عدّده) to mean that he makes it a reserve against the events of time.

Wa-ʿadadahu (وعَدَدَه) is also read: he gathers the wealth, keeps track of its amount, and counts it; or he gathers his wealth and the people who support him. This derives from saying that someone possesses ʿadad (عَدَد) and ʿudad (عُدَد) when he has a plentiful number of helpers and the resources to sustain them. It is also said that wa-ʿadadahu (وعَدَدَه) means wa-ʿaddahu (وعدّه), “and counted it,” with the assimilation undone, like ḍaninū (ضَنِنُوا) for ḍannū (ضَنّوا), “they were miserly.”



T104.04 · The relative clause and the unassimilated form [XVI:571–572]

أو نصب على الذم

On “or accusative in a construction of blame”: it is said that it may be an adjective of kull, “every,” because kull is definite. Thus under “Every soul—with it a driver and a witness” [50:21], he says that “with it a driver” is circumstantial and accusative in position, referring to kull, which becomes definite through being annexed to something treated as definite.

On ḍaninū (ضَنِنُوا): that is, in the poet’s words:
“Gently, reproacher! Have you learned from my character
that I am generous to people even when they are miserly?”

His saying “It is also said that wa-ʿadadahu (وعَدَدَه) means wa-ʿaddahu (وعدّه)” is coordinated with “wa-ʿadadahu (وعَدَدَه): he gathers the wealth and keeps track of its amount.” On this latter analysis, it is the object of an omitted verb, as in:
“I fed her straw and cold water.”



al-Humazah · The illusion of immortality

K IV:794–796 · T XVI:568–576

Q 104:3

يَحْسَبُ أَنَّ مَالَهُۥٓ أَخْلَدَهُۥ ﴿3﴾

3. He thinketh that his wealth will render him immortal

K104.04 · The illusion of immortality [IV:795–796]



Akhladahu (أخلده) and khalladahu (خلّده) have the same meaning. Wealth has lengthened his hopes and filled him with distant aspirations, until, through excessive heedlessness and long hopes, he thinks that wealth has left him immortal in this world, never to die. Or, in erecting buildings secured with stone and brick, planting trees, and cultivating the earth, he acts as someone who thinks his wealth has kept him alive.

Or it is an indirect allusion to righteous action: that is what makes its possessor abide eternally in bliss, whereas wealth has made no one abide there eternally.

It is related that al-Akhnas possessed four thousand dinars; another account says ten thousand. From al-Ḥasan: he visited a wealthy man who was ill. The man said, “What do you say of thousands which I have neither used to ransom myself from a base person nor bestowed upon an honourable one?” He asked, “But what did you keep them for?” The man said, “For the hostility of the times, the ruler’s harshness, the calamities of life, and fear of poverty.” He replied, “Then you will leave them to someone who will not praise you, and come before One who will not excuse you.”



T104.05 · Two main readings, with three resulting explanations [XVI:572–573]

أو يعمل

On “or he acts”: this is coordinated with “he thinks.” “Or it is an indirect allusion” is coordinated, in sense, with the whole explanation beginning “wealth has lengthened his hopes.” Hence the expression changes. There are thus two principal interpretations which yield three explanations.

The analysis is this: “he thinks” is circumstantial, referring to the pronoun in “amasses.” The supposition concerns either immortality in this world or everlasting bliss, as with the speaker who said, “If I am returned to my Lord, I shall certainly find a better place of return than this” [18:36], and al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil, who said, “I shall certainly be given wealth and children” [19:77]. On the first interpretation, the supposition is either literal—as intended by “he thinks that wealth has left him immortal in this world”—or figurative, as intended by “or, in erecting buildings, he acts,” just as God says, “Do you build on every elevation a monument, amusing yourselves, and take strong structures as though you would live for ever?” [26:128–129]. On the second interpretation, the verse contains an indirect allusion.

It is appropriate on the first interpretation to make “who” a substitute for “every.” The meaning is: woe to the man who amassed wealth and counted it, then entertained long hopes and fell into delusion, thinking his wealth had left him immortal in this world. On the second interpretation, it should be accusative for blame. The meaning is: woe to the impious slanderer—I mean the one whom accumulating wealth and relying on men emboldened to slander and impiety, while he also thinks his wealth will make him live eternally in bliss. “No indeed! He will surely be cast into the Crusher.” What makes its possessor abide in the lasting bliss of Paradise is righteous action, as in His saying, “The day when neither wealth nor sons avail, except one who comes to God with a sound heart” [26:88–89]. The two interpretations thus unfold what was folded together in “who is a substitute for every, or accusative in a construction of blame.” God knows best.



al-Humazah · The Crusher and the hearts

K IV:794–796 · T XVI:568–576

Q 104:4–7

كَلَّا ۖ لَيُنۢبَذَنَّ فِى ٱلْحُطَمَةِ ﴿4﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا ٱلْحُطَمَةُ ﴿5﴾ نَارُ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمُوقَدَةُ ﴿6﴾ ٱلَّتِى تَطَّلِعُ عَلَى ٱلْأَفْـِٔدَةِ ﴿7﴾

4. Nay, but verily he will be flung to the Consuming One 5. Ah, what will convey unto thee what the Consuming One is 6. (It is) the fire of Allah, kindled 7. Which leapeth up over the hearts (of men)

K104.05 · The Crusher and the hearts [IV:796]



“No indeed!” restrains him from his supposition. La-yunbadhānni (لَيُنْبَذَانِّ) is also read: he and his wealth will surely be cast away. Another reading has la-yunbadhunna with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) on the dhāl (ذال (ذ)): he and his supporters will be cast away. La-yanbidhannahu, “he/it will surely cast him,” is also read.

“Into the Crusher”: into the fire whose nature is to crush everything thrown into it. Of a voracious man one says, “He is a crusher.” Al-ḥāṭima (الحاطمة), “the crushing one,” is also read. The meaning is that it enters their interiors until it reaches their breasts and rises over their afʾida (أفئدة), the centres of their hearts. Nothing in the human body is more delicate than the fuʾād (فؤاد) or more acutely pained by the slightest hurt touching it. How, then, when Jahannam’s fire rises over it and takes possession of it?

The hearts may also be singled out because they are the seats of unbelief, corrupt beliefs, and evil intentions. The fire’s rising over them means that it overtops, overcomes, and encompasses them. Or, figuratively, it examines the mines from which what necessitates it is drawn.



T104.06 · Ransoming one’s honour and the rebuke [XVI:573–574]

لم أفتد بها من لئيم

On “I have not used them to ransom myself from a base person”: I did not make my wealth a ransom for my honour, to escape his harm. He cites:
“I protect my honour with my wealth and do not stain it;
may God not bless wealth once honour is lost!”

On “the hostility of the times,” al-Asās says: “Nabā (نبا) ʿannī fulān means he parted from me. Between us is nabwa, estrangement. He complains of the times’ estrangement and harshness.”

On “‘No indeed!’ restrains him from his supposition,” the Imām says: “It is not as he imagined, that wealth and numbers make one immortal; rather, knowledge and righteousness do. ʿAlī, God be pleased with him, said: ‘The hoarders of wealth have died while still alive, but the learned remain as long as time remains.’ Or kallā (كلّا) means ‘truly’: he will surely be cast away. The lām (لام (ل)) answers an oath, indicating the presence of an oath in kallā (كلّا). Casting away conveys humiliation and contempt, since he imagined himself among the honoured.”



T104.07 · The fuʾād and the fire’s discernment [XVI:574–575]

ولا شيء في بدن الإنسان ألطف من الفؤاد

On “nothing in the human body is more delicate than the fuʾād (فؤاد),” al-Rāghib says: “Fuʾād (فؤاد) is like qalb (قلب), ‘heart,’ but it is called fuʾād (فؤاد) when the sense of tafaʾʾud (تفؤّد), burning, is considered in it. Faʾadtu (فأدت) al-laḥma (اللحمة) means I roasted the meat; laḥm (لحم) faʾīd (فئيد) is roasted meat. Singling out the hearts in ‘which rises over the hearts’ calls attention to the extreme effect upon them.”

On “or, figuratively, it examines the mines from which what necessitates it is drawn”: choosing the word “mines” hints at the reverse of the Prophet’s saying, “People are mines, like mines of gold and silver.” Since these people’s hearts are the settled seats of the pollution and filth of corrupt beliefs that necessitate the Fire, and the commencement of each person’s burning is fixed according to what he deserves, it is said that the Fire figuratively examines the mines of what necessitates it.

In al-Taysīr, Abū Saʿīd says: “It knows how much punishment each of them deserves because of the unbelief and corrupt beliefs in his heart.” This comes from saying iṭṭalaʿa (اطّلع) fulānun ʿalā amrinā: he became acquainted with our affair and knew it. God has made the fire such that it burns each person according to his deserts, neither adding nor subtracting, as though it had ascertained the amount he deserves. He says: “Since it may be described as enraged and as calling whoever turned his back and withdrew, it may also be described in this way.”



al-Humazah · Closed doors and extended pillars

K IV:794–796 · T XVI:568–576

Q 104:8–9

إِنَّهَا عَلَيْهِم مُّؤْصَدَةٌ ﴿8﴾ فِى عَمَدٍ مُّمَدَّدَةٍۭ ﴿9﴾

8. Lo! it is closed in on them 9. In outstretched columns

K104.06 · Closed doors and extended pillars [IV:796]



“Closed”: shut over them. The poet says:
“My she-camel longs for the mountains of Mecca,
while before her the gates of Ṣanʿāʾ are closed.”

Fī (في) ʿumud (عُمُد) is read with two ḍammas (ضَمَّات (ـُ)); ʿumd (عُمْد) with the mīm (ميم (م)) quiescent; and ʿamad (عَمَد) with two fatḥas (فَتَحَات (ـَ)). The meaning is that their despair of escape and certainty of everlasting imprisonment are reinforced: the doors are closed upon them and pillars stretched across the doors, fastening upon fastening.

Or the meaning is that it is closed over them while they are secured in extended pillars, like the stocks in which thieves are confined. God, protect us from the Fire, O best of those in whom refuge is sought!



T104.08 · Closure and the readings of “pillars” [XVI:575]

{مؤصدة

On “muʾṣada (مؤصدة): closed,” al-Rāghib says: “A waṣīda (وصيدة) is a chamber made in a mountain for storing property. One says awṣadtu al-bāba (أوصدت الباب) and āṣadtuhu (آصدته): I closed the door and secured it. God Most High says, ‘Over them is a closed fire’ [90:20]; it is also read with hamza (همزة (ء)).”

On “fī (في) ʿumud (عُمُد) is read”: Abū Bakr, Ḥamza, and al-Kisāʾī read it with two ḍammas (ضَمَّات (ـُ)); the others with two fatḥas (فَتَحَات (ـَ)).

On “pillars are stretched across the doors”: it is said that, on this interpretation, fī (في) ʿumud (عُمُد) is circumstantial, referring to the pronoun in muʾṣada (مؤصدة)—namely the one referring to the doors. On the interpretation “secured in pillars,” it is circumstantial, referring to the pronoun in ʿalayhim, “over them.”



T104.09 · Stocks, the particular occasion, and the general scope [XVI:576]

مثل المقاطر

On “like the stocks,” al-Jawharī says: “The miqṭara (مقطرة) is the falaq (فلق): a piece of wood with holes into which prisoners’ legs are inserted.”

I say: the first interpretation suits the report that the verse was revealed concerning al-Akhnas ibn Sharīq, Umayya ibn Khalaf, or al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra and his backbiting God’s Messenger. Having explained that the Crusher is the fire which examines the mines of what necessitates it, God follows this with “It is closed over them.” That is, the fire has ascertained these people’s deserts for backbiting the best of humankind, and is therefore closed and shut over them. Their despair of escape and certainty of everlasting imprisonment are thus reinforced.

The second interpretation suits taking “every backbiter and slanderer” generally. This is indicated by his saying, “the laughing-stock who produces outlandish acts and comical displays,” because he attacks people’s reputations like a thief stealing their property. On this interpretation, their remaining for ever in the Fire would follow.

End of the sūrah.



al-Humazah · Closing report

K IV:794–796 · T XVI:568–576

Q 104:1–9

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ وَيْلٌ لِّكُلِّ هُمَزَةٍ لُّمَزَةٍ ﴿1﴾ ٱلَّذِى جَمَعَ مَالًا وَعَدَّدَهُۥ ﴿2﴾ يَحْسَبُ أَنَّ مَالَهُۥٓ أَخْلَدَهُۥ ﴿3﴾ كَلَّا ۖ لَيُنۢبَذَنَّ فِى ٱلْحُطَمَةِ ﴿4﴾ وَمَآ أَدْرَىٰكَ مَا ٱلْحُطَمَةُ ﴿5﴾ نَارُ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْمُوقَدَةُ ﴿6﴾ ٱلَّتِى تَطَّلِعُ عَلَى ٱلْأَفْـِٔدَةِ ﴿7﴾ إِنَّهَا عَلَيْهِم مُّؤْصَدَةٌ ﴿8﴾ فِى عَمَدٍ مُّمَدَّدَةٍۭ ﴿9﴾

1. Woe unto every slandering traducer 2. Who hath gathered wealth (of this world) and arranged it 3. He thinketh that his wealth will render him immortal 4. Nay, but verily he will be flung to the Consuming One 5. Ah, what will convey unto thee what the Consuming One is 6. (It is) the fire of Allah, kindled 7. Which leapeth up over the hearts (of men) 8. Lo! it is closed in on them 9. In outstretched columns

K104.07 · Closing report [IV:796]



From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites Sūrat al-Humazah, God gives him ten good deeds for every person who mocked Muḥammad and his companions.”



al-Fīl · Heading and verses

K IV:797–799 · T XVI:577–584

Q 105:1–5

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ أَلَمْ تَرَ كَيْفَ فَعَلَ رَبُّكَ بِأَصْحَـٰبِ ٱلْفِيلِ ﴿1﴾ أَلَمْ يَجْعَلْ كَيْدَهُمْ فِى تَضْلِيلٍ ﴿2﴾ وَأَرْسَلَ عَلَيْهِمْ طَيْرًا أَبَابِيلَ ﴿3﴾ تَرْمِيهِم بِحِجَارَةٍ مِّن سِجِّيلٍ ﴿4﴾ فَجَعَلَهُمْ كَعَصْفٍ مَّأْكُولٍۭ ﴿5﴾

1. Hast thou not seen how thy Lord dealt with the owners of the Elephant 2. Did He not bring their stratagem to naught 3. And send against them swarms of flying creatures 4. Which pelted them with stones of baked clay 5. And made them like green crops devoured (by cattle)

K105.01 · Heading and verses [IV:797]



Meccan; five verses. [The DKA heading additionally places its revelation after al-Kāfirūn.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

Have you not seen how your Lord dealt with the People of the Elephant? (1) Did He not bring their scheme to nothing, (2) and send against them birds in flocks, (3) pelting them with stones of sijjīl (سجّيل), (4) and make them like eaten crop-stalks? (5)



al-Fīl · Abraha’s expedition

K IV:797–799 · T XVI:577–584

Q 105:1–2

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ أَلَمْ تَرَ كَيْفَ فَعَلَ رَبُّكَ بِأَصْحَـٰبِ ٱلْفِيلِ ﴿1﴾ أَلَمْ يَجْعَلْ كَيْدَهُمْ فِى تَضْلِيلٍ ﴿2﴾

1. Hast thou not seen how thy Lord dealt with the owners of the Elephant 2. Did He not bring their stratagem to naught

K105.02 · Abraha’s expedition [IV:797]



It is related that Abraha ibn al-Ṣabbāḥ al-Ashram, ruler of Yemen on behalf of Aṣḥama the Negus, built a church in Ṣanʿāʾ, named it al-Qullays, and intended to divert the pilgrims to it. A man of Kināna went and “sat” in it at night, angering him. Another account says an Arab travelling party lit a fire, which the wind carried to the church and burned it. Abraha swore to demolish the Kaʿba and set out with the Abyssinians, bringing his enormous, powerful elephant Maḥmūd and twelve other elephants. Eight are also reported; another account says a thousand, while [another has] the elephant alone. [The transmitted conjunction is awkward; the alternatives are not harmonised.]

When he reached al-Mughammas, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib went out and offered him a third of Tihāma’s wealth to turn back. He refused, arranged his army, and put the elephant in front. Whenever they directed it toward the sanctuary it knelt and would not move; toward Yemen or any other direction it trotted. God sent black birds—also said to be green, or white—each carrying a stone in its beak and two in its feet, larger than a lentil and smaller than a chickpea. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have seen about a qafīz-measure of them at Umm Hāniʾ’s, striped red like Ẓafār agate.

The stone struck a man’s head and emerged from his rear, and each bore the name of the person it would strike. They fled and perished along every road and watering place. Abraha sickened; his fingertips and limbs fell away, and he did not die until his chest split open over his heart. His minister Abū Yaksūm escaped, with his bird circling above him, until he reached the Negus and told him the story. When he finished, the stone fell on him and he collapsed dead before him.



T105.01 · Names and vocabulary of the expedition [XVI:577–578]

الأشرم

Meccan; five verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

On al-Ashram: sharm means cutting the nose-tip or a she-camel’s vulva. It is said he was called al-Ashram because his father struck him with a spear and split his nose and forehead.

On “he sat in it at night”: an indirect expression meaning he relieved himself. Al-Mughammas is said to be a place between Mecca and Minā. On “he arranged his army,” al-Jawharī says: “ʿAbbaytu (عبّيت) l-jaysha taʿbiyatan (تعبيةً), taʿbiʾatan (تعبئةً) and taʿbīʾan (تعبيئًا) means I stationed it in its positions; Abū Zayd gives ʿabaʾtuhu (عبأته) with hamza (همزة (ء)).”

On “Abraha sickened”: dawā (دواء), with short final alif (ألف (ا)), means illness; dawiya, with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)), means became ill. It is also explained as “sickened from disease.” On “his limbs”: irb means a limb, as in prostrating on seven ārāb (آراب). On “a bird circling”: taḥlīq (تحليق) means rising high in flight.



al-Fīl · ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and the prayer

K IV:797–799 · T XVI:577–584

Q 105:1–2

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ أَلَمْ تَرَ كَيْفَ فَعَلَ رَبُّكَ بِأَصْحَـٰبِ ٱلْفِيلِ ﴿1﴾ أَلَمْ يَجْعَلْ كَيْدَهُمْ فِى تَضْلِيلٍ ﴿2﴾

1. Hast thou not seen how thy Lord dealt with the owners of the Elephant 2. Did He not bring their stratagem to naught

K105.03 · ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and the prayer [IV:797–798]



It is said that Abraha was the grandfather of the Negus who lived in the Messenger’s time, [Abraha’s rule being] forty years [before the prophetic mission]; twenty-three years is also reported. [The bracketed temporal clarification follows Ṭībī’s explanation below.] ʿĀʾisha says: “I saw the elephant’s driver and keeper, blind and crippled, begging for food.”

The narrative also relates that Abraha took two hundred camels belonging to ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. He went to recover them, and his imposing appearance impressed Abraha, for he was large and handsome. Abraha was told, “This is Quraysh’s chief and the lord of Mecca’s caravan, who feeds people on the plain and wild animals on the mountain-tops.” When he stated his request, Abraha said, “You have fallen in my estimation. I have come to destroy the House that is your religion and your fathers’ religion, your protection and your ancient honour, yet a few confiscated camels distract you from it!” He replied, “I am the lord of the camels; the House has a Lord who will protect it.” He returned to the House’s door, grasped its ring, and said:
“O God, a man protects his household: protect Your people!
Let not their cross and their power soon overcome Your power.
If You leave them with our Kaʿba, then [do] whatever seems good to You.
Lord, I hope for none against them but You;
Lord, protect Your sanctuary from them.”



T105.02 · Chronology and appearance [XVI:579]

الذي كان في زمن النبي? 

“Who lived in the Prophet’s time” distinguishes which Negus is meant. The author of al-Jāmiʿ says: “Negus is the title of Abyssinia’s king. The one who became Muslim and believed in the Prophet was Aṣḥama. He became Muslim before the conquest, died before it, and the Prophet prayed over him.” [His wording is retained.]

“Forty years” means before the prophetic mission. It is a second predicate of the first kāna: he existed and ruled forty years before the mission. This account is closer than twenty-three years, since, by the agreement of transmitters, the Prophet was born in the Elephant’s year and commissioned forty years later. The Negus accepted Islam in the fifth year after the mission. Ibn al-Jawzī reports that the Messenger was born on Monday, ten days into Rabīʿ al-Awwal in the Elephant’s year; Ibn Isḥāq gives twelve days. Ibn Qutayba says: “They agree that God’s Messenger was born in the Elephant’s year.”

“Concerning them” means concerning the camels and recovering them. On “his appearance impressed him,” al-Asās says: “Jahartuhu, ijtahartuhu and istajhartuhu: I saw him as imposing in appearance. Jaharanī (جهرني) fulān: his beauty and bearing struck me with awe.”



T105.03 · The prayer’s wording [XVI:580–581]

ذود أخذ لك

Dhawd (ذود) denotes between three and ten camels. Though the camels were very numerous, Abraha seemingly diminishes their number to belittle the request and deter him from making it in that situation.

On the verses beginning “Lāhumma (لاهمّ)”: its original form is Allāhumma (اللهمّ). Riḥālaka (رحالك) is transmitted, and also ḥilālaka (حلالك), plural of ḥilla (حِلّة), a place where people settle. Ḥilāl (حلال), with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)), also means people settled together as neighbours: the sanctuary’s inhabitants. Al-Asās gives “I settled among the people” and “I settled in the dwelling”; it is their maḥalla and ḥilla (حِلّة); a tribe described as ḥilla (حِلّة) or ḥilāl (حلال) is settled in one place.

On “their cross”: one says the Byzantines came with crosses. Maḥāla (محالة) and miḥāl (محال) mean resourceful contrivance, as in “Man fails, but resourcefulness does not.” Miḥāl (محال) is also said to mean punishment or strength, as in “He is mighty in power” [13:13].

In “fa-amrun mā (ما),” mā (ما) is either additional for reinforcement or relative: whatever benefit seems good to You. Al-Nihāya explains ghaduwwan, with dotted ghayn (غين (غ)): “Ghaduww is the original form of ghad, the day following today; its final root letter was dropped. The full form occurs only in verse, as in:
‘People are only like dwellings and their inhabitants:
inhabited the day they settle, and tomorrow desolate.’
ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib did not mean the particular next day, but a near time.”



al-Fīl · The birds and “Have you not seen?”

K IV:797–799 · T XVI:577–584

Q 105:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ أَلَمْ تَرَ كَيْفَ فَعَلَ رَبُّكَ بِأَصْحَـٰبِ ٱلْفِيلِ ﴿1﴾ أَلَمْ يَجْعَلْ كَيْدَهُمْ فِى تَضْلِيلٍ ﴿2﴾ وَأَرْسَلَ عَلَيْهِمْ طَيْرًا أَبَابِيلَ ﴿3﴾

1. Hast thou not seen how thy Lord dealt with the owners of the Elephant 2. Did He not bring their stratagem to naught 3. And send against them swarms of flying creatures

K105.04 · The birds and “Have you not seen?” [IV:798–799]



As he prayed he turned and saw birds approaching from Yemen. “By God,” he said, “these are strange birds, neither seabirds nor birds of Tihāma.” The narrative also says that Mecca’s people took possession of the invaders’ property, and ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib amassed a great fortune from their jewels and gold, which made him wealthy. Asked about the birds, Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī said that Mecca’s pigeons descend from them. It is said that they arrived in the evening and attacked next morning. ʿIkrima says anyone struck developed smallpox, the first smallpox to appear.

Alam tar is also read with a quiescent rāʾ (راء (ر)), emphatically displaying the effect of the jussive governor. The meaning is: you have seen the effects of God’s act upon the Abyssinians and heard reports of it through widespread transmission (tawātur), so these take the place of direct witnessing. Kayfa, “how,” is accusative through “your Lord dealt,” not through “have you not seen,” because of its interrogative meaning.

“To nothing” (fī (في) taḍlīl (تضليل)): into waste and nullity. Ḍallala kaydahu means making a scheme stray and fail; compare “The unbelievers’ scheming is only in vain” [40:25]. Imruʾ al-Qays was called “the errant king” because he squandered his father’s kingdom. First they plotted against the House by building al-Qullays, intending to supersede it by directing pilgrims there; He frustrated their scheme by burning it. They then plotted to destroy the House; He frustrated that by sending the birds.



T105.04 · A great fortune [XVI:581]

الجور

Al-jawr (الجَوْر), with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) on jīm (جيم (ج)), quiescent wāw (واو (و)) and rāʾ (راء (ر)), occurs in a copy collated against the author’s handwriting: abundant wealth, so called because its accumulation exceeds the limit. Al-ḥawz (الحَوْز), with ḥāʾ (حاء (ح)) and zāy (زاي (ز)), is also transmitted. Al-Jawharī says: “Ḥawz is gathering; whoever brings something into his possession has ḥāzahu (حازه) ḥawzan (حوزًا) wa-ḥiyāzatan (وحيازةً), and iḥtāzahu (احتازه).” Al-juʾr (الجؤر) also occurs. Al-Jawharī says: “Ghayth juʾr (جؤر) is copious, abundant rain; juʾar (جؤر), like nughar, is also said.” They recite:
“Let no heavy, thunder-roaring downpour water it.”
ʿAzf (عزف) here is the rumble of thunder.



T105.05 · Seeing how, and the prelude to prophecy [XVI:582]

والمعنى: أنك رأيت آثار فعل الله بالحبشة

On “you have seen the effects of God’s act,” the Qāḍī says: “‘Have you not seen?’ addresses God’s Messenger. Though he did not witness the event, he saw its effects and heard widely transmitted reports, as though he had seen it. ‘How He dealt’ is said rather than ‘what He did’ because the point is to recall its indications of God’s perfect knowledge and power and His Prophet’s dignity and honour: it belongs to the anticipatory signs of prophecy.”

The Imām says: “Things have entities and modes. Their modes are what theologians call ‘the aspect establishing the proof’ (wajh al-dalīl). Desert of praise is discerned by seeing modes, not merely entities. Hence: ‘Have they not looked at the sky above them, how We built it?’ [50:6]. This event undoubtedly laid the foundation for his prophethood and anticipated his mission.” Irhāṣ (إرهاص) derives from rahṣ (رهص), the bottom course of a wall: something resembling a miracle precedes the prophetic claim, such as clouds shading the Messenger and stones and clods speaking to him.

Ḥazāʾiq (حزائق) means groups. Al-Asās gives ḥizqa (حزقة), ḥazīqa (حزيقة) and ḥazīq (حزيق) for a group, and “they followed one another like bands of locusts.”



al-Fīl · Flocks, sijjīl, and eaten stalks

K IV:797–799 · T XVI:577–584

Q 105:3–5

وَأَرْسَلَ عَلَيْهِمْ طَيْرًا أَبَابِيلَ ﴿3﴾ تَرْمِيهِم بِحِجَارَةٍ مِّن سِجِّيلٍ ﴿4﴾ فَجَعَلَهُمْ كَعَصْفٍ مَّأْكُولٍۭ ﴿5﴾

3. And send against them swarms of flying creatures 4. Which pelted them with stones of baked clay 5. And made them like green crops devoured (by cattle)

K105.05 · Flocks, sijjīl, and eaten stalks [IV:799–800]



Abābīl (أبابيل) means bands, singular ibbāla (إبّالة). Their proverb says, “A handful upon a great bundle.” Ibbāla (إبّالة) is the great bundle: the birds’ tightly gathered band is likened to it. It is also said that abābīl (أبابيل), like ʿabādīd (عباديد) and shamāṭīṭ (شماطيط), has no singular.

Abū Ḥanīfa read yarmīhim (يرميهم), “he pelts them”: God, or the birds, for ṭayr (طير) is a masculine collective noun and is made feminine only according to its meaning.

Sijjīl (سجّيل) appears to be a proper name for the register containing the unbelievers’ punishment, just as sijjīn (سجّين) names the register of their deeds: stones forming part of the recorded punishment. It derives from isjāl (إسجال), sending, because punishment is described as sent: “He sent against them birds”; “We sent against them the flood” [7:133]. Ibn ʿAbbās explains it as clay baked like bricks. It is also said to be Arabicised from Persian sang-gil. Another explanation is “from His severe punishment,” citing Ibn Muqbil’s verse:
“A severe striking (sijjīlan (سجّيلًا)), enjoined by champions upon one another.”
But the actual word is sijjīnan (سجّينًا): the poem is a well-known nūn-rhyming poem in his collection.

They are likened to crop leaves when eaten—that is, infested by worms—or to straw eaten and excreted by animals. The expression observes Qurʾānic propriety, like “They both ate food” [5:75]. Or it means that the grain was eaten, leaving the stalks empty.

From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites Sūrat al-Fīl, God spares him throughout his life from being swallowed by the earth and from bodily transformation.”



T105.06 · Bundles, scattered bands, and the disputed verse [XVI:583–584]

ضغث على إبالة

On “a handful upon a great bundle,” al-Maydānī says: “Ibbāla (إبّالة) is a bundle of firewood; ḍighth (ضغث) a handful of mixed fresh and dry grass. Ībāla (إيبالة) is also transmitted, and some lighten it to ibāla (إبالة). Its meaning is one affliction upon another.”

On ʿabādīd (عباديد) and shamāṭīṭ (شماطيط), al-Jawharī says: “ʿAbādīd (عباديد) are groups of people going in every direction. Shamāṭīṭ (شماطيط) are scattered pieces; horses arriving shamāṭīṭ (شماطيط) arrive dispersed in separate groups.”

On isjāl (إسجال), sending, al-Asās says: “This is musjal: released, unrestricted; one may take it or leave it. Asjaltu l-bahīmata maʿa (مع) ummihā means I let the young animal go with its mother.”

On “His severe punishment,” al-Zajjāj says: “When Arabs describe something disagreeable as sijjīl (سجّيل) they mean severity; the term is not applied otherwise. Ibn Muqbil says:
‘And foot-soldiers striking the bright blades openly,
a severe striking, enjoined by champions upon one another.’”

In his book’s marginal note: “Abū ʿUbayda cited it thus in his Majāz (مجاز); Ibn Muqbil’s poetry has sijjīnan (سجّينًا), and that is correct. Rijla is the collective of rājil (راجل), foot-soldier. A thing’s ḍāḥiya (ضاحية) is its exposed side. Sijjīnan (سجّينًا) qualifies ḍarban (ضربًا).” Another transmission instead has “the bright blades from various sides” (al-bīḍ (البيض) ʿan (عن) ʿurḍ (عرض)). Al-bīḍ (البيض) means swords. ʿUrḍ (عرض), with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)), means the middle or side of something. Thus: many foot-soldiers strike with swords in battle from different sides, striking fiercely as champions enjoin upon one another.



T105.07 · Qurʾānic propriety and the empty stalks [XVI:584]

كقوله: {كانا يأكلان الطعام

On “like ‘they both ate food’” [5:75]: in the two verses animal dung and human waste are expressed indirectly, observing propriety. Their dismemberment is likened to the dispersal of particles of dung, at once preserving propriety and displaying the ugliness of their state and outcome.

On “its grain was eaten, leaving it empty”: devoid of good. The meaning is stalks whose grain has been eaten, like saying “So-and-so is beautiful” meaning beautiful of face: what is understood is omitted. This is al-Ḥasan’s interpretation.

End of the sūrah.



Quraysh · Heading, verses, and the governing construction

K IV:800–802 · T XVI:585–590

Q 106:1–4

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ لِإِيلَـٰفِ قُرَيْشٍ ﴿1﴾ إِۦلَـٰفِهِمْ رِحْلَةَ ٱلشِّتَآءِ وَٱلصَّيْفِ ﴿2﴾ فَلْيَعْبُدُوا۟ رَبَّ هَـٰذَا ٱلْبَيْتِ ﴿3﴾ ٱلَّذِىٓ أَطْعَمَهُم مِّن جُوعٍ وَءَامَنَهُم مِّنْ خَوْفٍۭ ﴿4﴾

1. For the taming of Qureysh 2. For their taming (We cause) the caravans to set forth in winter and summer 3. So let them worship the Lord of this House 4. Who hath fed them against hunger and hath made them safe from fear

K106.01 · Heading, verses, and the governing construction [IV:800–801]



Meccan; four verses. [The DKA heading additionally places its revelation after al-Tīn.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

For the accustomed security of Quraysh—(1) their accustomed winter and summer journey—(2) let them worship the Lord of this House, (3) who has fed them against hunger and secured them against fear. (4)

“For the accustomed security of Quraysh” (li-īlāfi (لإيلاف) Quraysh) depends grammatically on “let them worship”: He commands them to worship Him because He has accustomed them to the two journeys. If you ask why fāʾ (فاء (ف)) then enters, I reply: because the statement carries a conditional meaning. Its sense is: if for nothing else, let them worship Him for their accustomed security. God’s blessings upon them are innumerable; if they do not worship Him for His other blessings, let them worship Him for this one conspicuous blessing.

It is also said to mean: “Marvel at Quraysh’s accustomed security.” Another interpretation connects it to what precedes: “He made them like eaten stalks for the accustomed security of Quraysh.” This resembles enjambment (taḍmīn) in poetry: the meaning of one verse depends upon the preceding verse in such a way that it cannot stand correctly without it. In Ubayy’s codex the two are one sūrah, with no division. It is related of ʿUmar that he recited them both in the second unit of the sunset prayer, having recited “By the fig” in the first.

The meaning is that He destroyed the Abyssinians who intended to attack them, so that people would hear of this, stand in greater awe of them, and accord them increased respect. Thus security would be established for their two journeys and no one would dare attack them.



T106.01 · The fāʾ and the implied condition [XVI:585–586]

فلم دخلت الفاء

Meccan; four verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

On “why, then, does fāʾ (فاء (ف)) enter?”: the fāʾ (فاء (ف)) [in the question fa-lima] indicates objection. That is: if li-īlāf (لإيلاف) depends on fa-l-yaʿbudū (فليعبدوا), why does the fāʾ (فاء (ف)) of succession stand between the governing expression and what it governs? He answers that fāʾ (فاء (ف)) introduces the consequence of an omitted condition. This must be supplied, because if one reconstructs simply “let them worship Him for the accustomed security of Quraysh,” the fāʾ (فاء (ف)) remains without anything to which it relates.

It may also be understood as reinforcement, with fāʾ (فاء (ف)) marking succession: “For Quraysh’s accustomed security, let them worship Him—so let them worship.” Likewise His saying “so let them rejoice” [10:58]. Its permissibility has already been transmitted from al-Zubayr, from al-Zajjāj. His saying “and your Lord—magnify Him” [74:3] follows this pattern. He says: “Fāʾ (فاء (ف)) enters because of the conditional meaning, as though it said, ‘Whatever happens, do not cease to magnify Him.’”

On “the meaning is: immā (إمّا) lā (لا), let them worship Him”: the author is reported to have said, “The Arabs say ‘Do this, immā (إمّا) lā (لا),’ meaning ‘If you will not do something else, do this.’ Mā (ما) is additional, compensating for the omitted kāna. They pronounce lā (لا) with imāla (إمالة) because it stands in place of the verb, like balā, both taking the verb’s place. One also says, ‘Give me this, immā (إمّا) lā (لا).’”



T106.02 · Linking the two sūrahs [XVI:586]

فجعلهم كعصف مأكول لإيلاف فريش

On “He made them like eaten stalks for the accustomed security of Quraysh,” al-Zajjāj says: “The meaning is: God destroyed the People of the Elephant so that Quraysh and their accustomed winter and summer journeys might remain.”

On “in the second [unit] of the sunset prayer”: that is, in the second rakʿa; in the first rakʿa he recited Sūrat “By the fig.” This apparently indicates that they are one sūrah. [This is Ṭībī’s inference, retained as his statement.]



Quraysh · The journeys and variant forms

K IV:800–802 · T XVI:585–590

Q 106:1–2

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ لِإِيلَـٰفِ قُرَيْشٍ ﴿1﴾ إِۦلَـٰفِهِمْ رِحْلَةَ ٱلشِّتَآءِ وَٱلصَّيْفِ ﴿2﴾

1. For the taming of Qureysh 2. For their taming (We cause) the caravans to set forth in winter and summer

K106.02 · The journeys and variant forms [IV:801]



Quraysh had two journeys: they travelled to Yemen in winter and Syria in summer, obtaining provisions and trading. They were secure on both journeys because they were the people of God’s sanctuary and custodians of His House; no one molested them, whereas other people were seized and raided.

Īlāf (إيلاف) comes from ālaftu l-makāna (آلفت المكان), ūlifuhu īlāfan (أولفه إيلافًا): I became accustomed to a place; hence I am muʾlif (مؤلف). The poet says:
“Among those accustomed to the [herbage / easy journey], not those grazing arāk (أراك).”
[The DKA transcription reads al-rahw, “easy travelling”; Ṭībī’s printed base reads al-zahw and explains both forms below.]

The reading li-ilāfi (لإلاف) Quraysh also occurs, meaning “because of Quraysh’s becoming accustomed.” It is said that one says aliftuhu ilfan wa-ilāfan (وإلافًا). Abū Jaʿfar reads li-ilfi Quraysh. Both forms occur in the poet’s words:
“You claim that your brothers are Quraysh;
they have ilf (إلف), while you have no ilāf (إلاف).”



T106.03 · The camel verse and the verbal noun [XVI:587]

من المؤلفات

On “among those accustomed”: one says ālaftu l-makāna (آلفت المكان), ūlifuhu īlāfan (أولفه إيلافًا) when one becomes accustomed to a place; hence muʾlif (مؤلف). [The cited wording is] al-zahw ghayra l-awārik. Zahw (زهو) denotes being short of ripeness; zahw (زهو) also means herbage and dates beginning to colour. One says zahat al-ibilu zahwan when camels travel for a night or more after watering. Zahawtuhā, “I drove them,” is also used: the verb is both transitive and intransitive. Zāhiya (زاهية) camels do not graze saline plants. Some transmit al-rahw with rāʾ (راء (ر)): easy travelling. One says, “The horses came at an easy pace.” Awārik (أوارك) is the plural of ārika (آركة): camels eating arāk (أراك). Al-Jawharī says: “Arakat means that she stayed among the arāk (أراك), which belongs to the saline vegetation; she is ārika (آركة), plural awārik (أوارك).”

On “that is, because of Quraysh’s becoming accustomed”: according to this interpretation, ilāf (إلاف) is the verbal noun of fāʿala (فَاعَلَ), and means muʾālafa (مؤالفة), like ḍāraba (ضارب), muḍārabatan (مضاربةً) and ḍirāban (ضرابًا).



Quraysh · The name Quraysh and the syntax of “journey”

K IV:800–802 · T XVI:585–590

Q 106:1–2

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ لِإِيلَـٰفِ قُرَيْشٍ ﴿1﴾ إِۦلَـٰفِهِمْ رِحْلَةَ ٱلشِّتَآءِ وَٱلصَّيْفِ ﴿2﴾

1. For the taming of Qureysh 2. For their taming (We cause) the caravans to set forth in winter and summer

K106.03 · The name Quraysh and the syntax of “journey” [IV:802]



ʿIkrima reads li-yaʾlafa (ليألف) Qurayshun, ilfahum riḥlata l-shitāʾi wa-l-ṣayf (رحلة الشتاء والصيف). Quraysh are the descendants of al-Naḍr ibn Kināna. They were named with the diminutive of al-qirsh, a great sea creature that attacks ships and can be overcome only with fire. Muʿāwiya is reported to have asked Ibn ʿAbbās, “Why was Quraysh so named?” He replied, “After a sea creature that eats but is not eaten and overcomes but is not overcome,” and recited:
“Quraysh is the creature dwelling in the sea;
after it Quraysh was named Quraysh.”

The diminutive conveys magnification. Another explanation derives it from qarsh, earning, because they earned through trade and travelling through the lands.

Īlāf (إيلاف) is first stated without qualification, then replaced by an appositional expression specifying the two journeys, to magnify this accustomed security and remind them of the greatness of its blessing. Riḥla (رِحْلة) is accusative as the object of īlāfihim (إيلافهم), just as yatīman (يتيمًا) is made accusative by iṭʿām (إطعام) [90:14–15]. The two journeys of winter and summer are meant. The singular is used because confusion is impossible, as in:
“Eat into part of your belly [rather than fill your bellies].”



T106.04 · Two poems [XVI:588–589]

وقيل

On “it is also said”: this indicates a verbal noun of faʿala (فَعَلَ), like kataba kitāban (كتب كتابًا).

After the verse beginning “You claim” comes:
“Those people were secured against hunger and fear,
whereas the sons of Asad have hungered and feared.”

Its author is Musāwir ibn Hind, satirising Banū Asad. He says: you are not of Quraysh, nor are Quraysh of you; your claim to their brotherhood is false, for they were fed against hunger and secured against fear, whereas you were not. The author says: “This belongs to the verses with subtle meanings: the first hemistich reports their claim, while the second argues against them and binds them to its consequence.”

On the verse beginning “Quraysh is the creature”: al-Wāḥidī and Muḥyī l-Sunna transmit the following as al-Jumaḥī’s:
“Quraysh is the creature dwelling in the sea;
after it Quraysh was named Quraysh.
It eats the lean and the fat, and never leaves
a feather to anything with two wings.
So too, throughout the lands, the tribe Quraysh
devours the lands in vigorous fashion.
In the latter age they shall have a prophet
who multiplies killing and scratching wounds among them.”



T106.05 · The object and the destination [XVI:589]

كما نصب {يتيما

On “just as yatīman (يتيمًا) is made accusative by iṭʿām (إطعام)” [90:14–15], Abū l-Baqāʾ says: “Yatīman (يتيمًا) is the object of iṭʿām (إطعام). Some Basrans hold that when the verbal noun governs an object, it contains a pronoun like the pronoun within the active participle.”

On “the destination to which one travels,” al-Kawāshī says: “Originally riḥla (رِحْلة) meant travelling on a riding camel; it then came to be used for every kind of travel.”

End of the sūrah [XVI:590; no further unique gloss follows the final Kashshāf block].



Quraysh · Hunger, fear, and the concluding report

K IV:800–802 · T XVI:585–590

Q 106:2–4

إِۦلَـٰفِهِمْ رِحْلَةَ ٱلشِّتَآءِ وَٱلصَّيْفِ ﴿2﴾ فَلْيَعْبُدُوا۟ رَبَّ هَـٰذَا ٱلْبَيْتِ ﴿3﴾ ٱلَّذِىٓ أَطْعَمَهُم مِّن جُوعٍ وَءَامَنَهُم مِّنْ خَوْفٍۭ ﴿4﴾

2. For their taming (We cause) the caravans to set forth in winter and summer 3. So let them worship the Lord of this House 4. Who hath fed them against hunger and hath made them safe from fear

K106.04 · Hunger, fear, and the concluding report [IV:803]



Ruḥla (رُحْلة) is also read, with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)): the destination to which one travels. The indefiniteness of “hunger” and “fear” indicates their severity. Through the two journeys He fed them against the severe hunger they had previously suffered and secured them against great fear: fear of the People of the Elephant, or fear of being seized in their own country and on their routes.

It is also said that hardship had afflicted them until they ate carrion and burnt bones; and that He secured them against fear of leprosy, so that it does not afflict them in their country. All this, it is said, came through Abraham’s prayer. Among novel interpretations is: “He secured them against fear” that the caliphate might belong to others. Min khawf is also read with concealment of the nūn (نون (ن)) [ikhfāʾ (إخفاء)].

From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites the sūrah ‘For the accustomed security of Quraysh,’ God gives him ten good deeds for every person who circumambulates the Kaʿba and stays there in devotional retreat.”



al-Māʿūn · Heading, verses, and readings

K IV:803–805 · T XVI:591–599

Q 107:1–7

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ أَرَءَيْتَ ٱلَّذِى يُكَذِّبُ بِٱلدِّينِ ﴿1﴾ فَذَٰلِكَ ٱلَّذِى يَدُعُّ ٱلْيَتِيمَ ﴿2﴾ وَلَا يَحُضُّ عَلَىٰ طَعَامِ ٱلْمِسْكِينِ ﴿3﴾ فَوَيْلٌ لِّلْمُصَلِّينَ ﴿4﴾ ٱلَّذِينَ هُمْ عَن صَلَاتِهِمْ سَاهُونَ ﴿5﴾ ٱلَّذِينَ هُمْ يُرَآءُونَ ﴿6﴾ وَيَمْنَعُونَ ٱلْمَاعُونَ ﴿7﴾

1. Hast thou observed him who belieth religion 2. That is he who repelleth the orphan 3. And urgeth not the feeding of the needy 4. Ah, woe unto worshippers 5. Who are heedless of their prayer 6. Who would be seen (at worship) 7. Yet refuse small kindnesses

K107.01 · Heading, verses, and readings [IV:803]



The DKA heading describes the first three verses as Meccan and the remainder as Medinan, seven verses in all, and places revelation after al-Takāthur. Ṭībī’s embedded Kashshāf heading says: Meccan, also said to be Medinan; seven verses.
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

Have you seen the one who denies recompense? (1) That is the one who violently repulses the orphan (2) and does not urge feeding the needy. (3) So woe to those who pray, (4) yet are heedless of their prayer, (5) who make a display, (6) and withhold assistance. (7)

A-rayta is also read with the hamza (همزة (ء)) omitted. It is not the preferred form, since dropping that hamza (همزة (ء)) is peculiar to the imperfect; raytu (رأيت) is not established Arabic usage. What makes it easier here is the initial interrogative particle. Compare:
“Friend, have you seen or heard of a herdsman
returning to the udder what he gathered in the milking bowl?”



al-Māʿūn · The orphan and signs of denial

K IV:803–805 · T XVI:591–599

Q 107:1–7

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ أَرَءَيْتَ ٱلَّذِى يُكَذِّبُ بِٱلدِّينِ ﴿1﴾ فَذَٰلِكَ ٱلَّذِى يَدُعُّ ٱلْيَتِيمَ ﴿2﴾ وَلَا يَحُضُّ عَلَىٰ طَعَامِ ٱلْمِسْكِينِ ﴿3﴾ فَوَيْلٌ لِّلْمُصَلِّينَ ﴿4﴾ ٱلَّذِينَ هُمْ عَن صَلَاتِهِمْ سَاهُونَ ﴿5﴾ ٱلَّذِينَ هُمْ يُرَآءُونَ ﴿6﴾ وَيَمْنَعُونَ ٱلْمَاعُونَ ﴿7﴾

1. Hast thou observed him who belieth religion 2. That is he who repelleth the orphan 3. And urgeth not the feeding of the needy 4. Ah, woe unto worshippers 5. Who are heedless of their prayer 6. Who would be seen (at worship) 7. Yet refuse small kindnesses

K107.02 · The orphan and signs of denial [IV:804]



Ibn Masʿūd read a-raʾaytaka (أرأيتك), adding the address-marker, as in “Tell me of this one whom You have honoured above me” [17:62]. The meaning is: do you know who denies recompense? If not, that denier is the one who pushes the orphan away violently, with cruelty and injury, rejecting him harshly with rebuke. Yadaʿu (يدع) is also read: abandons and treats coldly. “Does not urge”: does not encourage his household to give the needy food.

Withholding kindness and daring to injure the weak are made the sign of denying recompense. Had he believed in recompense and been certain of the threat, he would have feared God and His punishment and not done this. His doing it reveals denial. How severe these words, how fearful the situation, how forceful the warning against sin and its fitness to indicate weak faith and a slack bond of certainty!

“So woe to those who pray” is then connected: if this is so, woe to those who neglect prayer from indifference, until they miss it or its time passes; or who fail to perform it as the Messenger and the early generations did, but peck through it without humility and submission or avoiding disliked acts—playing with beard and clothes, excessive yawning and looking about. One finishes without knowing how many units he prayed or which sūrahs he recited, like the prayer of so many you see, habitually displaying their deeds and withholding their wealth’s dues.

Their neglect of prayer, religion’s pillar and the distinction between faith and unbelief; their ostentation, a branch of associating partners with God; and their refusal of alms, prayer’s counterpart and Islam’s bridge, are all the more fitting signs that they deny recompense. How many bearing Islam’s name—even scholars—you see like this! What a calamity!



T107.01 · The heading, reading, and poetic illustration [XVI:591–592]

قرئ: "أريت"

Ṭībī’s own heading: Medinan; six verses. [This differs from the embedded base text and is preserved, not silently corrected.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

A-rayta is al-Kisāʾī’s reading. The initial interrogative makes a second hamza (همزة (ء)) burdensome, hence its omission. The verse beginning “Friend” resembles Abū l-Ṭayyib’s:
“Youth once gone cannot be recovered;
no passing day can be brought back.”
Ṣāḥ (صاح) originally means yā ṣāḥib (صاحب), shortened by truncation. Qary is gathering water in a basin. An ʿulba (عُلْبة) is a wooden milking bowl, plural ʿulab (عُلَب) or ʿilāb (عِلاب). The poet asks whether his friend ever saw or heard of a herdsman returning to the udder milk already drawn and gathered in the bowl.

On adding the address-marker: the kāf (كاف (ك)) reinforces the address already expressed by tāʾ (تاء (ت)). Al-Rāghib says: “Ḥaḍḍ (حضّ) is urging, like ḥathth (حثّ), except that ḥathth (حثّ) may concern travel and driving onward, while ḥaḍḍ (حضّ) does not. Its origin is urging onto the ḥaḍīḍ (حضيض), the ground’s lower level.”



T107.02 · Culpable heedlessness and hurried prayer [XVI:593–594]

الذين يسهون عن الصلاة

Al-Rāghib says: “Sahw (سهو) is an error from inattention, of two kinds. One arises from causes a person himself brought about: someone drinks wine and commits wrongdoing without intending it. The other does not arise from his own causes: an insane person insults someone. The second is pardoned; the first is accountable. The first kind is what God condemns in ‘woe to those who pray, heedless of their prayer.’”

“Or fail to perform it” is conjoined to “neglect prayer.” Thus neglect means delaying prayer beyond its time through indifference, or inattentively omitting its constituent acts, forms, proprieties and stillness. Hence “they peck through it like a bird pecking grain.” Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasāʾī transmit from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān (الرحمن) ibn Shibl: the Messenger forbade pecking like a crow, spreading oneself like a predatory beast, and claiming a habitual place as a camel does.

Al-Bukhārī and al-Nasāʾī transmit from Zayd ibn Wahb: Ḥudhayfa saw a man praying deficiently and asked how long he had prayed this way. “Forty years.” “You have not prayed for forty years. If you died praying thus, you would die outside Muḥammad’s original way.” He added, “A person may pray briefly yet completely and well.”



T107.03 · The escalating argument [XVI:594–595]

والرياء ... ومنع الزكاة

“Ostentation” and “withholding alms” are nominative, conjoined to “their neglect,” the subject of yakūna (يكون); “a sign” is the predicate, understood similarly for the coordinated terms, as in:
“We with what we have, and you with what you have,
are content, though judgment differs.”

These things are signs of denying recompense because “woe to those who pray” follows as consequence from cause and result from condition, with an escalation: do you recognise the denier? He repulses the orphan and withholds his good. Do you recognise something still greater and more dreadful? Abandoning prayer and alms and displaying one’s deeds are greater, because worship is the intrinsic purpose of the world’s creation. On this account māʿūn (ماعون) must mean withholding alms, completing the mention of prayer rather than introducing a further escalation. Denial of recompense thus fundamentally nullifies the wisdom in creating heaven and earth, legislating worship, and urging all other good deeds. God protect us from it.

The Imām says: “Denying resurrection is like the root of every kind of unbelief and sin. God makes harming the weak and withholding kindness its sign: belief in recompense and certainty of the threat would prevent them. Denial of resurrection therefore produces the sin.”



al-Māʿūn · Another construction; neglect of prayer versus lapses in it

K IV:803–805 · T XVI:591–599

Q 107:1–5

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ أَرَءَيْتَ ٱلَّذِى يُكَذِّبُ بِٱلدِّينِ ﴿1﴾ فَذَٰلِكَ ٱلَّذِى يَدُعُّ ٱلْيَتِيمَ ﴿2﴾ وَلَا يَحُضُّ عَلَىٰ طَعَامِ ٱلْمِسْكِينِ ﴿3﴾ فَوَيْلٌ لِّلْمُصَلِّينَ ﴿4﴾ ٱلَّذِينَ هُمْ عَن صَلَاتِهِمْ سَاهُونَ ﴿5﴾

1. Hast thou observed him who belieth religion 2. That is he who repelleth the orphan 3. And urgeth not the feeding of the needy 4. Ah, woe unto worshippers 5. Who are heedless of their prayer

K107.03 · Another construction; neglect of prayer versus lapses in it [IV:804–805]



Alternatively fa-dhālika (فذلك) is conjoined to “the one who denies,” either joining one person to another or one attribute to another. The answer to a-raʾayta (أرأيت) is omitted, indicated by what follows: tell me what you say about one who denies recompense and one who harms the orphan and does not feed the needy—is his conduct good? Then: “woe to those who pray,” meaning, once his wrongdoing is known, woe to them. Their description replaces their pronoun, because along with denial and the other attributed acts they neglected prayer, made a display, and failed to purify their property by alms.

If you ask how the plural “those who pray” replaces the pronoun of the singular “one who denies,” I reply: the meaning is plural, because the class is intended.

What distinguishes “heedless of their prayer” (ʿan (عن)) from “heedless in their prayer” (fī (في))? ʿAn (عن) means neglecting and abandoning it, paying it little attention: the conduct of hypocrites or brazenly sinful Muslims. Fī (في) means lapses occurring during it through Satan’s whispering or inward thoughts, from which scarcely any Muslim is free. Even God’s Messenger had lapses during prayer, let alone others; hence jurists include chapters on the prostration for forgetfulness. Anas said, “Praise God that He did not say ‘in their prayer.’” Ibn Masʿūd read lāhūna (لاهون), “distracted.”



T107.04 · Who are “those who pray”? [XVI:595–596]

إما عطف ذات على ذات، أو صفة على صفة

On joining persons or attributes: in the first earlier construction fāʾ (فاء (ف)) answers an omitted condition, “If you do not know, then recognise him as that denier.” If a particular person is intended, “the one” denotes someone known; if an attribute, it can also denote the class. Hence the differing identifications: Muqātil gives al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil; al-Suddī and Muqātil give al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra; Ibn ʿAbbās gives a hypocrite. These occur in al-Maʿālim.

Al-Kawāshī says: “Do not pause after ‘the needy’ if ‘the one’ denotes the class and the praying people belong to the same statement. What a-raʾayta (أرأيت) relates to is then omitted: what do you say of one who denies truth, repulses the orphan and injures the needy—has he acted well? Woe to them! ‘Those who pray’ replaces ‘them.’”

I say: this shows that on the first interpretation “woe to those who pray” is separate from the preceding statement as regards its referent. The denier is an unbeliever such as al-Walīd or al-ʿĀṣ; the praying people are Muslims. Making injury and withheld kindness signs of denial signals their severity, so believers avoid such conduct, characteristic of unbelievers denying Judgment Day. Hence the author’s exclamations about the severe words and fearful situation, and their indication of weak faith.

His saying “he then connected ‘woe to those who pray,’ as though saying ‘if the matter is thus’” also indicates distinct referents: he mentions “the matter,” without saying an explicit noun replaces a pronoun. By contrast, on the final interpretation he expressly says “woe to them.” There the praying people are the deniers, since he says they were heedless of prayer “alongside their denial.” The Imām says: “On this interpretation the verse shows an unbeliever receiving additional punishment for prohibited acts and omitted religious obligations. This supports al-Shāfiʿī’s position that unbelievers are addressed by the subsidiary ordinances of revealed law.”



T107.05 · Objections to the distinction between ʿan and fī [XVI:597]

وعن أنس: الحمد لله على أن لم يقل: في صلاتهم

The Imām says: “ʿAṭāʾ transmits from Ibn ʿAbbās: had God said ‘heedless in their prayer,’ believers would be more exposed to this threat. Instead He says ‘heedless of their prayer’: not remembering it and being disengaged from it. This interpretation is weak: heedlessness of prayer cannot mean abandoning prayer, since ‘those who pray’ affirms their prayer; moreover, abandoning it through heedlessness is neither hypocrisy nor unbelief.

“The first objection may be answered: He calls them praying people regarding the outward prayer, and says they have entirely forgotten prayer regarding its meaning, as in ‘When they stand for prayer, they stand lazily, showing themselves to people, and remember God only a little’ [4:142].”

I say: “those who pray” may mean those whose proper role is to discharge gratitude owed for God’s blessings. He therefore attributes prayer to them—“their prayer”—indicating an established duty upon the morally responsible. They should not pass from maintaining its pillars, forms and customary practices into inattention, much less abandonment. This rests on unbelievers’ being addressed by subsidiary ordinances.

The Imām says: “The second objection is answered by understanding forgetfulness of prayer as remaining forgetful of God throughout every part. This proceeds only from a hypocrite who thinks prayer useless. A Muslim who believes its benefits cannot fail throughout all its parts to recall religion, reward or punishment. He may indeed lapse during some parts. Thus inattention in prayer is a believer’s act, and inattention of prayer an unbeliever’s.” [The argumentative claim is translated as his, without turning it into a diagnostic rule.]



al-Māʿūn · Ostentation and assistance

K IV:803–805 · T XVI:591–599

Q 107:6–7

ٱلَّذِينَ هُمْ يُرَآءُونَ ﴿6﴾ وَيَمْنَعُونَ ٱلْمَاعُونَ ﴿7﴾

6. Who would be seen (at worship) 7. Yet refuse small kindnesses

K107.04 · Ostentation and assistance [IV:805–806]



If you ask what ostentation (murāʾāh (مراءاة)) means, I reply: it is the reciprocal form from showing. The ostentatious person shows others his deed, and they show him praise and admiration. Displaying a righteous deed does not make someone ostentatious if it is obligatory. Obligations should be public and manifest, as in the Prophet’s saying, “There is no concealment in God’s obligations”: they are Islam’s signs and religion’s rites. Their omission deserves blame and abhorrence, so suspicion must be removed by making performance visible.

A voluntary deed should be concealed: leaving it incurs neither blame nor suspicion. Showing it so that others follow the example is good. Ostentation consists specifically in showing it so that eyes see and people praise one’s righteousness. Someone saw a man prolonging a prostration of thanksgiving in the mosque and said, “How fine this would be in your house!” He said so because he discerned display and desire for reputation. Avoiding ostentation is difficult except for those trained in sincerity. Hence the Messenger said, “Ostentation is more hidden than a black ant’s movement on black haircloth in a dark night.”

Al-māʿūn (الماعون) means obligatory alms. Al-Rāʿī says:
“A people remaining in Islam, who have not withheld
