their alms nor neglected declaring God’s oneness.”

Ibn Masʿūd explains it as things ordinarily lent among people: an axe, cooking pot, bucket, fire-kindler and the like. ʿĀʾisha says: water, fire and salt. Withholding these may be forbidden when urgently needed, and contrary to decent conduct even when necessity is absent.

From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites Sūrat ‘Have you seen,’ God forgives him if he pays the obligatory alms.”



T107.06 · Public obligations, the poem, and lending [XVI:598–599]

ولا غمة

“No concealment” is also transmitted as “no gharar (غرر).” Al-Nihāya explains Wāʾil ibn Ḥujr’s report: God’s obligations are not covered and hidden; they are displayed, announced and voiced publicly.

The poem “a people remaining in Islam” alludes to apostates who withheld alms: we are not apostates, so do not treat us like them.

On things ordinarily passed between borrowers, al-Jawharī says: “Iʿtawarū (اعتوروا) l-shayʾ means they passed it among themselves; taʿawwarūhu (تعوّروه) and taʿāwarūhu (تعاوروه) mean the same.”

End of the sūrah.



al-Kawthar · Heading, verses, and the meaning of al-kawthar

K IV:806–807 · T XVI:600–605

Q 108:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِنَّآ أَعْطَيْنَـٰكَ ٱلْكَوْثَرَ ﴿1﴾ فَصَلِّ لِرَبِّكَ وَٱنْحَرْ ﴿2﴾ إِنَّ شَانِئَكَ هُوَ ٱلْأَبْتَرُ ﴿3﴾

1. Lo! We have given thee Abundance 2. So pray unto thy Lord, and sacrifice 3. Lo! it is thy insulter (and not thou) who is without posterity

K108.01 · Heading, verses, and the meaning of al-kawthar [IV:806]



Meccan; three verses. [The DKA heading additionally places its revelation after al-ʿĀdiyāt.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

We have indeed given you abundant good. (1) So pray to your Lord and sacrifice. (2) Surely it is the one who hates you who is cut off. (3)

In the recitation of God’s Messenger: innā (إِنّا) anṭaynāka (أنطيناك), with nūn (نون (ن)) [instead of the ʿayn (عين (ع)) in aʿṭaynāka (أعطيناك)]. In his saying: “And give the middling portion” (wa-anṭū (وأنطوا) l-thabaja).

Al-kawthar (الكوثر) has the pattern fawʿal (فوعل), from kathra, abundance: it means what is exceedingly abundant. A Bedouin woman whose son had returned from a journey was asked, “What did your son bring back?” She replied, “He returned with kawthar (كوثر).” The poet says:
“You are abundant in good, son of Marwān, and wholesome;
your father, the son of noble women, was abundant in good.”



T108.01 · The dialectal verb and the poem [XVI:600–601]

وأنطوا الثبجة

Meccan; three verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

On “and give the middling portion,” al-Nihāya says: “This is the language of Yemen. He wrote to Wāʾil: ‘Anṭū (أنطوا) l-thabaja,’ meaning: give the middle quality as alms, neither the choicest property nor its poorest. The feminine ending was attached because the word shifted from noun to adjective.”

On “son of noble women”: women of the choicest kind; the ʿaqīla (عقيلة) of anything is its noblest part. A kawthar (كوثر) among men is one abundant in good and giving. The verse is by al-Kumayt.



al-Kawthar · The river and its first visitors

K IV:806–807 · T XVI:600–605

Q 108:1

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِنَّآ أَعْطَيْنَـٰكَ ٱلْكَوْثَرَ ﴿1﴾

1. Lo! We have given thee Abundance

K108.02 · The river and its first visitors [IV:807]



It is also said that al-Kawthar (الكوثر) is a river in Paradise. From the Prophet: when this sūrah was revealed, he recited it and said, “Do you know what al-Kawthar (الكوثر) is? It is a river in Paradise that my Lord has promised me, containing abundant good.” Its description is transmitted as follows: “Sweeter than honey, whiter than milk, colder than snow, and softer than butter; its banks are chrysolite, and its vessels are silver, as numerous as the stars of heaven.”

It is also transmitted: “Whoever drinks from it never thirsts again. The first to reach it are the poor Emigrants: dirty in clothing, dishevelled in hair, who are not given pampered women in marriage and for whom the doors of the entrance halls are not opened.” One of them dies with his need still turning within his breast; were he to adjure God, God would fulfil his oath.

From Ibn ʿAbbās: he interpreted al-kawthar (الكوثر) as abundant good. Saʿīd ibn Jubayr said to him, “Some people say it is a river in Paradise!” He replied, “That is part of the abundant good.” [The attribution differs in the report that Ṭībī quotes below; both authors’ formulations are retained.]



T108.02 · Reports concerning the river [XVI:601]

إنه نهر في الجنة

On “it is a river in Paradise”: we transmit in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said concerning al-kawthar (الكوثر): “It is abundant good.” Ibn Jubayr was asked, “But people maintain that it is a river in Paradise?” Saʿīd replied, “The river in Paradise is part of the good that God Most High gave him.”

From Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal, al-Tirmidhī, Ibn Mājah and al-Dārimī, from Ibn ʿUmar: God’s Messenger said, “Al-Kawthar (الكوثر) is a river in Paradise. Its banks are gold, and its course runs over pearls and rubies. Its soil is more fragrant than musk, and its water sweeter than honey and whiter than snow.”

In ʿĀʾisha’s report: “Its banks are hollowed pearls, and its vessels as numerous as the stars of heaven.” Al-Bukhārī transmitted it.



T108.03 · Those denied entry at the doors [XVI:602]

لا تفتح لهم أبواب السدد

On “for whom the doors of the entrance halls are not opened”: the report is transmitted by al-Tirmidhī from Thawbān. God’s Messenger said: “My pool is as extensive as the distance between Aden and ʿAmmān of al-Balqāʾ. Its water is whiter than snow and sweeter than honey, and its cups are as numerous as the stars of heaven. Whoever drinks from it never thirsts afterward. The first people to reach me there are the poor Emigrants, dishevelled in hair and dirty in clothing, who do not marry pampered women and for whom the doors of the entrance halls are not opened.”

Al-Tirmidhī says: ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said, “I have married a pampered woman, Fāṭima daughter of ʿAbd al-Malik, and the doors of the entrance halls have been opened for me. Therefore I shall not wash my head until it becomes dishevelled, nor the garment next to my body until it becomes dirty.”

In al-Jāmiʿ: “Sudad is the plural of sudda, which here means door.” In al-Nihāya: “A sudda is like an awning over a doorway, protecting it from rain. It is also said to be the open space before the door, or the door itself: that is, doors are not opened for them. In Abū l-Dardāʾ’s report, he came to Muʿāwiya’s door and was not admitted. He said, ‘Whoever frequents the ruler’s entrance is made to stand and sit.’”

I say: the more fitting interpretation is to take the genitive construction ‘doors of the sudad’ as explanatory, making it an indirect expression for the doors of kings and great men, with sudda understood as the awning or forecourt.



T108.04 · The report concerning al-Rubayyiʿ [XVI:602–603]

لو أقسم على الله لأبره

On “were he to adjure God, God would fulfil his oath”: he said this in the report concerning al-Rubayyiʿ. We transmit from al-Bukhārī, Muslim, Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasāʾī, from Anas ibn Mālik (مَالِك): his paternal aunt al-Rubayyiʿ broke a girl’s incisor. They sought a pardon from her people, but they refused. They offered compensation, but they refused. They came to God’s Messenger and insisted on retaliation. God’s Messenger ordered retaliation.

Anas ibn al-Naḍr said, “Messenger of God, will al-Rubayyiʿ’s incisor be broken? No! By Him who sent you with the truth, her incisor shall not be broken.” God’s Messenger said, “Anas, does not God’s Book prescribe retaliation?” Then the people were satisfied and pardoned her. God’s Messenger said, “Among God’s servants are those who, were they to adjure God, He would fulfil their oath.” It means: were such a person to ask God, He would answer him. Adjuration here means an appeal for favour (istiʿṭāf (استعطاف)).



al-Kawthar · Prayer, sacrifice, and the double gift

K IV:806–807 · T XVI:600–605

Q 108:2

فَصَلِّ لِرَبِّكَ وَٱنْحَرْ ﴿2﴾

2. So pray unto thy Lord, and sacrifice

K108.03 · Prayer, sacrifice, and the double gift [IV:807]



Naḥr (نحر) means slaughtering sacrificial camels. According to ʿAṭiyya, the prayer is the dawn prayer at Jamʿ [Muzdalifa], and the slaughter is at Minā. It is also said to mean the festival prayer and sacrifice. Another interpretation takes prayer generically and naḥr (نحر) as placing the right hand over the left.

The meaning is: you have been given limitless good in both abodes, such as no one else has been given, and the giver of all this is I, the God of the worlds. Thus the two exalted grounds for being envied without ill will have come together for you: receiving the noblest and most abundant gift from the most generous giver and greatest benefactor. So worship your Lord, who has honoured you through His giving, ennobled you, and protected you from dependence on the favours of created beings, thereby defying your people who worship other than God. When you slaughter, slaughter for His sake and in His name, opposing their slaughter for idols.



T108.05 · The Giver and the gift [XVI:603]

ومعطي ذلك كله أنا إله العالمين

On “the giver of all this is I, the God of the worlds”: this signals a preference for Ibn ʿAbbās’s interpretation, that al-kawthar (الكوثر) means abundant good. It also draws out what the plural pronoun conveys of greatness and majesty, for its speaker is none other than the God of the worlds; and [the text is difficult here] “even if the gift were not great, the Giver is great.”

Because of these two correspondences, “So pray to your Lord” follows, with an explicit noun replacing a pronoun. That is: since both gift and Giver are great, perform the greatest bodily and financial acts of worship that you can. Slaughter is preferred as the expression so as to incorporate the meaning of cutting the soul off from immediate pleasures. “Surely it is the one who hates you who is cut off” is then joined to this, completing the good news. The Imām says: “Having given him good news of immense blessings, and knowing that their completion consists in the subjugation of enemies, He said, ‘Surely it is the one who hates you who is cut off.’”

Al-Sulamī transmits from Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq: “We have given you a light in your heart that guided you to Me and cut you off from everything besides Me.” From al-Qāsim: “Your hater is the one cut off from the good things of both abodes.” God knows best.



al-Kawthar · The one truly cut off

K IV:806–807 · T XVI:600–605

Q 108:3

إِنَّ شَانِئَكَ هُوَ ٱلْأَبْتَرُ ﴿3﴾

3. Lo! it is thy insulter (and not thou) who is without posterity

K108.04 · The one truly cut off [IV:807–808]



It is the person among your people who hates you because you oppose them who is cut off, not you. Every believer born until the Day of Resurrection is among your children and descendants. Your remembrance is raised on pulpits and minarets and on the tongue of every scholar and person who remembers God until the end of time: God is mentioned first and you second. In the hereafter you have what description cannot encompass. Someone such as you is not called cut off. The one cut off is your hater, forgotten in this world and the hereafter; if mentioned, he is mentioned with a curse.

They used to say, “Muḥammad is a ṣunbūr (صنبور): when he dies, his remembrance dies.” It is said that the verse was revealed concerning al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil, who called him al-abtar. Al-abtar means someone without offspring; hence a tailless donkey is called al-ḥimār (الحمار) al-abtar.



T108.06 · Minarets, indirect expression, and ṣunbūr [XVI:604]

والمنار

On “and the manār (منار),” al-Nihāya says: “Manār (منار) is the plural of manāra (منارة): the marker between two boundaries. Hence Abū Hurayra’s report, ‘Islam has waymarks and manār (منار)’: signs and ordinances by which it is recognised.” It is also said that manāʾir (منائر) is the plural of the manāra (منارة) from which the call to prayer is made. Its original form is manāwir (مناور), since it derives from nūr (نور), light; hamza (همزة (ء)) replaces wāw (واو (و)). A root letter may be treated like an added one, as in maṣāʾib (مصائب), originally maṣāwib (مصاوب).

On “someone such as you is not called cut off”: this resembles “someone such as you is not miserly” as an indirect expression (kināya). It means: someone with your attribute—every believer born until the end of time being his child—is not called cut off.

On ṣunbūr (صنبور), al-Nihāya says: “A person cut off, without offspring. Originally ṣunbūr (صنبور) is a palm shoot growing on the trunk rather than in the ground. It is also said to be a solitary palm whose lower part is slender. They meant that when he was uprooted his remembrance would cease, just as the trace of a ṣunbūr (صنبور) disappears, because he has no offspring.”



al-Kawthar · Closing report

K IV:806–807 · T XVI:600–605

Q 108:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِنَّآ أَعْطَيْنَـٰكَ ٱلْكَوْثَرَ ﴿1﴾ فَصَلِّ لِرَبِّكَ وَٱنْحَرْ ﴿2﴾ إِنَّ شَانِئَكَ هُوَ ٱلْأَبْتَرُ ﴿3﴾

1. Lo! We have given thee Abundance 2. So pray unto thy Lord, and sacrifice 3. Lo! it is thy insulter (and not thou) who is without posterity

K108.05 · Closing report [IV:808]



From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites Sūrat al-Kawthar (الكوثر), God gives him drink from every river in Paradise, and ten good deeds are recorded for him for every offering the servants have offered on the Day of Sacrifice or will offer.”



T108.07 · “Or will offer” [XVI:605]

أو يقربونه

On “or will offer”: according to some, “or” distinguishes the kinds [past and future offerings].

End of the sūrah.



al-Kāfirūn · Heading and verses

K IV:808–809 · T XVI:606–612

Q 109:1–6

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلْكَـٰفِرُونَ ﴿1﴾ لَآ أَعْبُدُ مَا تَعْبُدُونَ ﴿2﴾ وَلَآ أَنتُمْ عَـٰبِدُونَ مَآ أَعْبُدُ ﴿3﴾ وَلَآ أَنَا۠ عَابِدٌ مَّا عَبَدتُّمْ ﴿4﴾ وَلَآ أَنتُمْ عَـٰبِدُونَ مَآ أَعْبُدُ ﴿5﴾ لَكُمْ دِينُكُمْ وَلِىَ دِينِ ﴿6﴾

1. Say: O disbelievers 2. I worship not that which ye worship 3. Nor worship ye that which I worship 4. And I shall not worship that which ye worship 5. Nor will ye worship that which I worship 6. Unto you your religion, and unto me my religion

K109.01 · Heading and verses



Meccan; six verses. [K-DKA adds: revealed after al-Māʿūn (الماعون).] This sūrah and Sūrat al-Ikhlāṣ are called al-Muqashqishatān (المقشقشتان): the two that clear one of hypocrisy.
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.
“Say: O unbelievers! I do not worship what you worship, nor are you worshippers of what I worship. Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped, nor are you worshippers of what I worship. To you your religion, and to me my religion.”



al-Kāfirūn · Those addressed and the occasion of revelation

K IV:808–809 · T XVI:606–612

Q 109:1

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلْكَـٰفِرُونَ ﴿1﴾

1. Say: O disbelievers

K109.02 · Those addressed and the occasion of revelation



Those addressed are particular unbelievers whom God knew would not believe. It is reported that a group of Quraysh said: “Muḥammad, come: follow our religion and we will follow yours. You worship our gods for a year and we will worship your God for a year.” He replied: “God forbid that I associate anything else with God!” They said: “Then touch some of our gods in reverence; we will believe you and worship your God.” The sūrah was then revealed. In the morning he went to the Sacred Mosque, where the leaders of Quraysh were gathered, stood before them, and recited it to them. They lost hope.



T109.01 · Grammar of the proposed bargain; istilām (XVI, 606–607)

ونتبع

On “and we will follow”: according to one explanation, this is coordinated with the syntactic position of “then follow,” because if that expression were an imperfect verb, it would be jussive as the response to “come” (halumma (هلمّ)). “We will worship,” and what follows, explains the proposal.
On “then touch in reverence” (fa-stalim): that is, kiss. One says istalama al-ḥajara, meaning that he touched the Stone with his hand; the expression was then extended to every kind of contact.



al-Kāfirūn · Future and past; the use of mā

K IV:808–809 · T XVI:606–612

Q 109:2–5

لَآ أَعْبُدُ مَا تَعْبُدُونَ ﴿2﴾ وَلَآ أَنتُمْ عَـٰبِدُونَ مَآ أَعْبُدُ ﴿3﴾ وَلَآ أَنَا۠ عَابِدٌ مَّا عَبَدتُّمْ ﴿4﴾ وَلَآ أَنتُمْ عَـٰبِدُونَ مَآ أَعْبُدُ ﴿5﴾

2. I worship not that which ye worship 3. Nor worship ye that which I worship 4. And I shall not worship that which ye worship 5. Nor will ye worship that which I worship

K109.03 · Future and past; the use of mā



“I do not worship” intends worship in the future, because lā (لا) is used only with an imperfect verb having a future meaning, just as mā (ما) is used only with an imperfect having a present meaning. Do you not see that lan emphatically negates what lā (لا) negates? Al-Khalīl said that lan originally consists of lā (لا) an. The meaning is: in the future I will not do what you ask of me, namely worship your gods; nor will you do in the future what I ask of you, namely worship my God.
“Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped” means: at no time in the past was I a worshipper of what you worshipped then. You never knew me to worship an idol in the age of ignorance; how, then, can this be expected of me in Islam? “Nor are you worshippers of what I worship” means: at no time have you worshipped that which I now worship.
If you ask why He did not say “what I worshipped” (mā (ما) ʿabadtu (عبدت)), as He said “what you worshipped” (mā (ما) ʿabadtum (عبدتم)), I answer: they used to worship idols before the prophetic mission, whereas he was not worshipping God at that time. [Ṭībī's sustained objection to this statement is translated below.]
If you ask why mā (ما), “what,” is used rather than man, “who,” I answer: the intended consideration is the description, as though He said, “I do not worship the false, and you do not worship the true.” It is also said that mā (ما) forms a verbal noun: I do not perform your worship, and you do not perform my worship.



T109.02 · Why “what I worship” rather than “what I worshipped”? (607–608)

فهلا قيل

On “why did He not say…?”: “Nor are you worshippers of what I worship” is paired with “nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped.” Why, then, is the second altered to “what I worship,” when “what I worshipped” would appear to be expected, corresponding to “what you worshipped” in the first?
On “he was not worshipping God at that time”: al-Intiṣāf says: “This statement is mistaken both in its underlying principle and in its consequence. As for the principle, the Qadarī holds that before his mission the Prophet was not following the religion of a previous prophet, because that would be a blemish in his standing and discourage people from following him. They also hold that all people are obliged, by the requirements of reason, to reflect upon God's signs and the proofs of His oneness and knowledge of Him, and that reflection is obligatory through reason rather than revealed instruction. That is worship before the mission, and they must not suppose that he failed to perform it. Their own principle thus entails that he worshipped God before his mission.
“Al-Zamakhsharī preserved his principle concerning the absence of adherence to an earlier prophet, but failed to draw the consequence of his other principle: the rational obligation of worship. The truth is that he worshipped before revelation and devoted himself to religious observance in the cave of Ḥirāʾ. If the use of ‘I worship’ is because the worship intended by the verse had not occurred in the past, the matter concerning ‘what I worshipped’ should be understood as the full body of actual worship known only through revealed law, not the mere affirmation of God's oneness and knowledge of Him, which were always established for him before the mission.
“As for its imperfect form, this vividly presents his worship to the hearer's mind and fixes it there. Compare: ‘Have you not seen that God sent down water from the sky, and the earth becomes green?’ (22:63). The expected form is ‘became’; it is altered for the reason just stated.”
I say: it may be understood as continuity in both past and future, as indicated by the parallel expressions. Compare: “Those who recite God's Book and established prayer” (35:29), where a past verb is coordinated with an imperfect. The correct position is that before the mission he practiced worship according to a revealed law.



T109.03 · Earlier revealed law before and after the mission (608–609)



Ibn al-Jawzī transmits in al-Wafāʾ from Imām Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal: “To say that God's Messenger followed his people's religion is an evil statement. Did he not refrain from eating what was slaughtered on the sacrificial stones?” Abū al-Wafāʾ ʿAlī ibn ʿAqīl said: “Before his mission God's Messenger practiced as religion whatever he knew to be soundly established of Abraham's revealed law. After his mission, did he practice according to the law of those before him? There are two transmitted positions.
“The first is that he practiced whatever was soundly established of the laws of those before him through revelation to him, not through their own accounts, transmissions, or revealed books. Abū al-Ḥasan al-Tamīmī preferred this position; it is also the position of Abū Ḥanīfa's followers.
“The second is that he was bound by none of the earlier laws, but only by what was revealed to him of his own law. This is the position of the Muʿtazila and the Ashʿarīs. Al-Shāfiʿī's followers have two views corresponding to these two transmitted positions.
“Those who say he practiced according to an earlier law differ over which law it was. Some say Moses' law, apart from what was abrogated in our law. The apparent sense of Aḥmad's words is that he practiced everything soundly established as the law of an earlier prophet, unless its abrogation was established, because God says: ‘Those are the ones God guided; follow, then, their guidance’ (6:90).
“Ibn Qutayba said: The Arabs continued to observe remnants of Ishmael's religion. These included pilgrimage to the House, circumcision, making divorce final when it reached three, the husband's right to take his wife back after one or two divorces, a blood-money payment of one hundred camels for a life, washing after major ritual impurity, and the prohibition of marriage to those forbidden through blood relationship or marriage affinity. God's Messenger thus shared their belief in God and their practice of these religious ordinances. As for ‘You did not know what the Book was, nor faith’ (42:52), this means the ordinances of faith, not faith in the sense of acknowledging God.” Here ends Ibn al-Jawzī's discussion.



T109.04 · Ṭībī's objection to the distribution of past and future (609)

ما أعبد

I say: the author's purpose in venturing this objectionable statement was to remove repetition from the discourse by distinguishing the two times, future and past. He made the first pair refer to the future and the latter pair to the past; hence the question arose against him. The sounder explanation is that the discourse does not concern God's Messenger's past worship or what he worshipped in earlier times. It concerns the future, as the occasion of revelation attests. “What I worship” therefore remains in its apparent sense. As for the past form “what you worshipped,” it intensifies his repudiation of them and their worship, and is thus understood beyond the immediately apparent temporal sense.



T109.05 · Four explanations that avoid repetition (610)



The Imām [Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī] says: “There are two positions concerning the passage. The first is that it contains no repetition, and this has several explanations.
“First: the opening pair concerns the future, since lā (لا) enters only upon an imperfect verb with future meaning: in the future I will not do what you ask of me, worshipping your gods; nor will you do in the future what I ask of you, worshipping my God. Then He says, ‘Nor am I a worshipper of what you worshipped’: I am not at present worshipping your objects of worship, nor are you at present worshipping my object of worship.
“Second: reverse this, making the first pair concern the present and the second the future. This is the position of al-Zajjāj, al-Wāḥidī, and Muḥyī al-Sunna. Al-Wāḥidī says: ‘Mā (ما) is used instead of man to correspond to “what you worship,” making the second expression conform to the first.’ Al-Zajjāj and Muḥyī al-Sunna say: ‘This addresses those whom God already knew would not believe.’
“Third: Abū Muslim's position. The first two statements concern the object of worship, and mā (ما) means ‘that which’: I do not worship idols, and you do not worship God. In the latter two, mā (ما) forms a verbal noun: I do not worship with a worship like yours, founded on doubt; nor do you worship with a worship like mine, founded on certainty.
“Fourth: understand the first pair as denying worship on the particular consideration they proposed, and the second as a general denial in every respect. I do not worship what you worship in the hope that you will worship God, nor do you worship in the hope that I will worship your idol. He then says: I will not worship your idol for any purpose whatsoever, in any manner whatsoever; similarly, you do not worship God for any purpose. It is like someone inviting another to commit injustice to obtain pleasure, who replies: ‘I do not commit injustice for pleasure; indeed, I do not commit injustice at all, whether for pleasure or anything else.’”



T109.06 · Two explanations accepting repetition; Ṭībī's preference (611)



“The second position accepts that repetition occurs, for two reasons. First, repetition conveys emphasis, and the greater the need for emphasis, the more fitting the repetition. No situation needs emphasis more than this one: they returned to him repeatedly with this proposal and entertained hopes because they saw his eagerness for their faith.”
Muḥyī al-Sunna says: “Most scholars say that the Qurʾān was revealed in the Arabs' language and according to their ways of addressing one another. Their usages include repetition for emphasis and explanation, just as they include abbreviation for lightness and concision.”
I say: this is the explanation we have chosen because it fits the situation. Next in preference is the fourth explanation under the first position.
“Second: they stated the proposal twice: you worship our gods for a month and we will worship your God for a month; you worship our gods for a year and we will worship your God for a year. The response therefore repeats itself in conformity with their speech. There is a kind of mockery in this: someone who repeats the same statement for a corrupt purpose is answered with repeated rejection of that statement, in disdain.” Muḥyī al-Sunna transmits this explanation more briefly from al-Qutaybī.



al-Kāfirūn · “To you your religion” and the closing report

K IV:808–809 · T XVI:606–612

Q 109:6

لَكُمْ دِينُكُمْ وَلِىَ دِينِ ﴿6﴾

6. Unto you your religion, and unto me my religion

K109.04 · “To you your religion” and the closing report



“To you your religion, and to me my religion”: to you your association of partners with God, and to me my affirmation of His oneness. The meaning is: I am a prophet sent to you to call you to truth and salvation. If you will not accept what I bring or follow me, leave me alone without injury and do not invite me to associate partners with God.
God's Messenger is reported to have said: “Whoever recites Sūrat al-Kāfirūn, it is as though he recited a quarter of the Qurʾān. The rebellious devils withdraw far from him, he is cleared of associating partners with God, and he is spared the greatest terror.”



T109.07 · Kafāf; the disagreement over abrogation (611–612)

فدعوني كفافا

On “leave me alone without injury” (fa-daʿūnī kafāfan (فدعوني كفافًا)): al-Nihāya says: “Kafāf (كفاف) is what does not exceed a thing and is proportionate to the need for it. It is accusative as a circumstantial expression. It is also said that the meaning is: with their harm held back from me. Or: you do not injure me, and I do not injure you—you refrain from me, and I refrain from you.”
On this understanding, “To you your religion, and to me my religion” conveys mutual disengagement, each party allowing the other to remain upon its religion; it would then be abrogated by the verse commanding fighting.
The Qāḍī says: “‘To me my religion’ means the religion I follow and will not abandon. It neither grants permission for unbelief nor prohibits fighting, and therefore is not abrogated.” Dīn (دين) has also been explained as reckoning, recompense, supplication, and worship.



T109.08 · The quarter of the Qurʾān (612)

فكأنما قرأ ربع القرآن

On “it is as though he recited a quarter of the Qurʾān”: we have transmitted from al-Tirmidhī, from Ibn ʿAbbās and Anas, that God's Messenger said: “Whoever recites ‘Say: O unbelievers,’ it is equivalent for him to a quarter of the Qurʾān.”
The sūrah is complete.



al-Naṣr · Heading and verses

K IV:810–812 · T XVI:613–621

Q 110:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِذَا جَآءَ نَصْرُ ٱللَّهِ وَٱلْفَتْحُ ﴿1﴾ وَرَأَيْتَ ٱلنَّاسَ يَدْخُلُونَ فِى دِينِ ٱللَّهِ أَفْوَاجًا ﴿2﴾ فَسَبِّحْ بِحَمْدِ رَبِّكَ وَٱسْتَغْفِرْهُ ۚ إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ تَوَّابًۢا ﴿3﴾

1. When Allah's succour and the triumph cometh 2. And thou seest mankind entering the religion of Allah in troops 3. Then hymn the praises of thy Lord, and seek forgiveness of Him. Lo! He is ever ready to show mercy

K110.01 · Heading and verses [IV:810]



Revealed at Minā during the Farewell Pilgrimage, it is counted as Medinan. It is the last sūrah revealed, and has three verses. [The DKA heading also places it after al-Tawba.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

When God’s help and the conquest come, (1) and you see people entering God’s religion in multitudes, (2) glorify your Lord with praise and ask His forgiveness: He has ever turned mercifully toward the repentant. (3)



al-Naṣr · Help and conquest

K IV:810–812 · T XVI:613–621

Q 110:1

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِذَا جَآءَ نَصْرُ ٱللَّهِ وَٱلْفَتْحُ ﴿1﴾

1. When Allah's succour and the triumph cometh

K110.02 · Help and conquest [IV:810]



“When” (idhā (إذا)) is accusative through sabbiḥ (سبّح), “glorify,” and concerns the future. Announcing this before its occurrence is one of the signs of prophethood. It is reported that the sūrah was revealed during the days of tashrīq at Minā in the Farewell Pilgrimage.

If you ask how help differs from conquest, so that the latter is conjoined to the former, I reply: naṣr (نصر) is succour and giving mastery over an enemy. Hence naṣara (نصر) llāhu l-arḍa means God watered the earth with rain. Fatḥ (فتح) is the conquest of countries. The meaning is help for God’s Messenger against the Arabs, or against Quraysh, and the conquest of Mecca. It is also said to mean God’s help for believers generally and His opening the polytheists’ countries to them.

Mecca was conquered ten days into Ramaḍān in the eighth year. With God’s Messenger were ten thousand Emigrants, Helpers, and members of Arab tribes. He stayed fifteen nights, then went out against Hawāzin. On entering Mecca he stood at the Kaʿba’s door and said: “There is no god but God alone, without partner. He fulfilled His promise, helped His servant, and routed the confederates alone.” Then: “People of Mecca, what do you think I shall do with you?” They replied, “Good: you are a noble brother and son of a noble brother.” He said, “Go: you are released.” God’s Messenger thus freed them, although God had forcibly delivered their persons into his power and they were his booty. Therefore Mecca’s people were called “the released.” They then pledged allegiance to him in Islam.



T110.01 · A chronological objection [XVI:613–614]

أو على قريش وفتح مكة

Medinan; three verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

On “or against Quraysh, and the conquest of Mecca,” the Qāḍī [al-Bayḍāwī] says: “It is said to mean God’s help generally and the conquest of Mecca and other countries for them. What is obtained is figuratively described as coming, suggesting that decreed events proceed from eternity toward their appointed times, approaching gradually. That is: help has drawn near its time, so await its arrival and prepare to give thanks.”

I say: this and the author’s statement require examination, because Mecca’s conquest preceded the sūrah’s revelation. We transmit from Muslim, from ʿUbayd Allāh (الله) ibn ʿAbd Allāh (الله) ibn ʿUtba: Ibn ʿAbbās asked me, “Do you know the last complete sūrah revealed?” I said, “Yes: ‘When God’s help and the conquest come.’” He said, “Correct.” The author’s own words indicate this: he dates Mecca’s conquest to ten days into Ramaḍān of year eight, whereas it is said that the sūrah descended during the days of tashrīq at Minā in the Farewell Pilgrimage. That pilgrimage was in year ten: he remained nine years without performing pilgrimage, then was permitted it in the tenth.



al-Naṣr · Entering in multitudes

K IV:810–812 · T XVI:613–621

Q 110:2

وَرَأَيْتَ ٱلنَّاسَ يَدْخُلُونَ فِى دِينِ ٱللَّهِ أَفْوَاجًا ﴿2﴾

2. And thou seest mankind entering the religion of Allah in troops

K110.03 · Entering in multitudes [IV:810–811]



“Into God’s religion”: into the religion of Islam, for no other religion is attributed to Him: “Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will not be accepted from him” [3:85]. “In multitudes”: dense groups. An entire tribe would enter after people had previously entered singly or in pairs.

Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh (الله) once wept. Asked why, he said, “I heard God’s Messenger say: ‘People entered God’s religion in multitudes and will leave it in multitudes.’” It is also said that “people” means the Yemenis. Abū Hurayra says: when the sūrah was revealed, God’s Messenger said, “God is greatest! God’s help and the conquest have come, and the people of Yemen have come, a people tender of heart. Faith is Yemeni, understanding is Yemeni, and wisdom is Yemeni.” He also said, “I find your Lord’s relief coming from the direction of Yemen.” [The DKA transcription has nafīr (نفير); Ṭībī’s base and gloss have nafas (نَفَس).]

Al-Ḥasan says: when God’s Messenger conquered Mecca, the Arabs turned to one another and said, “Now that he has overcome the people of the sanctuary, we have no strength against him. God had protected them from the People of the Elephant and everyone who intended to attack them.” So they entered Islam in multitudes without fighting.

Ibn ʿAbbās read “God’s conquest and help.” Yudkhalūna (يُدْخَلُون), “are made to enter,” is also read in the passive. If you ask the grammatical position of “entering,” I reply: accusative, either as a circumstantial clause, with raʾayta meaning “saw” or “recognised,” or as its second object, with raʾayta meaning “knew.”



T110.02 · Jābir’s report and Yemeni faith [XVI:614–616]

وعن جابر بن عبد الله -رضي الله عنه-، أنه بكى ذات يوم

On Jābir’s weeping: Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal transmitted this report from him; al-Dārimī transmitted it from Abū Hurayra.

On “faith is Yemeni”: al-Bukhārī, Muslim and al-Tirmidhī transmit from Abū Hurayra that God’s Messenger said, “The people of Yemen have come to you: they are more tender in feeling and softer of heart. Faith is Yemeni, and wisdom is Yemeni.” Another transmission has “understanding is Yemeni.”

Al-Nihāya says: “He said this because faith began in Mecca, which belongs to Tihāma, and Tihāma is part of Yemen; hence the expression ‘the Yemeni Kaʿba.’ It is also said that he spoke at Tabūk, with Mecca and Medina then between him and Yemen: he pointed toward Yemen, intending Mecca and Medina. Or he meant the Helpers, who were Yemenis: they supported faith and the believers and sheltered them, so faith was ascribed to them,” as in “those who had established themselves in the abode and in faith” [59:9]. According to another authority, wisdom means the Sunna and religious understanding, as in “and teaches them the Book and wisdom” [62:2].

“Understanding is Yemeni” is also transmitted. This praises the Yemenis for hastening to faith and accepting it well. I say: perhaps “understanding” (fiqh) means what al-Ḥasan meant in the report we transmit from al-Dārimī, from ʿImrān. I once said to al-Ḥasan about something he said, “Abū Saʿīd, that is not what the jurists say.” He replied, “Woe to you! Have you ever seen a person of understanding? Such a person renounces attachment to this world, desires the hereafter, sees clearly in his religion, and perseveres in worshipping his Lord.”



T110.03 · Relief from Yemen and the Arabs’ words [XVI:616]

أجد نفس ربكم من قبل اليمن

On “I find your Lord’s nafas (نَفَس) from Yemen,” al-Nihāya says: “Nafas (نَفَس) is borrowed from the air drawn inward by breathing, which cools and moderates the inward heat; or from the breath of wind one inhales and finds refreshing; or from a garden’s breath, its pleasant scents, through which distress is relieved. One says, ‘You have breathing space in your affair,’ and ‘Act while you have breathing space in your life’: breadth and room.”

On ammā (أمّا) idh ẓafira (ظفر), “now that he has prevailed”: ammā (أمّا) is transmitted both lightened and doubled. The doubled form is preferable, for ammā (أمّا) distinguishes cases: before he prevailed over the sanctuary’s people we hoped to overcome him; now that he has prevailed we have no strength against him.



al-Naṣr · Glorification and asking forgiveness

K IV:810–812 · T XVI:613–621

Q 110:3

فَسَبِّحْ بِحَمْدِ رَبِّكَ وَٱسْتَغْفِرْهُ ۚ إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ تَوَّابًۢا ﴿3﴾

3. Then hymn the praises of thy Lord, and seek forgiveness of Him. Lo! He is ever ready to show mercy

K110.04 · Glorification and asking forgiveness [IV:811–812]



“Glorify your Lord with praise”: say “Glory be to God,” praising Him. That is: marvel that God has made possible what never occurred to you or anyone else—that someone should overcome the people of the sanctuary—and praise Him for His action. Or remember Him, glorifying and praising, increasing your worship and praise as His beneficence to you increases. Or perform prayer for Him. Umm Hāniʾ relates that when the Kaʿba’s door was opened, he prayed eight units of the forenoon prayer. ʿĀʾisha relates that before his death he frequently said, “Glory be to You, O God, and praise be Yours; I ask Your forgiveness and turn to You.”

The command to seek forgiveness alongside glorification completes the command concerning religion’s foundation: combining obedience with guarding against disobedience. His being commanded thus, despite his protection from sin, is a gracious aid to his community. Asking forgiveness also entails humility before God and abasement of the self, and is therefore worship in itself. From the Prophet: “I seek forgiveness a hundred times during a day and night.”



T110.04 · Wonder or worship? [XVI:617–618]

فقل: سبحان الله: حامدا له، أي: فتعجب

On “say ‘Glory be to God,’ praising Him; that is, marvel”: the bāʾ (باء (ب)) in “with your Lord’s praise” introduces a circumstance. Say the glorification while engaged in praise. On this interpretation the mention of glorification does not aim at remembrance as such. He says: “Originally God is glorified upon seeing something wondrous among His works; usage then broadened until it was applied to anything that occasions wonder.” Al-Intiṣāf says: “The command here has the meaning of a statement: an imperative in an expression of wonder is not intended as a command. The point is that this event is such as to arouse wonder.”

On “or remember Him, glorifying and praising”: on this interpretation glorification implies remembrance by semantic inclusion (taḍmīn), which is why he expresses glorification as a circumstance. “With your Lord’s praise” is a circumstance nested within that circumstance, since the incorporated meaning is most often expressed circumstantially. The Qāḍī says: “Extol God through the attributes of majesty while praising Him for the attributes of beneficence.”

I say: this interpretation is preferable and more coherent. In Sūrat al-Fatḥ it was explained that God made Mecca’s conquest a ground for forgiveness because it occasioned the command that His Messenger attend to his own particular concern after expending his utmost effort in conveying the message and struggling against religion’s enemies: turning to worship and piety and preparing to travel to the exalted stations and join the Highest Companion. His saying “God gave a servant a choice between this world and meeting Him, and he chose meeting God” points to this. Hence his uncle al-ʿAbbās wept when the sūrah was recited and said, “Your death has been announced to you.”

This is also what his cousin, the learned authority of the community, understood when he answered those elders, saying that the Prophet’s death had been announced; ʿUmar confirmed him.



T110.05 · Connecting passages by meaning [XVI:618–619]

ليغفر لك الله ما تقدم من ذنبك وما تأخر

Muḥyī l-Sunna transmits from Muḥammad ibn Jarīr that “so that God may forgive your earlier and later sin” [48:2] refers back to “When God’s help and the conquest come” through “and ask His forgiveness”: ask forgiveness so that God may forgive you. What is meant is that this explanation of purpose depends on something understood after “We have granted you a manifest conquest”: “[so glorify your Lord with praise and ask His forgiveness,] so that He may forgive you.” Both sūrahs refer to one event and one situation. It does not mean that “so that God may forgive you” is directly governed by that very wording “ask His forgiveness.” That would disrupt the miraculous composition, beyond human powers and capacities. How could it, when “We have granted you” was revealed before Mecca’s conquest, after the Messenger’s return from al-Ḥudaybiya, while Sūrat al-Naṣr was revealed two years after the conquest? In Sūrat Hūd we have already set out a rule for bringing together portions of one narrative occurring in different contexts and forms.

If you ask: the narrative’s unity indicates this implied expression, but what of Muḥyī l-Sunna’s further transmission from al-Ḥusayn ibn al-Faḍl, that “so that God may forgive you” refers back to “ask forgiveness for your sin and for believing men and women” [47:19]—ask forgiveness so that God may forgive you and “admit believing men and women to gardens beneath which rivers flow” [48:5]?

I reply: this strengthens our preference for a connection in meaning. If you make it a direct verbal connection, you find yourself in a trackless wilderness, stumbling blindly. Notice how he joins “so that He may admit the believers” to “so that God may forgive you.” The former explains “He sent tranquillity into the believers’ hearts” [48:4], itself explained by “so that they might increase in faith”; “and punish the hypocritical men and women” is conjoined to it. As the author says: “Among its consequences is that He calmed the believers’ hearts,” continuing through “so that they deserve reward, and He rewards them and punishes the unbelievers and hypocrites.”

This accords with what we transmit from Muslim and al-Tirmidhī, from Anas. When “We have granted you a manifest conquest” through “a mighty triumph” [48:1–5] was revealed on the return from al-Ḥudaybiya, they were affected by sorrow and dejection, and he had slaughtered the sacrificial animals there. God’s Messenger said, “A verse has been revealed to me that is dearer to me than the entire world.” Al-Tirmidhī’s version adds: they said, “May it bring you joy and benefit, Messenger of God! God has made clear what He will do with you; what will He do with us?” Then “so that He may admit believing men and women to gardens beneath which rivers flow” was revealed.

Perhaps the speaker considered that if God’s Messenger asks forgiveness for his sin and the believers’ sin, God must forgive him and answer his prayer for his community, as in: “Had they, after wronging themselves, come to you and asked God’s forgiveness, and the Messenger asked forgiveness for them, they would have found God ever relenting, merciful” [4:64]. He therefore connected the passages in meaning. For this subtle reason he preferred “refers back” and “is referred back” to “is grammatically dependent.” God knows best.



T110.06 · Completing obedience with caution [XVI:620]

صلاة الضحى ثماني ركعات

On “eight units of the forenoon prayer”: we transmit the report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī. On “he frequently said before his death”: al-Bukhārī and Muslim transmitted it.

On “the command to ask forgiveness alongside glorification completes”: in the rhetorical discipline, completion (takmīl (تكميل)) consists in bringing a statement that appears incomplete, then completing it with another. Here glorification commands obedience; acts of obedience are incomplete without guarding against sins. The Qāḍī says: “Ask His forgiveness, humbling yourself and regarding your deeds as insufficient, and remedying what escaped you through attending to another. It is also said: ask forgiveness for your community. Putting glorification, then praise, before seeking forgiveness follows the descent from the Creator to creation.”

On “I seek forgiveness a hundred times during a day [and night]”: al-Bukhārī and al-Tirmidhī transmitted it from Abū Hurayra.



al-Naṣr · The announcement of his departure

K IV:810–812 · T XVI:613–621

Q 110:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِذَا جَآءَ نَصْرُ ٱللَّهِ وَٱلْفَتْحُ ﴿1﴾ وَرَأَيْتَ ٱلنَّاسَ يَدْخُلُونَ فِى دِينِ ٱللَّهِ أَفْوَاجًا ﴿2﴾ فَسَبِّحْ بِحَمْدِ رَبِّكَ وَٱسْتَغْفِرْهُ ۚ إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ تَوَّابًۢا ﴿3﴾

1. When Allah's succour and the triumph cometh 2. And thou seest mankind entering the religion of Allah in troops 3. Then hymn the praises of thy Lord, and seek forgiveness of Him. Lo! He is ever ready to show mercy

K110.05 · The announcement of his departure [IV:812]



It is related that when God’s Messenger recited the sūrah to his Companions, they rejoiced, but al-ʿAbbās wept. “What makes you weep, uncle?” he asked. “Your death has been announced to you.” He replied, “It is as you say.” He then lived two years, during which he was not seen laughing in gladness. It is also said that Ibn ʿAbbās made this remark, and God’s Messenger said, “This boy has been given much knowledge.”

It is related that when the sūrah was revealed, God’s Messenger preached: “God gave a servant a choice between this world and meeting Him, and he chose meeting God.” Abū Bakr understood and said, “May we ransom you with ourselves, our property, our fathers and our children.”

From Ibn ʿAbbās: ʿUmar used to bring him close and admit him with the veterans of Badr. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān (الرحمن) asked, “Do you admit this youth with us, when among our sons are his equals?” He replied, “He is one whose standing you know.” Ibn ʿAbbās says: one day he admitted them, and me with them, and asked about “When God’s help comes.” I think he asked only because of me. Some said, “God commands His Prophet, when He grants him conquest, to ask forgiveness and repent.” I said, “Rather, his death has been announced to him.” ʿUmar said, “I understand it only as you do,” then, “How can you blame me concerning him after what you have seen?”

The Prophet called Fāṭima and said, “Daughter, my death has been announced to me.” She wept. He said, “Do not weep: you will be the first of my household to join me.” Ibn Masʿūd says this sūrah is called the Sūrah of Farewell.

“He has ever turned mercifully”: in past ages, ever since creating morally responsible beings, He has turned toward them when they sought forgiveness. Every seeker of forgiveness should therefore expect the same.



T110.07 · Ibn ʿAbbās and Fāṭima [XVI:621]

وعن ابن عباس: أن عمر رضي الله عنه يدينه

On Ibn ʿAbbās’s report that ʿUmar brought him close: Imām Aḥmad, al-Bukhārī and al-Tirmidhī transmitted it. “Brought him close” means gave him precedence, treated him on a level with the elders, and permitted him to enter his presence.

On his calling Fāṭima: this is an abridgment of al-Dārimī’s transmission from Ibn ʿAbbās.

End of the sūrah.



al-Naṣr · Closing report

K IV:810–812 · T XVI:613–621

Q 110:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ إِذَا جَآءَ نَصْرُ ٱللَّهِ وَٱلْفَتْحُ ﴿1﴾ وَرَأَيْتَ ٱلنَّاسَ يَدْخُلُونَ فِى دِينِ ٱللَّهِ أَفْوَاجًا ﴿2﴾ فَسَبِّحْ بِحَمْدِ رَبِّكَ وَٱسْتَغْفِرْهُ ۚ إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ تَوَّابًۢا ﴿3﴾

1. When Allah's succour and the triumph cometh 2. And thou seest mankind entering the religion of Allah in troops 3. Then hymn the praises of thy Lord, and seek forgiveness of Him. Lo! He is ever ready to show mercy

K110.06 · Closing report [IV:813]



From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites the sūrah ‘When God’s help comes’ receives the reward of someone who was present with Muḥammad on the day Mecca was conquered.”



al-Masad · Heading, verses, and destruction

K IV:813–816 · T XVI:622–631

Q 111:1–5

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ تَبَّتْ يَدَآ أَبِى لَهَبٍ وَتَبَّ ﴿1﴾ مَآ أَغْنَىٰ عَنْهُ مَالُهُۥ وَمَا كَسَبَ ﴿2﴾ سَيَصْلَىٰ نَارًا ذَاتَ لَهَبٍ ﴿3﴾ وَٱمْرَأَتُهُۥ حَمَّالَةَ ٱلْحَطَبِ ﴿4﴾ فِى جِيدِهَا حَبْلٌ مِّن مَّسَدٍۭ ﴿5﴾

1. The power of Abu Lahab will perish, and he will perish 2. His wealth and gains will not exempt him 3. He will be plunged in flaming Fire 4. And his wife, the wood-carrier 5. Will have upon her neck a halter of palm-fibre

K111.01 · Heading, verses, and destruction [IV:813]



Meccan; five verses. [The DKA heading places it after al-Fātiḥa.]
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

May Abū Lahab’s hands perish—and perish he did! (1) His wealth and what he earned have availed him nothing. (2) He shall burn in a flaming fire, (3) and his wife, the carrier of firewood, (4) with a rope of twisted fibre about her neck. (5)

Tabāb (تباب) means destruction. Hence their question, “Is she young, or tābba (تابّة)?”—worn out by decrepitude and old age. His hands perish because, according to a report, he picked up a stone to throw at God’s Messenger; “and he perished” means his whole person perished. Or his hands are declared ruined with the destruction of his entire person intended, as in “because of what your two hands sent ahead” [22:10]. “And he perished” then means this occurred and was accomplished, as in:
“He requited me—may God requite him with the worst requital—
with the requital of howling dogs; and He has done so.”



T111.01 · Loss and old age [XVI:622]

التباب: الهلاك

Meccan; five verses. In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.

On “tabāb (تباب) means destruction,” al-Rāghib says: “Tabb (تبّ) and tabāb (تباب) mean persistence in loss. One says tabban lahu and tabba lahu; tabbabtuhu means I said that to him. Because persistence is included, istatabba li-fulān means something became established for him. ‘Abū Lahab’s hands perished’ means they persisted in loss; ‘they added only destruction to them’ [11:101] means causing loss.” On “becoming aged”: ʿajizat (عَجِزَت) and ʿajjazat (عَجَّزَت) describe a woman becoming old, just as tathayyabat means becoming previously married.



T111.02 · Hands, the whole person, and certainty [XVI:623–624]

والمراد: هلاك جملته

On “his entire person is meant,” compare:
“A man whose hands begrudge another man
a benefit from someone else is surely miserly.”
That is, he begrudges him. Al-Jawharī: “‘This is what your hands have committed’ means what you committed.”

On “‘and he perished’: it happened”: according to one explanation tabba is an imprecation in the first interpretation and a statement in the second; tabbat is an imprecation in either. The Imām says the first can mean the destruction of his deeds, the second of himself: a man strives for his own welfare and that of his work, and God declares him deprived of both.

I say: the composition supports this. What follows explains it: “His wealth and earnings availed him nothing” points to his deeds’ destruction, and “He shall burn in a flaming fire” to his own. The past tense first signals certainty, as God’s announcements of future events do; the future tense second depicts the coming state before the listener’s eyes. Ibn Masʿūd’s “he has indeed perished” supports this, since qad confirms, as in the poet’s “and He has done so.” The poem means: he requited me like howling dogs—“running dogs” is also transmitted—may God give him His worst requital, and He has already done it.



al-Masad · The warning on al-Ṣafā and his byname

K IV:813–816 · T XVI:622–631

Q 111:1

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ تَبَّتْ يَدَآ أَبِى لَهَبٍ وَتَبَّ ﴿1﴾

1. The power of Abu Lahab will perish, and he will perish

K111.02 · The warning on al-Ṣafā and his byname [IV:814]



Ibn Masʿūd’s reading “and he has indeed perished” supports this. It is related that when “Warn your nearest kinsfolk” [26:214] was revealed, he climbed al-Ṣafā and cried, “Alarm!” People gathered from every direction. He said, “Sons of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, sons of Fihr: if I told you there was cavalry at this mountain’s foot, would you believe me?” “Yes.” “Then I warn you before the Hour.” Abū Lahab said, “Ruin to you! Is this why you summoned us?” The sūrah was revealed.

If you ask why he is given his kunya, when this is an honour, I reply: there are three explanations. First, he may have been known by his kunya rather than his name. A man may be known by one rather than the other; hence either may follow the other as explanatory apposition. Since the intention was to make him notorious by a curse that would remain his mark, the better-known designation was used. This is supported by the reading yadā (يدا) Abū Lahab, like “ʿAlī ibn Abū Ṭālib” and “Muʿāwiya ibn Abū Sufyān”: nothing is altered in the familiar name, lest the listener become confused. Fulayta ibn Qāsim, Mecca’s ruler, had two sons, one called ʿAbdi-llāh with the genitive, the other ʿAbda-llāh with the accusative. There was a man in Mecca known only as ʿAbdi-llāh, with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) on the dāl (دال (د)).

Second, his name was ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā (العزى), so the kunya was used instead. Third, he belonged to the people of the Fire and would end in a flaming fire, so his condition suited his kunya, “Father of Flame,” making it fitting. One calls someone Abū Lahab as one calls a wicked person “Father of Evil” and a good person “Father of Good,” or as the Messenger called al-Muhallab’s father Abū Ṣufra because of yellow colouring in his face. It is also said that his cheeks glowed and shone, which gave him the name; using it may therefore mock both him and his pride in it.

Abī Lahb is also read with quiescent hāʾ (هاء (ه)): an alteration peculiar to proper names, like Shums ibn Mālik (مَالِك) with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)).



T111.03 · The cry of warning [XVI:624]

وروي أنه لما نزل {وأنذر عشيرتك الأقربين

The report upon “Warn your nearest kinsfolk” [26:214] is transmitted by al-Bukhārī, Muslim, Imām Aḥmad and al-Tirmidhī from Ibn ʿAbbās: the Prophet climbed al-Ṣafā, calling the branches of Quraysh, “Sons of Fihr! Sons of ʿAdī!” They gathered; anyone unable to come sent someone to discover the matter. Abū Lahab and Quraysh came. He said, “If I told you cavalry in the valley intended to raid you, would you believe me?” “Yes: we have experienced only truthfulness from you.” “Then I warn you before a severe punishment.” Abū Lahab said, “Ruin to you all day! Is this why you gathered us?” The sūrah descended.

Al-Nihāya explains yā ṣabāḥāh (صباحاه): “A cry for aid, originally the alarm for a raid, since raids commonly occurred in the morning: morning has come, so prepare.” Safḥ (سفح) is the mountain’s lower slope, where water pours down.



T111.04 · Names, readings, and al-Muhallab [XVI:625–626]

لئلا يغير منه شئ فيشكل على السامع

On preserving the name unchanged, al-Intiṣāf says: “This indicates that the nominative precedes the other case forms. They preserve its familiar shape; the name became known thus. His name ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā (العزى) was avoided in favour of his kunya because it was disliked.”

Fulayta has fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) on fāʾ (فاء (ف)) and kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) on lām (لام (ل)); Fukayta with kāf (كاف (ك)) and the diminutive is also transmitted. The report that the Messenger named al-Muhallab’s father Abū Ṣufra is not mentioned in Jāmiʿ al-uṣūl (الأصول). Al-Muhallab is Abū Saʿīd al-Muhallab ibn Abī Ṣufra; his father’s name was Ẓālim ibn Surrāq ibn Ṣubayḥ al-Azdī. Al-Muhallab fought the celebrated wars against the Khārijites and died in 83 at Marw al-Rūdh under ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān. He belongs to the first generation of Basran Successors and saw ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb.

“It is also said he was so named” subdivides the third explanation; it is not a fourth. The kunya is preferred either because it uniquely identifies him and his name would confuse; or both identify him equally and the kunya was chosen though the name was possible; or for a subtle point: to signify that he belongs to Hell, either simply or with mockery. The author of al-Miftāḥ indicates the first explanation and the first version of the third.

Ibn Kathīr reads Lahb with quiescent hāʾ (هاء (ه)); the others with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)). Abū l-Baqāʾ calls them two dialectal forms. Mā (ما)’s accusative position is as an absolute object: “What benefit?” Abū l-Baqāʾ gives the two interpretations and says it cannot mean “that which.” The author is reported to have said: māl (مال) is general; Bedouins use it of camels, while their landed notables use it of an estate.



al-Masad · Wealth and earnings

K IV:813–816 · T XVI:622–631

Q 111:2

مَآ أَغْنَىٰ عَنْهُ مَالُهُۥ وَمَا كَسَبَ ﴿2﴾

2. His wealth and gains will not exempt him

K111.03 · Wealth and earnings [IV:814–815]



Mā (ما) aghnā (أغنى) is either an interrogative expressing denial, with mā (ما) accusative, or a negation. Wa-mā (وما) kasaba is nominative; mā (ما) is either relative, “what he earned,” or converts the clause into a verbal noun, “his earning.” His wealth and what he earned through it did not benefit him: capital and profits; or livestock and their offspring and benefits—he owned abundant breeding stock; or inherited wealth and what he acquired himself; or long-held and newly acquired property.

Ibn ʿAbbās explains “what he earned” as his children. Abū Lahab’s sons are said to have brought a dispute before him and begun fighting. He rose to separate them; one pushed him and he fell. Angry, he said, “Take this foul earning away from me!” Hence the Prophet’s saying, “The best of what a man eats is from his earning, and his child is from his earning.” Al-Ḍaḥḥāk explains it as his wealth and evil action, meaning his schemes of hostility against the Messenger. Qatāda takes it as the deeds he thought amounted to something, as in “We turned to whatever deeds they had done” [25:23]. It is related that he said, “If my nephew’s claims are true, I shall ransom myself from it with my wealth and children.”



T111.05 · Livestock, earnings, and the reading [XVI:627]

وكان ذا سايباء

On sābiyāʾ (سابياء) [the transcription also writes sāyibāʾ (سايباء)], al-Nihāya says: “The offspring and abundance of livestock; one says a household has sābiyāʾ (سابياء), plural sawābī (سوابي). Originally it is the membrane from which the newborn emerges, or the afterbirth.” Some say it is a diptote naming the offspring. Tālid (تالد) means long-held wealth, opposite to newly acquired ṭārif (طارف). Abū Dāwūd transmitted “the best of what a man eats” from ʿĀʾisha. The reading sa-yaṣlā (سَيَصْلَى) with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) is the well-known one; the reading with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) is noncanonical.



al-Masad · His wife and the firewood

K IV:813–816 · T XVI:622–631

Q 111:3–4

سَيَصْلَىٰ نَارًا ذَاتَ لَهَبٍ ﴿3﴾ وَٱمْرَأَتُهُۥ حَمَّالَةَ ٱلْحَطَبِ ﴿4﴾

3. He will be plunged in flaming Fire 4. And his wife, the wood-carrier

K111.04 · His wife and the firewood [IV:815]



Sa-yaṣlā (سَيَصْلَى) is read with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) on yāʾ (ياء (ي)) and also with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)), in both lightened and doubled forms. Sīn (سين (س)) expresses threat: it will inevitably occur, however delayed. His wife is Umm Jamīl daughter of Ḥarb, Abū Sufyān’s sister. She carried bundles of thorns, prickles and saʿdān (سعدان) and scattered them at night on the Messenger’s path. Or she went about carrying tales. A malicious gossip who corrupts relations is said to “carry firewood among them”: he kindles their hostility and causes evil. The poet says:
“A fair woman never found upon a blameworthy course,
nor walking among the tribe with damp firewood.”
The wood is called damp to suggest smoke, an additional evil.

“His wife” is nominative, conjoined to the pronoun in sa-yaṣlā (سَيَصْلَى): he and his wife will burn; “about her neck” then expresses a circumstance. Or “his wife” is the subject and “about her neck” its predicate. “Carrier of firewood” is also read accusative as abuse. I prefer this reading: whoever loves reviling Umm Jamīl has approached the Messenger through something fine (jamīl). Ḥammālatun (حمّالةٌ) li-l-ḥaṭab (للحطب) and ḥammālatan (حمّالةً) li-l-ḥaṭab (للحطب) are also read, with tanwīn (تنوين (ـً ـٍ ـٌ)) and nominative or accusative. Wa-murayʾatuhu (ومُرَيْئته), “and his little wife,” is read as a diminutive.



T111.06 · The woman and damp wood [XVI:628]

من البيض لم تصطد

“Never hunted” means never found: she is likened to an oryx and its description applied to her. Lāʾima (لائمة) means something blameworthy: she was never found pursuing a blameworthy quality. The poet praises her honour. Al-khaṭar al-raṭb is also transmitted: damp wood used to make an enclosure. The meaning is that she did not go among people carrying tales and instilling hostility.

On smoke as an additional evil: he did not merely make it wood, but damp wood, an intensifying completion (īghāl (إيغال) and tatmīm (تتميم)) for hyperbole. Imruʾ al-Qays says:
“I bore a Rudaynī spear whose point was like
the brilliance of a flame unmixed with smoke.”
ʿĀṣim reads ḥammālata l-ḥaṭab (حمّالةَ الحطب) accusative; the others nominative.



al-Masad · The rope, the taunt, and punishment

K IV:813–816 · T XVI:622–631

Q 111:5

فِى جِيدِهَا حَبْلٌ مِّن مَّسَدٍۭ ﴿5﴾

5. Will have upon her neck a halter of palm-fibre

K111.05 · The rope, the taunt, and punishment [IV:815–817]



Masad (مسد) is tightly twisted cord, whether palm fibre, leather, or another material. The poet says, “And a rope tightly twisted from she-camels.” A man mamsūd (ممسود) in build is firmly knit. Thus she has a twisted rope about her neck, carrying and tying up the thorns like wood-gatherers. This debases and belittles her, depicting her as one of the serving-women who gather wood, to anger both her and her husband despite their house of honour and their wealth and prosperity.

Someone taunted al-Faḍl ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿUtba ibn Abī Lahab with “the carrier of firewood.” He replied:
“Why have you turned to insulting and diminishing me?
What reproach is there in the carrier of firewood?
A radiant woman, whose broad blaze shone in glory,
offspring of an elder of profound ancestral distinction.”

Or the meaning is that in Hell she will appear as she did when carrying the thorn-bundle: always bearing a bundle of hellfire wood from zaqqūm (زقّوم) or ḍarīʿ (ضريع), with a rope twisted from the Fire’s chains about her neck. Every criminal is punished in a manner corresponding to his crime.

From God’s Messenger: “Whoever recites Sūrat Tabbat, I hope God will not place him and Abū Lahab together in one abode.”



T111.07 · The twisted camel-hide rope [XVI:629]

ومسد أمر من أيانق

The verse “And a rope tightly twisted from she-camels” continues, according to al-Zajjāj:
“Reddish-white, choice, with marrow approaching maturity.”
Al-Muṭliʿ has “neither old she-camels nor young ones of the ḥiqqa (حِقّة) age.” Umirra means twisted. Ayāniq (أيانق) is the plural of aynuq, itself a plural of nāqa (ناقة): the cord is made from their hides. Ṣuhb qualifies ayāniq (أيانق). An aṣhab (أصهب) camel has white mingled with red. Rāhiq (راهق) is borrowed from a boy approaching maturity. Anyāb (أنياب) is the plural of nāb (ناب) [an old she-camel]. The rope is made neither from a very young nor an old animal’s hide, but from a strong young adult’s.

On “firmly knit,” al-Jawharī gives “a girl of well-knit build.” Mawāhin (مواهن) is plural of māhina (ماهنة): mahna, with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) on mīm (ميم (م)), is service; māhin (ماهن) is a servant.



T111.08 · Anger, the poem, and final syntax [XVI:630–631]

لتمتعض

Maʿiḍtu (مَعِضْتُ) and imtaʿaḍtu (امتعضت) mean I became angry and found something distressing. In “what did you intend toward insulting me,” aradta (أردت) includes the meaning of turning toward, and therefore takes ilā. A shādikha (شادخة) is a horse’s broad white blaze extending from forelock to nose without touching the eyes, a feature of noble horses. “The elder” is taken as ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib—but this is not correct, since she is Ḥarb’s daughter and Abū Sufyān’s sister, as stated.

On the interpretation that her appearance in Hell matches her former appearance: “and his wife, carrier of firewood” is then a circumstantial clause referring to the pronoun in sa-yaṣlā (سَيَصْلَى), or “his wife” is conjoined to that pronoun. On the earlier interpretation it cannot be a circumstantial clause, but one sentence is conjoined to another. Abū l-Baqāʾ says: “Imraʾatuhu (امرأته) has two analyses: a subject with ḥammāla (حمّالة) as predicate; or conjoined to the pronoun in sa-yaṣlā (سَيَصْلَى). On the latter, ḥammāla (حمّالة) is either an adjective for the preceding noun or stands for ‘while she is a carrier.’”

End of the sūrah.



al-Ikhlāṣ · Sūrat al-Ikhlāṣ

K IV:817–819 · T XVI:632–643

Q 112:1–4

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ ﴿1﴾ ٱللَّهُ ٱلصَّمَدُ ﴿2﴾ لَمْ يَلِدْ وَلَمْ يُولَدْ ﴿3﴾ وَلَمْ يَكُن لَّهُۥ كُفُوًا أَحَدٌۢ ﴿4﴾

1. Say: He is Allah, the One 2. Allah, the eternally Besought of all 3. He begetteth not nor was begotten 4. And there is none comparable unto Him

K112.01 · Text and explanation



Sūrat al-Ikhlāṣ. Meccan; it is also said to be Medinan. It has four verses.
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.
“Say: He is God, One. God, the One to whom all turn in need (al-Ṣamad (الصمد)). He neither begot nor was begotten, and no one is equal to Him.” [112:1–4]
[REVIEW K112-A: The separate four-volume transcription additionally prints “revealed after al-Nās (الناس)” in its heading and repeats the basmala (بسملة). These are absent from the corresponding Dubai base-text heading. Their authorial status is unestablished; they are recorded rather than silently inserted.]



al-Ikhlāṣ · Huwa [He/it] is the pronoun of the matter (ḍamīr al-shaʾn), and “God is One” is that matter, as when

K IV:817–819 · T XVI:632–643

Q 112:1

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ ﴿1﴾

1. Say: He is Allah, the One

K112.02 · Text and explanation



Huwa (هو) [He/it] is the pronoun of the matter (ḍamīr al-shaʾn (ضمير الشأن)), and “God is One” is that matter, as when you say, “It is: Zayd is departing.” It is as though one had said: the matter is this—namely, that God is one, with no second beside Him.
If you ask, “What is the syntactic position of huwa (هو)?”, I answer: nominative, as the subject (mubtadaʾ (مبتدأ)); its predicate is the sentence.
If you ask, “A sentence functioning as a predicate must contain something referring back to the subject. Where, then, is that reference?”, I answer: this sentence has the same status as the single predicate in your statement “Zayd is your servant-boy”: in meaning, it is the subject itself. For “God is One” is precisely the matter expressed by huwa (هو). This is not the case with “Zayd—his father is departing,” since Zayd and the sentence denote two different meanings; something connecting them is therefore indispensable.
Ibn ʿAbbās reports: Quraysh said, “Muḥammad, describe to us your Lord, to whom you summon us,” and the sūrah was revealed. That is: the one whose description you have asked me for is God. Aḥad (أحد) is a substitution (badal) for “God”; alternatively, [it is construed with an understood subject] “He is One.” It has the meaning of wāḥid (واحد), and its original form is waḥad (وَحَد).



T112.01 · “The one whose description you asked me for is God, and aḥad is a substitution”

الذي سألتموني وصفه هو الله، و {أحد

Sūrat al-Ikhlāṣ. Meccan; it is also said to be Medinan. Four verses.
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.
His words, “The one whose description you asked me for is God, and aḥad (أحد) is a substitution.” Abū al-Baqāʾ says: “Huwa (هو) is a subject meaning ‘the one asked about,’ for they said, ‘Is your Lord made of copper or gold?’ On this interpretation, Allāh (الله) may be the predicate of the subject, with aḥad (أحد) a substitution, or the predicate of an omitted subject. Allāh (الله) may also be a substitution, with aḥad (أحد) the predicate. The hamza (همزة (ء)) of aḥad (أحد) replaces wāw (واو (و)), since it means al-wāḥid (الواحد).” Substitution of hamza (همزة (ء)) for a wāw (واو (و)) carrying fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) is rare. It is also said that the hamza (همزة (ء)) is original, like the hamza (همزة (ء)) in aḥad (أحد) used for generality.



T112.02 · “It has the meaning of wāḥid”

وهو بمعنى واحد

His words, “It has the meaning of wāḥid (واحد).” There are two possibilities. First, this may attach to the second interpretation: huwa (هو) answers their request, “Describe your Lord to us,” and is the pronoun referring to the one asked about. In that case, a distinction must be drawn between wāḥid (واحد) and aḥad (أحد). He says in al-Aḥzāb: “Aḥad (أحد) originally means waḥad (وَحَد), that is, al-wāḥid (الواحد); then it was assigned to general negation, applying equally to masculine and feminine, one and more than one.”
The author of al-Nihāya transmits from al-Azharī: “The difference between al-wāḥid (الواحد) and al-aḥad (الأحد) is that aḥad (أحد) was formed to negate whatever number is mentioned with it: you say, ‘No one came to me.’ Wāḥid (واحد) is a name formed for the beginning of number: you say, ‘One person came to me,’ but do not say [in this affirmative construction], ‘Aḥad (أحد) came to me.’ Al-wāḥid (الواحد) is unique in essence, without likeness or counterpart; al-aḥad (الأحد) is unique in meaning. It is also said: al-wāḥid (الواحد) is that which is not divisible, is not doubled, does not admit division, and has neither counterpart nor likeness. Only God Most High combines these two descriptions.”
Al-Azharī says in Tafsīr asmāʾ (أسماء) Allāh (الله) al-ḥusnā: “Al-aḥad (الأحد) is among the attributes God has reserved to Himself: nothing shares it with Him, and nothing other than God is described as al-aḥad (الأحد). One does not say ‘an aḥad (أحد) man’ or ‘an aḥad (أحد) dirham’; one says ‘a wāḥid (واحد) man.’”
Knowing this, we say: when they said, “Describe to us your Lord, to whom you summon us,” they were told: the one asked about is God, one, unique in essence, without likeness or counterpart. The discourse thus serves to distinguish, and the description differentiates. Although it entails magnification, [when] huwa (هو) is taken as the pronoun of the matter, the descriptions serve magnification alone. For this is the beginning of the Messenger's ﷺ mission, guiding the people and drawing attention to an object of worship of immense dignity and overwhelming dominion. It is as though one had said: Say, Muḥammad, the matter and affair is that God is One, with no second beside Him. Allāh (الله) indicates all the attributes of perfection; aḥad (أحد), all the attributes of majesty. Accordingly, it is appropriate to say “One, with no second beside Him,” because aḥad (أحد) indicates the negation of whatever is mentioned with it.
The second possibility is that [his explanation] attaches to both interpretations together: either huwa (هو) is the pronoun of the matter, or huwa (هو) means the one asked about. There would then be no distinction between aḥad (أحد) and wāḥid (واحد). Al-Jawharī says, “Al-aḥad (الأحد) means al-wāḥid (الواحد), and is the beginning of number.” The author of al-Nihāya says, “Al-wāḥid (الواحد) is the solitary one who has always been alone, with no other beside Him.”
[TEXT CORRECTIONS T112-C1: The scan at p.633 reads لا يتجزأ and إلا الله, correcting the transcription. At p.634 the words على أن يكون «هو» ضمير الشأن، فإجراء الأوصاف لمجرد التعظيم are present in the scan but absent in OpenITI. They are translated above. The transition following وإن استلزم التعظيم remains syntactically awkward; bracketed “when” makes the understood construction explicit.]



al-Ikhlāṣ · ʿAbd Allāh and Ubayy read “Huwa Allāhu aḥad,” without “Say

K IV:817–819 · T XVI:632–643

Q 112:1–2

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ ﴿1﴾ ٱللَّهُ ٱلصَّمَدُ ﴿2﴾

1. Say: He is Allah, the One 2. Allah, the eternally Besought of all

K112.03 · Text and explanation



ʿAbd Allāh (الله) and Ubayy read “Huwa (هو) Allāhu (اللَّهُ) aḥad (أحد),” without “Say.” In the Prophet's ﷺ reading it is “Allāhu (اللَّهُ) aḥad (أحد),” without “Say: He”; and he said, “Whoever recites ‘Allāhu (اللَّهُ) aḥad (أحد)’—it is equivalent to the Qurʾān.” Al-Aʿmash read “Qul huwa (هو) Allāhu (اللَّهُ) al-wāḥid (الواحد).” It has also been read “aḥadu (أحدُ) llāhu,” without tanwīn (تنوين (ـً ـٍ ـٌ)): the tanwīn (تنوين (ـً ـٍ ـٌ)) is dropped because it meets the lām (لام (ل)) of the definite article. Comparable to this is:
“Nor remembering God, except a little.”
The sounder usage is to retain the tanwīn (تنوين (ـً ـٍ ـٌ)), giving it a kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) because of the meeting of two unvocalized consonants.
Al-Ṣamad (الصمد) is on the pattern faʿal (فَعَل) with the meaning of a passive participle (mafʿūl (مفعول)), from ṣamada (صمد) ilayhi, “he directed himself towards him.” It is the master to whom people turn with their needs.
[REVIEW K112-B: The report about equivalence is translated as given; Ṭībī's correction to “one third” follows in T112.12. Its presence here is not a fresh authentication.]



T112.03 · “It was equivalent to the Qurʾān”

كان يعدل القرآن

His words, “It was equivalent to the Qurʾān.” It has been said: its recitation was equivalent to reciting the Qurʾān. The report is cited as evidence for this reading. Perhaps the meaning is that “Say: He” functions as an introduction and preparation for “God is One.” This works only if the pronoun is taken as the pronoun of the matter.



T112.04 · “Nor remembering God, except a little”

ولا ذاكر الله إلا قليلا

His words, “Nor remembering God, except a little.” Its opening is:
“I found him not returning to goodwill under reproach.”
That is: I reminded him. “Nor remembering” is intended with tanwīn (تنوين (ـً ـٍ ـٌ)); it was dropped because two unvocalized consonants met. Allāh (الله) consequently remains accusative, not genitive through annexation (iḍāfa (إضافة)). Dhākir (ذاكر) is genitive, coordinated with mustaʿtib (مستعتب): “nor remembering.” That is: I reminded him of the affection formerly between us, but he was found not to turn back, through reproach, from the ugliness of what he had done.
His words, “The sounder usage is tanwīn (تنوين (ـً ـٍ ـٌ))”: this is the well-known reading.
His words, “The master to whom people turn with their needs.” Al-Zajjāj cited al-Asadī:
“Early came the death-announcer with news of the two best of Banū Asad:
ʿAmr ibn Masʿūd and the master to whom all turn.”
Al-Ṣamad (الصمد): that is, the one to whom everything turns—the one who created all things, and whom nothing can do without. Muḥyī al-Sunna transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, al-Ḥasan and Saʿīd ibn Jubayr: “Al-Ṣamad (الصمد) is the one with no interior cavity.” Al-Shaʿbī said: “The one who neither eats nor drinks.”



al-Ikhlāṣ · The meaning is: He is the God whom you know, and whom you acknowledge as the creator of the heavens 

K IV:817–819 · T XVI:632–643

Q 112:1–4

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ ﴿1﴾ ٱللَّهُ ٱلصَّمَدُ ﴿2﴾ لَمْ يَلِدْ وَلَمْ يُولَدْ ﴿3﴾ وَلَمْ يَكُن لَّهُۥ كُفُوًا أَحَدٌۢ ﴿4﴾

1. Say: He is Allah, the One 2. Allah, the eternally Besought of all 3. He begetteth not nor was begotten 4. And there is none comparable unto Him

K112.04 · Text and explanation



The meaning is: He is the God whom you know, and whom you acknowledge as the creator of the heavens and the earth and your own creator. He is one, alone in divinity, with no partner in it. He is the one to whom every creature turns; they cannot dispense with Him, whereas He has no need of them.
“He did not beget,” because He shares no genus with anything, such that He might have a consort of His own genus and the two might produce offspring. His statement “How could He have a child, when He has no consort?” [6:101] indicates this meaning. “Nor was He begotten,” because everything begotten is originated and a body; He, however, is pre-eternal (qadīm (قديم)), with no beginning to His existence, and is not a body. No one equals Him: that is, no one resembles Him or is like Him. It is also possible to take this from suitability between spouses (kafāʾa (كفاءة)) in marriage, thereby denying Him a consort.



T112.05 · “His statement ‘How could He have a child, when He has no consort?’ indicates this meaning”

وأمه صديقة كانا ياكلان الطعام

Al-Rāghib: “That which has no interior cavity is of two kinds: what is below the human being, such as inanimate things; and what is above, namely the Creator, exalted and holy. The purpose of al-Ṣamad (الصمد) is to indicate that He differs from those to whom they ascribed divinity. Something of this kind is indicated by ‘His mother was a woman of truth; they both used to eat food’ [5:75].”
His words, “His statement ‘How could He have a child, when He has no consort?’ indicates this meaning”: these are coordinated with “because He shares no genus.” That is, “He did not beget” either indirectly signifies (kināya) His transcendence above belonging to a genus—for whoever shares a genus with anything takes a consort of his genus, and whoever takes a consort produces offspring—or [the reasoning proceeds] in reverse: how could He have a child when He has taken no consort? Generation occurs only between two partners of one genus. He transcends having a congener, so a consort cannot properly be ascribed to Him, and neither can generation. He states this in explaining the verse in al-Anʿām.



al-Ikhlāṣ · They asked him to describe Him to them, and what encompasses His attributes was revealed to him

K IV:817–819 · T XVI:632–643

Q 112:1–4

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ ﴿1﴾ ٱللَّهُ ٱلصَّمَدُ ﴿2﴾ لَمْ يَلِدْ وَلَمْ يُولَدْ ﴿3﴾ وَلَمْ يَكُن لَّهُۥ كُفُوًا أَحَدٌۢ ﴿4﴾

1. Say: He is Allah, the One 2. Allah, the eternally Besought of all 3. He begetteth not nor was begotten 4. And there is none comparable unto Him

K112.05 · Text and explanation



They asked him to describe Him to them, and what encompasses His attributes was revealed to him. “He is God” points them to the creator and originator of things. Implicit in that is His description as powerful and knowing, since creation requires power and knowledge, occurring as it does with the utmost precision, coherence and order. This also describes Him as living, hearing and seeing. “One” describes His oneness and denies partners. “Al-Ṣamad (الصمد)” describes Him as exclusively the one who is needed; if He is exclusively needed [and not needy], He is self-sufficient.
