His being self-sufficient together with His being knowing entails that He is just and does not perform evil acts: He knows the evil of the evil act, and knows that He has no need of it. “Nor was He begotten” describes pre-eternity and primordiality. “He did not beget” denies likeness and a shared genus. “No one is equal to Him” confirms this and settles the judgment definitively.



T112.06 · “Thus His statement ‘He is God.’”

فقوله: {هو الله}

His words, “Thus His statement ‘He is God.’” The fāʾ (فاء (ف)) introduces a detailed exposition; the general statement being expounded is “what encompasses His attributes.” Since Allāh (الله) names the essence—and in the Opening of the Book he established the impossibility of its being a descriptive epithet—it nevertheless has in each context a meaning appropriate to that context. The particular character of the polytheists' question required its explanation as “the Creator,” because of His statement, “If you ask them who created the heavens and the earth, they will certainly say: God” [31:25]. Thus Allāh (الله) here, as an answer, points them to the one who creates things. You know that knowledge and power are what make creatorship possible: these two attributes are therefore included under it. This is what “implicit in that is His description as powerful and knowing” indicates. He cannot be powerful and knowing without being knowing, living, hearing and seeing. Then these descriptions were followed by the meaning of oneness in “One.” Since uniqueness excludes recourse to another, He was affirmed to possess ṣamadiyya (صمدية), so that recourse would be to Him.
Once this established an essence entailing the attributes of creatorship, knowledge, power, life and divinity, their perfection was to be explained, together with their difference from creatures' attributes in past and future. At this point it was said: “He neither begot nor was begotten, and no one is equal to Him.”



T112.07 · Commentary



Ḥujjat al-Islām gives a concise discussion of them:
“‘Aḥad (أحد)’: He is the wāḥid (واحد) from whom partnership is excluded, and the aḥad (أحد) in whom there is no composition. Wāḥid (واحد) negates partner and likeness; aḥad (أحد) negates multiplicity within His essence. Al-Ṣamad (الصمد) is the self-sufficient one whom others need. He is aḥad (أحد) in essence and wāḥid (واحد) in attributes: for if He had a partner in His dominion, He would not be self-sufficient and needed by others; rather, He would depend for His subsistence and existence on the components of His composition. Thus ṣamadiyya (صمدية) proves both wāḥidiyya (واحدية) and aḥadiyya (أحدية).
“‘He did not beget’ proves that His continuing existence is not like human existence, whose species persists through generation and reproduction; rather, His existence continues eternally, without beginning or end. ‘Nor was He begotten’ proves that His existence is not like the existence of the human soul, which comes into being after nonexistence and then remains forever, either in a lofty Garden that does not perish or in an abyss that does not end.
“‘No one is equal to Him’ proves that His true existence is an existence that bestows existence on another without receiving existence from another. Thus ‘He is God, One’ proves His holy, transcendent essence. Ṣamadiyya (صمدية) entails His freedom from need and others' need of Him. ‘Nor was He begotten,’ at the end of the sūrah, removes from Him what characterizes others. There is no clearer path to knowledge of God Most High than removing from Him the attributes of creatures.”
[REVIEW T112-U1: The scan prints “nor was He begotten” in the last sentence, although it is not the sūrah's final clause. It is preserved. The wording about partnership, composition and attributes also needs comparison with the work being quoted.]



al-Ikhlāṣ · If you ask, “In eloquent Arabic, an adjunct (ẓarf) that is supplementary (laghw), rather than indisp

K IV:817–819 · T XVI:632–643

Q 112:4

وَلَمْ يَكُن لَّهُۥ كُفُوًا أَحَدٌۢ ﴿4﴾

4. And there is none comparable unto Him

K112.06 · Text and explanation



If you ask, “In eloquent Arabic, an adjunct (ẓarf (ظرف)) that is supplementary (laghw (لغو)), rather than indispensable to the predication (mustaqirr (مستقرّ)), is postponed, not placed first—Sībawayh explicitly says so in his book. Why, then, is it placed first in the most eloquent and most fully Arabic of speech?”, I answer: this utterance is directed precisely at denying equivalence to the essence of the Creator—glory be to Him. This adjunct [lahu, ‘to Him’] is where that meaning converges and is centred. It is therefore the most important and most pertinent element, the one most entitled and most suited to precedence.
Kufuʾan (كُفُؤًا) has been read with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) on both kāf (كاف (ك)) and fāʾ (فاء (ف)), and also with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) or kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) on the kāf (كاف (ك)) together with an unvocalized fāʾ (فاء (ف)) [kufʾan (كُفْئًا), kifʾan (كِفْئًا)].



T112.08 · “Exclusively the one who is needed”

ليس إلا محتاجا إليه

His words, “Exclusively the one who is needed”: the exception is unfilled (istithnāʾ mufarragh (استثناء مفرّغ)); that is, God is exclusively the one who is needed—relative, that is, to creatures.
His words, “Supplementary, not indispensable to the predication.” An indispensable adjunct (ẓarf (ظرف) mustaqirr (مستقرّ)) is one required to complete the utterance, as when it functions as the predicate in “There was not in it anyone better than you.” A supplementary adjunct (laghw (لغو)) is one without which the utterance is complete, as in “No one was better than you in it.” In the first case, the indispensable adjunct is put first because it is intended as the focus. The normal arrangement was abandoned in the verse because it was directed at explaining divine oneness.
Ibn al-Ḥājib says: “It was placed first out of concern for the correspondence of verse endings. Had it merely preceded aḥad (أحد), the aim would have been achieved; but it would then have separated the two parts which are [the predicate and] the subject of predication. It was therefore placed before both, and the aim was achieved.”
[REVIEW T112-U2: The printed wording is اللذين هما مسند إليه, apparently incomplete as a description of the two parts. The bracketed supplement conveys the apparent argument but requires checking the quoted source.]



T112.09 · Commentary



The author of al-Intiṣāf says: “Sībawayh reports hearing a rough Arab recite ‘wa-lam yakun aḥadun (أحدٌ) kufuwan (كُفُوًا) lahu.’ The uncouth man followed his habitual manner, his coarse disposition missing the subtle meaning that required the adjunct and predicate to precede the subject. The verse's intended aim is to deny equivalence and equality to God's essence. It was therefore preferable to put first the equivalence intended to be denied Him. Once it was brought forward for negation, the adjunct was mentioned with it, to distinguish the holy essence by the denial of equivalence.”
I say: its upshot is that attention to the meaning demanded by the context is more fitting, more justified and prior to attention to wording and verse endings.



T112.10 · “Kufuʾan has been read, with ḍamma on kāf”

وقرئ: {كفوا

His words, “Kufuʾan (كُفُؤًا) has been read, with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) on kāf (كاف (ك)).” Ḥafṣ reads with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) on it and on fāʾ (فاء (ف)), without hamza (همزة (ء)) [kufuwan (كُفُوًا)]. Ḥamza reads with unvocalized fāʾ (فاء (ف)) and hamza (همزة (ء)) when continuing; when stopping, he replaces it with a wāw (واو (و)) bearing fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)). The others read with ḍamma (ضَمَّة (ـُ)) on fāʾ (فاء (ف)) and with hamza (همزة (ء)).
Al-Rāghib: “Kufʾ (كفء) is [equality] in standing and worth. From it comes kifāʾ (كفاء) for a piece of fabric sewn to another, with which the rear of a tent is covered. One says that a person is another's equal in marriage, combat and the like. From it comes mukāfaʾa (مكافأة): equality and corresponding action. Ikfāʾ (إكفاء) is overturning something, as though removing equality; from this comes ikfāʾ (إكفاء) in poetry.”



al-Ikhlāṣ · If you ask, “Why is this sūrah the equivalent of the whole Qurʾān, despite its shortness and the clo

K IV:817–819 · T XVI:632–643

Q 112:1–4

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ هُوَ ٱللَّهُ أَحَدٌ ﴿1﴾ ٱللَّهُ ٱلصَّمَدُ ﴿2﴾ لَمْ يَلِدْ وَلَمْ يُولَدْ ﴿3﴾ وَلَمْ يَكُن لَّهُۥ كُفُوًا أَحَدٌۢ ﴿4﴾

1. Say: He is Allah, the One 2. Allah, the eternally Besought of all 3. He begetteth not nor was begotten 4. And there is none comparable unto Him

K112.07 · Text and explanation



If you ask, “Why is this sūrah the equivalent of the whole Qurʾān, despite its shortness and the closeness of its two ends?”, I answer:
“It is for some great cause that whoever becomes a master becomes one.”
That is solely because it encompasses God's attributes, His justice and His oneness. For anyone who acknowledges its excellence and believes the Messenger of God's ﷺ statement about it, this is sufficient proof that the science of divine oneness (ʿilm al-tawḥīd) has an exalted standing with God. How could it be otherwise? Knowledge follows its object: it is ennobled by the nobility of what is known, and lowered by its lowliness. The object of this science is God Most High and His attributes, and what is and is not admissible concerning Him. What, then, do you think of the nobility of its rank, the grandeur of its position, its eminence over every other science, and its winning the prize ahead of them? Whoever disparages it does so because of the weakness of his knowledge of its object, the smallness of his reverence for Him, his lack of fear of Him, and his remoteness from considering his own ultimate fate.
O God, gather us among those who know You, act for You, affirm Your justice and Your oneness, and fear Your threat.
It is called “The Sūrah of the Foundation,” because it encompasses the foundations of religion. Ubayy and Anas transmitted from the Prophet ﷺ: “The seven heavens and the seven earths were founded upon ‘Say: He is God, One.’” That is: they were created solely to serve as signs of God's oneness and of the knowledge of His attributes expressed by this sūrah.
It is related from the Messenger of God ﷺ that he heard a man reciting “Say: He is God, One,” and said, “It has become assured.” He was asked, “Messenger of God, what has become assured?” He answered, “Paradise has become assured for him.”



T112.11 · “The equivalent of the whole Qurʾān”

عدل القرآن كله

His words, “The equivalent of the whole Qurʾān.” It is transmitted with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)) or kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) on the ʿayn (عين (ع)). Al-Akhfash says: “ʿIdl (عِدْل), with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)), means an equivalent. ʿAdl (عَدْل), with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)), is originally the verbal noun in ‘I made this nicely equal [to that]’; you turn it into a noun for an equivalent to distinguish it from a ʿidl (عِدْل), a load of goods.” Al-Farrāʾ says: “ʿAdl (عَدْل), with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)), is what equals something while belonging to a different kind. ʿIdl (عِدْل), with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)), is its like. You say, ‘I have the ʿidl (عِدْل) of your servant-boy, or of your sheep,’ when it is a servant-boy equal to a servant-boy or a sheep equal to a sheep. If you mean its value in something of another kind, you pronounce the ʿayn (عين (ع)) with fatḥa (فَتْحَة (ـَ)). Some Arabs sometimes pronounce it with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)); that was an error on their part.”



T112.12 · “It is for some cause that whoever becomes a master becomes one”

لأمر ما يسود من يسود

The correct version is “one third of the Qurʾān.” We have transmitted from al-Bukhārī, Muslim, Mālik (مَالِك), Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasāʾī, from Abū Saʿīd, that a man heard another reciting “Say: He is God, One” repeatedly. In the morning he came to the Prophet ﷺ and mentioned it, apparently regarding it as little. The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “By Him in whose hand is my soul, it is indeed equivalent to one third of the Qurʾān.”
Al-Qāḍī says: “Because this sūrah, short as it is, encompasses all knowledge of divinity and refutes those who deviate concerning it, the report says that it equals one third of the Qurʾān. The Qurʾān's aims are confined to explaining beliefs, rulings and narratives. Whoever equates it with the whole takes into account what is intended for its own sake among these.”
His words, “It is for some cause that whoever becomes a master becomes one”: the opening is:
“I resolved to stay for a morning.”
Mā (ما) is an additional particle giving indefiniteness and magnification: that is, it is for a great cause that whoever becomes a master becomes one.
[REVIEW T112-U3: The isolated half-line إقامة ذي صباح lacks its full poetic context. The literal provisional rendering above should be checked against the poem.]



T112.13 · “It suffices as proof for whoever acknowledges”

وكفى دليلا من اعترف

His words, “It suffices as proof for whoever acknowledges.” Man iʿtarafa (من اعترف) [whoever acknowledges] is the object of kafā (كفى) [suffices]. Its subject is what is indicated by “because it encompasses God's attributes.” The pronoun in “its excellence” refers to the sūrah. “Believes” is coordinated with “acknowledges”; “the Messenger of God's ﷺ statement” attaches to “believes.” “That the science of divine oneness” attaches to “as proof,” which is a specification (tamyīz (تمييز)). That is: for whoever acknowledges the sūrah's excellence and believes the Messenger's statement, this suffices as proof that the science of divine oneness has an exalted standing with God.
The Prophet's ﷺ statement intended here is the one he transmits at the end of the sūrah: “The seven heavens were founded …,” to the end. I have not found this report in the recognized source collections.
Al-Tirmidhī, Abū Dāwūd and Ibn Māja transmit from Burayda that the Messenger of God ﷺ heard a man saying: “O God, I ask You by my testimony that You are God—there is no god but You—the One, al-Ṣamad (الصمد), who neither begot nor was begotten, and to whom no one is equal.” The Prophet ﷺ said: “By Him in whose hand is my soul, he has asked God by His greatest name: when invoked by it, He answers; when asked by it, He gives.”



T112.14 · “He said: It has become assured”

فقال: وجبت

His words, “He said: It has become assured.” Mālik (مَالِك), Aḥmad, al-Tirmidhī and al-Nasāʾī transmitted the report from Abū Hurayra.



T112.15 · Commentary



A conclusion from the words of Shaykh Faṣīḥ al-Dīn—may God have mercy on him:
“‘God, al-Ṣamad (الصمد)’ is not joined by a conjunction to the preceding sentence, because it confirms and explains its content. The same applies to ‘He did not beget,’ because it confirms the content of ‘God, al-Ṣamad (الصمد)’: the absolutely self-sufficient one, whom everything needs, cannot be a begetter or begotten, since that necessarily entails need.
“‘Nor was He begotten’ is joined to ‘He did not beget,’ because ‘was not begotten’ does not express the meaning of ‘did not beget,’ and thus does not confirm its meaning; rather, the two sentences confirm the content of the preceding sentence. ‘And no one is equal to Him’ is joined [by a conjunction] because its content does not confirm the content of the two preceding sentences: it expresses that He cannot have any equal in anything mentioned concerning essence and attributes. He is therefore one, with no partner—exalted, holy and supremely great.
“The predicate is definite in ‘God, al-Ṣamad (الصمد)’ to negate [the claim of] anyone who asserts and names another as ṣamad (صمد). It is indefinite in ‘God, One,’ because they did not call things aḥad (أحد) in this sense.”
[REVIEW T112-U4: The scan reads نفيا لنفي من زعم وسمى غيره صمدا, literally “to negate the negation of whoever asserted and named another ṣamad (صمد).” This double negation is problematic. The bracketed rendering gives the apparent intended argument, not a verified emendation. Retain the flag pending another witness or the quoted source.]
The sūrah is complete.



al-Falaq · Sūrat al-Falaq

K IV:820–822 · T XVI:644–651

Q 113:1–5

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلْفَلَقِ ﴿1﴾ مِن شَرِّ مَا خَلَقَ ﴿2﴾ وَمِن شَرِّ غَاسِقٍ إِذَا وَقَبَ ﴿3﴾ وَمِن شَرِّ ٱلنَّفَّـٰثَـٰتِ فِى ٱلْعُقَدِ ﴿4﴾ وَمِن شَرِّ حَاسِدٍ إِذَا حَسَدَ ﴿5﴾

1. Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of the Daybreak 2. From the evil of that which He created 3. From the evil of the darkness when it is intense 4. And from the evil of malignant witchcraft 5. And from the evil of the envier when he envieth

K113.01 · Text and explanation



Sūrat al-Falaq (الفلق). There is disagreement about it [whether Meccan or Medinan]; it has five verses.
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.
“Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of daybreak, from the evil of what He created; from the evil of a darkening one when it enters; from the evil of those who blow upon knots; and from the evil of an envier when he envies.” [113:1–5]
[TEXT VARIANT: The separate four-volume transcription's heading says “Meccan; also said to be Medinan,” adds “revealed after al-Fīl,” and repeats the basmala (بسملة). Dubai's base heading is translated above.]



al-Falaq · Al-falaq and al-faraq mean morning, because night is split and parted away from it: faʿal has the me

K IV:820–822 · T XVI:644–651

Q 113:1

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلْفَلَقِ ﴿1﴾

1. Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of the Daybreak

K113.02 · Text and explanation



Al-falaq (الفلق) and al-faraq (الفرق) mean morning, because night is split and parted away from it: faʿal (فَعَل) has the meaning of mafʿūl (مفعول). The proverb says, “It is clearer than the breaking of morning,” or “than the parting of morning.” Related to this is their expression saṭaʿa al-furqān (سطع الفرقان) when dawn rises.
It is also said to mean everything God splits open: the earth for plants, mountains for springs, clouds for rain, wombs for children, grain and date-stones, and other things. It is also said to be a valley in Hell or a pit within it, from their use of falaq (فلق) for a depression in the ground; the plural is fulqān (فلقان).
It is reported that one of the Companions came to Syria and saw the houses of the protected non-Muslim communities (ahl al-dhimma), their comfortable lives and the worldly plenty granted them. He said, “I do not mind. Is al-falaq (الفلق) not awaiting them?” He was asked, “What is al-falaq (الفلق)?” He answered: “A chamber in Hell which, when opened, makes all the inhabitants of the Fire cry out at the intensity of its heat.”



T113.01 · “Because night is split away from it”

لأن الليل يفلق عنه

Sūrat al-Falaq (الفلق). Meccan; also said to be Medinan. Five verses.
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.
His words, “Because night is split away from it”: that is, night splits away from morning and morning emerges. Faʿal (فَعَل) has the meaning of mafʿūl (مفعول): night is what is split away from it.
His words, “It is also said to mean everything He splits open.” Al-Qāḍī says: “It includes all possible beings, for He split the darkness of nonexistence away from them with the light of bringing into existence—particularly what emerges from an origin, such as springs, rains, plants and children. In conventional use it is especially morning, which is why it was explained that way. Morning is singled out because it changes the condition, replaces the loneliness of night with the joy of light, evokes good through the Day of Resurrection, and signals that the one able to remove the darkness of night from this world can remove from the person seeking refuge what he fears. The word Lord is more fitting here than the other names, because protection from harms is an act of nurture.”
[REVIEW T113-U1: The scanned quotation at p.645 has difficult wording محاكاة الخير بيوم القيامة and a final word read provisionally as تربية. OpenITI gives قريبة. The contextual rendering of this final clause is provisional and must be checked against al-Qāḍī's source.]



T113.02 · “I do not mind. Is al-falaq not awaiting them?”

لا أبالي، أليس من ورائهم الفلق؟ 

His words, “I do not mind. Is al-falaq (الفلق) not awaiting them?”: that is, I do not mind the beauty of their houses and the ease of their lives. He then begins a new interrogative sentence to elicit assent: “Is al-falaq (الفلق) not awaiting them?”
Comparable is what we transmitted from al-Bukhārī, Muslim, Aḥmad, al-Tirmidhī and al-Nasāʾī, from Ibn ʿAbbās in a long report from ʿUmar—may God be pleased with him: “I entered upon the Messenger of God ﷺ and greeted him. He was reclining upon the woven strands of a mat which had left marks upon his side. I sat down and lifted my head to look about the house. By God, I saw nothing there to catch the eye except three hides. I said: ‘Messenger of God, pray that God grant plenty to your community: He has granted plenty to Persia and Rome, although they do not worship God.’ He sat upright and said, ‘Are you in doubt, son of al-Khaṭṭāb? These are people whose good things have been given them in advance in this worldly life.’ I said: ‘Ask forgiveness for me, Messenger of God.’” The report [continues].
As for interpreting al-falaq (الفلق) as a valley in Hell, Muḥyī al-Sunna transmits one report from Ibn ʿAbbās that al-falaq (الفلق) is a prison in Hell, and from al-Kalbī that it is a valley in Hell.
[TEXT CORRECTION T113-C4: OpenITI contains nonsensical extra words after “his side.” They are absent from the inspected scan p.645 and are not translated.]



al-Falaq · “From the evil of what He created”: from the evil of His creation

K IV:820–822 · T XVI:644–651

Q 113:2

مِن شَرِّ مَا خَلَقَ ﴿2﴾

2. From the evil of that which He created

K113.03 · Text and explanation



“From the evil of what He created”: from the evil of His creation. Their evil consists in the disobedience and sins committed by morally responsible living beings, and the harm they do one another through injustice, aggression, killing, striking, abuse and so forth; in what the non-responsible among living beings do by devouring, tearing with the teeth, stinging and biting, such as predatory animals and creeping creatures; and in the kinds of harm God has placed in inanimate things, such as burning in fire and killing in poison.



T113.03 · “Their evil is what morally responsible living beings do”

وشرهم ما يفعله المكلفون من الحيوان

His words, “Their evil is what morally responsible living beings do.” Perhaps “among living beings” is supplied as an explanation of “the morally responsible” in order to exclude the angels.
Al-Qāḍī says: “The world of creation is singled out as that from which refuge is sought because evil is confined to it: the world of command is wholly good. Its evil is either voluntary—confined to its agent or extending to others, as in unbelief and injustice—or natural, as in fire's burning and poisons' destruction.”



al-Falaq · Al-ghāsiq is night when its darkness becomes dense, from His statement “… until the darkness of nigh

K IV:820–822 · T XVI:644–651

Q 113:3

وَمِن شَرِّ غَاسِقٍ إِذَا وَقَبَ ﴿3﴾

3. From the evil of the darkness when it is intense

K113.04 · Text and explanation



Al-ghāsiq (الغاسق) is night when its darkness becomes dense, from His statement “… until the darkness of night” [17:78]. Related expressions are “the eye ghasaqat”—filled with tears—and “the wound ghasaqat”—filled with blood. Its wuqūb (وقوب) is the entry of its darkness into everything. One says waqabat al-shams when the sun sets. In the report, when he saw that the sun had set, he said, “This is the time when it becomes lawful”—meaning the sunset prayer.
It is also said to be the moon when full. ʿĀʾisha—may God be pleased with her—reports: the Messenger of God ﷺ took my hand, pointed to the moon, and said, “Seek refuge in God from the evil of this, for it is the darkening one when it enters.” Its wuqūb (وقوب) is its entering eclipse and becoming dark.
Al-ghāsiq (الغاسق) may also mean a black snake; its waqb is its striking and piercing. Waqb means a hole, as in waqbat al-tharīd (الثريد), the hollow in a dish of bread soaked in broth.
Refuge is sought from the evil of night because evil spreads more widely in it and is harder to guard against. Hence their sayings, “Night conceals misfortune better,” and “Night has become treacherous.” When it grows dark, treachery increases. Evil is attributed to night because it accompanies it, occurring within it.
[TEXT VARIANT K113-C1: Dubai p.646 cites the opening of 17:78, “Establish prayer at the sun's decline,” rather than the words containing ghasaq. The independent four-volume transcription and Turkish p.1470 give the relevant latter clause, translated above. Both forms are recorded.]



T113.04 · “When its darkness becomes dense”

إذا اعتكر ظلامه

His words, “When its darkness becomes dense.” Al-Jawharī: “Iʿtakara al-ẓalām (اعتكر الظلام): it became intermingled, as though part of it returned upon another because of the slowness of its clearing.”
His words, “One says waqabat al-shams when it sets.” Al-Rāghib: “Waqb is like a hollow in something; hence waqabat al-shams. Īqāb (إيقاب) is its disappearing.”
His words, “This is the time when it becomes lawful”: ḥīn (حين) is nominative; in ḥillihā (حِلّها) the ḥāʾ (حاء (ح)) has kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)) and the lām (لام (ل)) is genitive. Al-Nihāya: “In the report: when he saw that the sun had waqabat, he said, ‘This is the time of its lawfulness.’ Waqabat means ‘set’; the time of its lawfulness is the time when its performance becomes lawful, meaning the sunset prayer. Wuqūb (وقوب) means entry into anything.”



T113.05 · “From ʿĀʾisha—may God be pleased with her”

وعن عائشة-رضي الله عنها-

His words, “From ʿĀʾisha—may God be pleased with her.” The report was transmitted by Imam Aḥmad and al-Tirmidhī; it does not contain “he took my hand.”
The Imam transmits from Ibn Qutayba: “The moon was called ghāsiq (غاسق) because it is eclipsed and darkens: its light departs and it becomes black. Its wuqūb (وقوب) is entering that blackness.” He also says: “It has been established that the moon is not luminous in its own body; it is therefore called ghāsiq (غاسق). Its wuqūb (وقوب) is the dark moon at the end of the month, when its strength is slight and it is at its lowest condition. For that reason sorcerers then occupy themselves with sorcery that produces illness. This accords with the occasion of revelation of the two sūrahs.” God knows best.
His words, “Night conceals misfortune better.” Al-Maydānī says: “That is, do what you wish at night, for it conceals your secret better. The first to say this was Sāriya ibn ʿUwaymir ibn ʿAdī al-ʿUqaylī.” Its occasion is mentioned in his book.
His words, “Night has become treacherous”: it is said to be like aḥṣada al-zarʿ (أحصد الزرع) [the crop became ready for harvest], that is, the time of its treachery has arrived; it is also said to mean “it has become possessed of treachery.”



al-Falaq · Al-naffāthāt are women, souls, or groups of female sorcerers who tie knots in threads, blow upon the

K IV:820–822 · T XVI:644–651

Q 113:4

وَمِن شَرِّ ٱلنَّفَّـٰثَـٰتِ فِى ٱلْعُقَدِ ﴿4﴾

4. And from the evil of malignant witchcraft

K113.05 · Text and explanation



Al-naffāthāt (النفّاثات) are women, souls, or groups of female sorcerers who tie knots in threads, blow upon them and recite incantations. Nafth is blowing with saliva. This has no effect—unless, indeed, something harmful is administered as food, drink or something to smell, or the bewitched person is brought into contact with it in some way. But God—mighty and majestic—may perform an act at that moment as a test by which those steadfast upon truth are distinguished from the Ḥashwiyya and the ignorant common people. The Ḥashwiyya and the rabble attribute it to the women and their blowing; those steadfast in the firm word pay it no attention and attach no weight to it.
If you ask, “What, then, does seeking refuge from their evil mean?”, I answer: there are three interpretations. First, refuge is sought from their activity, the practice of sorcery, and their sin in doing it. Second, refuge is sought from their leading people astray through their sorcery and deceiving them by their false practices. Third, refuge is sought from whatever evil God causes to befall someone at the moment of their blowing.
[TEXT CORRECTION K113-C2: Both electronic Arabic texts have النفخ من ريق. The Dubai scan p.648 clearly reads النفخ مع ريق, “blowing with saliva”; Turkish p.1470 agrees. The scan governs this translation. Ḥashwiyya is retained as the author's polemical label, not silently converted into a neutral modern school name.]



T113.06 · “By which those steadfast upon truth are distinguished from the Ḥashwiyya”

يتميز به الثبت على الحق من الحشوية

His words, “By which those steadfast upon truth are distinguished from the Ḥashwiyya.” Al-Intiṣāf: “The Qadariyya deny sorcery, whereas the Book and the Sunna attest its occurrence; the command to seek refuge from it is evidence for it. The Messenger of God ﷺ was bewitched with a comb, combings and the sheath of a male palm's spathe.”
I say: we transmitted the report from al-Bukhārī, Muslim and Ibn Māja, from ʿĀʾisha—may God be pleased with her:
“The Messenger of God ﷺ was bewitched, so that he imagined he had done something when he had not. One day, while with me, he prayed to God repeatedly, then said: ‘Have you realized, ʿĀʾisha, that God has given me an answer concerning what I asked Him?’ I said, ‘What is that, Messenger of God?’ He said: ‘Two men came to me. One sat at my head and the other at my feet. One asked his companion: What ails the man? He answered: He has been bewitched. He asked: Who bewitched him? He answered: Labīd ibn al-Aʿṣam, the Jew of Banū Zurayq. He asked: With what? He answered: With a comb, combings and the sheath of a male palm's spathe. He asked: Where is it? He answered: In the well of Dhī Arwān.’” The report [continues].
[TEXT CORRECTION T113-C5: The scan supplies the question to ʿĀʾisha and her answer, absent from the OpenITI transcription. They are retained here.]



T113.07 · Commentary

والله يعصمك من الناس

Al-Rāghib: “The effect of sorcery upon the Prophet ﷺ did not concern him inasmuch as he was a prophet; it concerned his body inasmuch as he was a human being or mortal, just as he ate, excreted, became angry, desired and fell ill, while remaining sound in his prophetic capacity. It would impair prophethood only [if] sorcery were found to affect something pertaining to prophethood. Just so, his being wounded and having his front teeth broken on the day of Uḥud did not impair the protection God guaranteed him in ‘God will protect you from people’ [5:67]. Likewise, the polytheists' gaining control over certain regions within Islam is not taken as invalidating the perfection of Islam stated in ‘Today I have perfected your religion for you’ [5:3].”
Al-Qāḍī says: “This does not make the unbelievers truthful in calling him bewitched, for they meant that he was mad through sorcery.”
[REVIEW T113-U2: The scan p.648 reads فيصح من حيث نبي and then أو وجد للسحر تأثير. The first is provisionally read as preservation of prophetic soundness; “[if]” expresses the apparently intended conditional of the second, whose printed أو is problematic. Check al-Rāghib's source before removing the flag. At p.649 the scan has بما يقع, correcting OpenITI's بما لا يقع, which reverses the argument.]



T113.08 · “The rabble”

الرعاع

Al-Nihāya: “He was bewitched with a comb and combings”: these are the hairs that fall from the head and beard when combed. It is also transmitted as mushāqa (مشاقة): “what breaks off from silk and flax when cleaned and combed. Mashq is pulling something to lengthen it.” “Juff is the casing of the spathe, the covering over it.”
His words, “The rabble”: immature youths and the base multitude.
His words, “Scheming women”: he likens their scheming to sorcery. The author of al-Intiṣāf abridged it and then said: “Had anyone other than al-Zamakhsharī offered this interpretation, it would have been counted among the innovations of exegesis.”



al-Falaq · They may also mean scheming women, from His statement “Your scheming is indeed great” [12:28], liken

K IV:820–822 · T XVI:644–651

Q 113:4–5

وَمِن شَرِّ ٱلنَّفَّـٰثَـٰتِ فِى ٱلْعُقَدِ ﴿4﴾ وَمِن شَرِّ حَاسِدٍ إِذَا حَسَدَ ﴿5﴾

4. And from the evil of malignant witchcraft 5. And from the evil of the envier when he envieth

K113.06 · Text and explanation



They may also mean scheming women, from His statement “Your scheming is indeed great” [12:28], likening their scheming to sorcery and blowing on knots; or women who tempt men by presenting themselves to them and displaying their attractions, as though bewitching them through that.
“When he envies”: when his envy becomes manifest and he acts as it dictates, seeking misfortunes for the person envied. If he does not manifest the effect of what he conceals, no harm from him reaches the person he envies. Rather, he harms himself by grieving at another's happiness. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz is reported to have said: “I have never seen a wrongdoer who more resembles someone wronged than an envier.”
The envier's evil may also mean his sin and the ugliness of his condition at the time of his envy and his displaying its effect.



al-Falaq · If you ask, “‘From the evil of what He created’ includes everything from which refuge is sought

K IV:820–822 · T XVI:644–651

Q 113:1–5

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلْفَلَقِ ﴿1﴾ مِن شَرِّ مَا خَلَقَ ﴿2﴾ وَمِن شَرِّ غَاسِقٍ إِذَا وَقَبَ ﴿3﴾ وَمِن شَرِّ ٱلنَّفَّـٰثَـٰتِ فِى ٱلْعُقَدِ ﴿4﴾ وَمِن شَرِّ حَاسِدٍ إِذَا حَسَدَ ﴿5﴾

1. Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of the Daybreak 2. From the evil of that which He created 3. From the evil of the darkness when it is intense 4. And from the evil of malignant witchcraft 5. And from the evil of the envier when he envieth

K113.07 · Text and explanation



If you ask, “‘From the evil of what He created’ includes everything from which refuge is sought. What is the point, then, of subsequently seeking refuge from the darkening one, the women who blow and the envier?”, I answer: their evil is singled out from all evil because its operation is hidden and reaches a person from where he does not know, as though he were treacherously struck by it. They say: “The worst enemy is the one who conceals his hostility and plots against you without your perceiving it.”
If you ask, “Why are some of those from whom refuge is sought made definite and others indefinite?”, I answer: al-naffāthāt (النفّاثات) is made definite because every such blower is evil. Ghāsiq (غاسق) is indefinite because evil is not present in every darkening night, but in some and not others. Likewise, not every envier causes harm. There is even praiseworthy envy: envy in good things. Hence his statement—blessings and peace be upon him—“There is no envy except in two cases.”
Abū Tammām said:
“One who envies noble accomplishments is no [blameworthy] envier.”
He also said:
“Loftiness is such that envy of its like is fair.”
It is transmitted from the Messenger of God ﷺ: “Whoever recites the two sūrahs of refuge (al-Muʿawwidhatān) is as though he had recited all the books God Most High has sent down.”
[TEXT VARIANT K113-C3: Dubai p.650 omits شر العداة, “the worst enemy,” before المداجي. It is present in the independent four-volume transcription and the Turkish Arabic/Turkish passage; it is included here and the difference recorded.]



T113.09 · “As though he were treacherously struck by it”

كأنما يغتال به

His words, “As though he were treacherously struck by it.” Al-Asās: “So-and-so treacherously attacks whoever passes him; he killed him by stealth; I fear his ghāʾila (غائلة), that is, the eventual consequence of his evil.”
His words, “There is no envy except in two cases.” We transmitted from al-Bukhārī, from Abū Hurayra, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “There is no envy except concerning two: a man to whom God has given the Qurʾān, who recites it at times throughout night and day. His neighbour hears him and says, ‘Would that I had been given what this man was given, so that I might do as he does!’ And a man to whom God has given wealth, who spends it as it should be spent. Someone says, ‘Would that I had been given what this man was given, so that I might do as he does!’”
Al-Nihāya: “Ḥasad is that a man sees a blessing enjoyed by his brother and wishes it would leave him and become his own instead. Ghabṭ (غبط) is to wish to have its like without wishing it to leave him. The report means: there is no harmless envy except in two cases.”



T113.10 · “One who envies …”

وما حاسد

His words, “One who envies …”: its opening is:
“I am indeed envied, and I excuse my envier.”
It is also said that its opening is:
“They envied him his glory—without being blameworthy;
one who envies noble accomplishments is no [blameworthy] envier.”
He also said:
“Excuse your envier for what has been bestowed especially upon you:
loftiness is such that envy of its like is fair.”
Mithl [like] here is like what occurs in your saying “[his like] is generous”: that is, loftiness itself is something of which envy is fair.
[REVIEW T113-U3: The scan's grammatical example is truncated: مثل هاهنا مثل ما في قولك: يجود. The bracketed words supply its apparent point; check the quoted poetic/grammatical context.]
The sūrah is complete.



al-Nās · Sūrat al-Nās

K IV:823–825 · T XVI:652–656

Q 114:1–6

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿1﴾ مَلِكِ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿2﴾ إِلَـٰهِ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿3﴾ مِن شَرِّ ٱلْوَسْوَاسِ ٱلْخَنَّاسِ ﴿4﴾ ٱلَّذِى يُوَسْوِسُ فِى صُدُورِ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿5﴾ مِنَ ٱلْجِنَّةِ وَٱلنَّاسِ ﴿6﴾

1. Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind 2. The King of mankind 3. The god of mankind 4. From the evil of the sneaking whisperer 5. Who whispereth in the hearts of mankind 6. Of the jinn and of mankind

K114.01 · Text and explanation



Sūrat al-Nās (الناس). There is disagreement about it [whether Meccan or Medinan]; it has six verses.
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.
“Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of humankind, the King of humankind, the God of humankind, from the evil of the whisperer who withdraws, who whispers in the breasts of humankind, from among jinn and humankind.” [114:1–6]
[TEXT VARIANT: The separate four-volume transcription calls it Meccan, also said to be Medinan, adds “revealed after al-Falaq (الفلق),” and repeats the basmala (بسملة). Dubai's base heading is translated above.]



al-Nās · “Qul aʿūdhu” has been read with the hamza omitted and its vowel transferred to the lām

K IV:823–825 · T XVI:652–656

Q 114:1

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿1﴾

1. Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind

K114.02 · Text and explanation



“Qul aʿūdhu (قل أعوذ)” has been read with the hamza (همزة (ء)) omitted and its vowel transferred to the lām (لام (ل)). Comparable is “fa-khudh arbaʿata (أربعة)” [2:260].
If you ask, “Why say ‘in the Lord of humankind,’ annexing Lord specifically to them?”, I answer: because refuge is sought from the evil of the one who whispers in human breasts. It is as though one had said: I seek refuge from the evil of the one who whispers to people, in their Lord who controls their affairs, who is their God and the one they worship—just as some dependants, when a calamity befalls them, seek help from their master, the one they serve, and the governor of their affairs.



T114.01 · “Why say ‘in the Lord of humankind’?”

لم قيل: {برب الناس}

Sūrat al-Nās (الناس). Meccan; also said to be Medinan. Six verses.
In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Merciful.
His words, “Why say ‘in the Lord of humankind’?”: that is, He is the Lord of all worlds, so why single out humankind here? He answers: it is humankind alone who seek help from their Lord, owner and object of worship against the affliction that befalls them.



T114.02 · “Just as some dependants, when a calamity befalls them, seek help from their master, the one they serve, and the governor of their affairs”

كما يستغيث بعض الموالي إذا اعتراهم خطب بسيدهم ومخدومهم ووالي أمرهم

His words, “Just as some dependants, when a calamity befalls them, seek help from their master, the one they serve, and the governor of their affairs.” He observes an ascent in the help sought: protection through governance is stronger than through being served, while protection through mastery is weaker than through being served. Likewise, the sense of irresistible dominion in divinity is higher than in ownership, and [that is higher] than in lordship.
Some commentaries say: “Repelling Satan's evil and whispering is through one of three things: either not enabling him to whisper, inasmuch as He is Lord; or enabling him but forcibly preventing him, inasmuch as He is King; or forbidding him to whisper as a rebuke, yet willing it by choice, inasmuch as He is God.”
Alternatively, it may be said: the servant seeks refuge in God from Satan, grounding the request in appropriate attributes in ascending order. He first describes Him—mighty and majestic—as Lord, because the first thing the servant knows of his Lord is that He bestows blessings upon his outward and inward being. He then advances to knowing that He governs him and owns him, and then to knowing that He alone is the object of worship without qualification and that there is no final resort except to Him.
[REVIEW T114-U2: The difficult third alternative reads لكن يريدها اختيارا. “Yet willing it by choice” preserves the wording; the subject and the intended theological distinction between prohibition and willing need checking in the unnamed source. Do not replace it with “the devil chooses it” without evidence.]



al-Nās · If you ask, “What is the relation of ‘King of humankind, God of humankind’ to ‘Lord of humankind’?”,

K IV:823–825 · T XVI:652–656

Q 114:1–3

بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَـٰنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿1﴾ مَلِكِ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿2﴾ إِلَـٰهِ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿3﴾

1. Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind 2. The King of mankind 3. The god of mankind

K114.03 · Text and explanation



If you ask, “What is the relation of ‘King of humankind, God of humankind’ to ‘Lord of humankind’?”, I answer: both are explanatory apposition (ʿaṭf bayān (عطف بيان)), as in “the conduct of Abū Ḥafṣ, ʿUmar, al-Fārūq.” He is clarified by “King of humankind,” then further clarified by “God of humankind.” For someone other than Him may be called “lord of humankind,” as in “They took their rabbis and monks as lords apart from God” [9:31]; someone may also be called “king of humankind.” But “God of humankind” is exclusive, admitting no partnership, and is therefore made the culmination of the clarification.
If you ask, “Why not be content with explicitly mentioning the annexed noun ‘humankind’ once?”, I answer: because explanatory apposition serves clarification, making it a suitable place for explicit mention rather than substitution by a pronoun.



T114.03 · “Someone may also be called king of humankind”

وقد يقال: ملك الناس

His words, “Someone may also be called king of humankind.” Al-Rāghib: “A king is one who directs the populace through command and prohibition. This is peculiar to governing rational beings; hence ‘king of humankind’ is said, but not ‘king of things.’”



al-Nās · Al-waswās is a noun meaning whispering (waswasa), as zalzāl means shaking (zalzala)

K IV:823–825 · T XVI:652–656

Q 114:4–5

مِن شَرِّ ٱلْوَسْوَاسِ ٱلْخَنَّاسِ ﴿4﴾ ٱلَّذِى يُوَسْوِسُ فِى صُدُورِ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿5﴾

4. From the evil of the sneaking whisperer 5. Who whispereth in the hearts of mankind

K114.04 · Text and explanation



Al-waswās (الوسواس) is a noun meaning whispering (waswasa (وسوسة)), as zalzāl (زَلْزال) means shaking (zalzala (زلزلة)). The verbal noun, however, is wiswās (وِسْواس), with kasra (كَسْرَة (ـِ)), like zilzāl (زِلْزال). What is meant is Satan: he is named with the verbal noun as though he were whispering itself, since that is his craft and the occupation to which he constantly devotes himself. Alternatively, “the possessor of whispering” is intended. Waswasa (وسوسة) is a faint sound; hence waswās (وَسْواس) al-ḥuly, the rustling of jewellery.
Al-khannās (الخنّاس) is one whose habit is to withdraw. It is a form associating him with khunūs (خنوس), retreating, like al-ʿawwāj (العوّاج) and al-battāt (البتّات) [occupational forms]. For it is related from Saʿīd ibn Jubayr that when a person remembers his Lord, Satan withdraws and turns away; when he is heedless, Satan whispers to him.
“Alladhī (الذي) yuwaswisu” [who whispers] may occupy a position corresponding to any of the three case vowels: genitive as a description, or nominative or accusative as an expression of invective. It is fitting for the reciter to stop at al-khannās (الخنّاس) and begin with alladhī (الذي) yuwaswisu on either of these two interpretations [nominative or accusative].
[REVIEW K114-U1: “These two interpretations” naturally refers to nominative/accusative in the base passage. Ṭībī explicitly glosses it as “description and invective” below; the Turkish translation follows that gloss. The disagreement is retained rather than silently resolved.]



T114.04 · “The verbal noun, however, is wiswās”

وأما المصدر فوسواس

His words, “The verbal noun, however, is wiswās (وِسْواس).” According to one explanation, by waswās (وَسْواس) he meant the noun which has the meaning of waswasa (وسوسة), itself the verbal noun. The Maghribī grammarians say: the difference between a verbal noun (maṣdar (مصدر)) and a noun of the verbal action (ism (اسم) al-maṣdar (المصدر)) is this. The meaning expressed by the real act—the origin of the grammatical verb—may be considered together with the agent's engagement in it, its proceeding from him, and its renewed occurrence. The expression assigned to that meaning, restricted by this qualification, is called a maṣdar (مصدر). If that qualification is not considered, the expression assigned to the meaning without the stated restriction is an ism (اسم) al-maṣdar (المصدر).
His words, “His craft”: another reading is “his livelihood” (ḍayʿatuhu (ضيعته)). Al-Nihāya: “A man's ḍayʿa (ضيعة) is that from which he earns his living, such as a craft, trade, an industry and so on.”
His words, “Associated with khunūs (خنوس)”: he says “associated” because withdrawing has been made his habit.
His words, “When a person remembers his Lord, he withdraws.” We transmitted in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, as a suspended report (taʿlīqan (تعليقًا)), from Ibn ʿAbbās: the Messenger of God ﷺ said, “Satan crouches upon the heart of the son of Adam. When he remembers God, he withdraws; when he is heedless, he whispers.”



T114.05 · Commentary

ويحسن أن يقف القارئ

His words from “It is fitting for the reciter to stop” to “on either of these two interpretations”: that is, description and invective.
In al-Kawāshī: “A stop at al-khannās (الخنّاس) is sufficient if you make [what follows] nominative or accusative in blame; it is not permissible if you make it genitive as a description of al-khannās (الخنّاس).”
I say: its impermissibility is questionable, considering the verse ending. The author of al-Murshid says: “When you have said ‘the All-Merciful, the Merciful,’ the stop is sufficient because it is the end of a verse. It is not a complete stop, because the genitive expression—I mean ‘Master of the Day of Judgment’—is left without its governor, and the epithet is separated from what it qualifies. Likewise a stop at ‘the straight [path]’ is permissible but not a good stop; it is permitted only because it is the end of the verse.”
[TEXT CORRECTION T114-C1: OpenITI reads وفي عدم الجواز نظرا للفاصلة. The scan p.654 reads وفي عدم الجواز نظر، نظرا للفاصلة: the missing نظر is restored in this translation. “Sufficient,” “complete” and “good” preserve distinct stop categories, not interchangeable praise.]



al-Nās · “From among jinn and humankind” explains “who whispers,” on the basis that Satan is of two kinds, ji

K IV:823–825 · T XVI:652–656

Q 114:6

مِنَ ٱلْجِنَّةِ وَٱلنَّاسِ ﴿6﴾

6. Of the jinn and of mankind

K114.05 · Text and explanation



“From among jinn and humankind” explains “who whispers,” on the basis that Satan is of two kinds, jinn and human, as He says: “devils of humankind and jinn” [6:112]. Abū Dharr—may God be pleased with him—is reported to have asked a man: “Have you sought refuge in God from human Satan?”
Min may also attach to yuwaswisu, denoting a starting point: that is, he whispers in their breasts from the direction of the jinn and from the direction of human beings.
It is also said that “from among jinn and humankind” explains al-nās (الناس), and that the name al-nās (الناس) applies to jinn. They adduce nafar (نفر) [a group] and rijāl (رجال) [men] in Sūrat al-Jinn as evidence. I do not accept it as established: jinn are called jinn because they are concealed, and people nās (ناس) because they are manifest, from īnās (إيناس), seeing, just as they are called bashar. Even if nās (ناس) applied to both classes, and that were sound and established, it would not suit the Qurʾān's eloquence and its freedom from affectation.



T114.06 · “From the direction of human beings”

ومن جهة الناس

His words, “From the direction of human beings”: for example, he whispers into a Muslim's heart, concerning astrologers and soothsayers, that they know the unseen; and concerning jinn, that they harm and benefit.
In al-Muṭṭalaʿ: “According to one explanation, if the phrase is taken as clarification, ‘from among jinn and humankind’ is a circumstantial expression (ḥāl) relating to the pronoun in ‘who whispers.’”



T114.07 · “I do not accept it as established”

وما أحقه

His words, “I do not accept it as established”: that is, I do not establish it, from their saying ḥaqqaqtu al-shayʾa aḥuqquhu (حقّقت الشيء أحقّه), “I established the thing.”
The Imam says: “It is said that ‘from among jinn and humankind’ gives two divisions included under ‘in the breasts of al-nās (الناس).’ It is as though the common element shared by jinn and humans were called insān, while the human being were also called insān; the expression insān would thus apply equivocally to both genus and species. The evidence is the report that a group of jinn came and were asked, ‘Who are you?’ They answered, ‘Nās (ناس) from among the jinn.’ God has also called them rijāl (رجال) in ‘There were men of humankind who sought refuge with men of the jinn, and they increased them in distress’ [72:6]. Thus it would be permissible to call them nās (ناس) here. This strained statement—he does not mean that it is weak—for making insān the name of a genus containing both jinn and humans is remote from the language.”
[REVIEW T114-U3: The final sentence is problematic in the print itself: وهذا القول المتعسف لا يريد أنه ضعيف. Its wording is retained, even though its denial of weakness conflicts with the following criticism. The phrase لا يريد may be displaced from the next gloss; that is a hypothesis, not an adopted correction. Check the Imam's source before repairing it. Mā (ما) in وما أحقه is negative, not an exclamation; the scan, Ṭībī's gloss and Turkish p.1480 agree.]



al-Nās · Better than that is to understand al-nās as al-nāsī [the forgetful one], like “the day the caller ca

K IV:823–825 · T XVI:652–656

Q 114:5–6

ٱلَّذِى يُوَسْوِسُ فِى صُدُورِ ٱلنَّاسِ ﴿5﴾ مِنَ ٱلْجِنَّةِ وَٱلنَّاسِ ﴿6﴾

5. Who whispereth in the hearts of mankind 6. Of the jinn and of mankind

K114.06 · Text and explanation



Better than that is to understand al-nās (الناس) as al-nāsī (الناسي) [the forgetful one], like “the day the caller calls” [54:6], and as “from where al-nās (الناس) departed” [2:199] has been read. It is then explained by “jinn and humankind,” since the two weighty kinds (al-thaqalān (الثقلان)) are the two species characterized by forgetting God's due—mighty and majestic is He.
It is related from the Messenger of God ﷺ: “Two sūrahs have been sent down to me the like of which has never been sent down. You will never recite two sūrahs more beloved or more pleasing to God than these”—meaning the two sūrahs of refuge. The two sūrahs of refuge are also called al-muqashqishatān (المقشقشتان).
[TRANSLATOR'S NOTE: The phonological argument is the omission of final yāʾ (ياء (ي)) in al-nāsī (الناسي), parallel to al-dāʿī (الداعي)/al-dāʿ (الداع). No claim that the ordinary reading means “forgetful” is being added.]



T114.08 · “Better than that”

وأجود منه

His words, “Better than that”: that is, better than this strained statement; he does not mean that it is an interpretation possessing merit. It is to take al-nās (الناس) in “the breasts of al-nās (الناس)” as “the forgetful one.” It can then be divided into jinn and humans, for they are two classes characterized by forgetting God's due.
[TEXT NOTE: The scan/transcription has صفتان موصوفان, literally “two attributes described.” The contextual “two classes” follows the preceding discussion and needs verification against another witness.]



T114.09 · “Al-muqashqishatān”

المقشقشتان

His words, “Al-muqashqishatān (المقشقشتان).” Al-Nihāya: “In the report, the sūrahs ‘Say: O unbelievers’ and ‘Say: He is God, One’ are called al-muqashqishatān (المقشقشتان): the two that free one from hypocrisy and association of partners with God, as a sick person is freed from his illness. One says taqashqasha al-marīḍ (المريض) when the sick person recovers and becomes well.”
[TRANSLATOR'S NOTE: The base text applies the term to al-Falaq (الفلق) and al-Nās (الناس); this quotation applies it to al-Kāfirūn and al-Ikhlāṣ. The differing identifications are preserved.]
The sūrah is complete.



al-Fātiḥa · source and review notes
Ṭībī coverage is only the completed first instalment, printed I:677–686. The remaining Fātiḥa commentary has not been translated in this release.

REVIEW RECORD AND OPEN FLAGS
1. Full first pass of the author’s sūrah commentary is now present. The opening of the entire book is a separate future unit.
2. K001.12–13 restore the major K-DKA transcription omission. They are checked against the separate K-JK transcription and the actual embedded Arabic text in the Dubai scan, I:734–741. The modern editorial source note after the proverb in K-JK is excluded.
3. The contrast مِلْك / مُلْك is ownership / kingship. This order is confirmed by the scanned vocalization and Turkish comparison. The preference for malik is Zamakhsharī’s, not an editorial choice to suppress the mālik reading.
4. K001-U1: both transcriptions say “Raḥmān of this world and the next, and Raḥīm of this world.” The unexpected final “this world” is also supported by the Turkish text, p.76, and is retained; further Arabic scan collation is pending. Do not silently correct it.
5. K001-U2: the vowel-assimilation examples and minor poetic/proper-name vocalizations require a second Arabic scan pass.
6. Reports are translated as reports. No new independent judgment of authenticity is supplied. Ṭībī’s comments on the closing reports must be translated separately at the end of his Fātiḥa layer.
7. Still needed: complete line-by-line scan comparison of K001.01–22 against ʿUbaykān and the YEK facing Arabic; a second English fidelity pass; the rest of Ṭībī I:677–770.
8. Technical policy: iltifāt, badal, ḥāl, tawfīq, alṭāf, milk/mulk, and the actual Arabic reading variants are preserved where their distinctions carry the argument. Square-bracketed clarifications are editorial, not medieval source text.




C04 TURKISH COMPARISON ADDENDUM
The complete Turkish Fātiḥa text, even-numbered pages 66–98, paragraphs 9–78, was read against this draft. The English poem was corrected to news concerning Abū al-Aswad, rather than transmission on his authority. Nibz and allaha were corrected in the lexical discussion. Arabic remains controlling; Turkish expansions, editorial hadith judgments, and occasional interpretive differences have not been silently incorporated. The Turkish numbered verse scheme excludes the opening basmala from its displayed 1–7 sequence; Arabic reference numbers in this draft retain the usual basmala-counting scheme.

The next argument distinguishes simple emphasis from exclusive restriction, discusses pagan invocation and the treaty of al-Ḥudaybiya, and then addresses the evidentiary force of Q11:41.

REVIEW NOTES
- All ten scanned pages I:677–686 have been inspected. Source quotations have been kept attributed; they are not presented as independent statements by the present translator.
- Al-Intiṣāf and al-Inṣāf are distinct titles. Do not merge them.
- “The seventh verse” in the Ibn ʿAbbās report is retained. It counts the basmala as completing the number seven, not as relocating it to the sūrah’s end.
- T001-U1 and T001-U2 remain open.
- The modern Dubai footnotes sometimes criticize the hadith chains or supply manuscript variants. They are separate editorial material, not automatically part of Ṭībī’s own commentary.

al-Sharḥ · source and review notes
SOURCE AND REVIEW NOTES
K094-U1: K-DKA's transcription has ʿumūm al-nubuwwa, “the full scope of prophethood,” where the Kashshāf quoted and discussed by Ṭībī has humūm al-nubuwwa, “the concerns of prophethood.” The latter is used here and the difference is retained for collation.
K094-U2: K-DKA's transcription has a damaged or variant form in ʿUmar's saying; Ṭībī preserves and explicitly explains sabahlalan. Likewise, the threefold explanation of lifting the burden is rendered according to the clearer quoted form in Ṭībī.
T094-U1: The text of the magnification argument on p. 499 is syntactically damaged around “required it to reach its utmost extent.” Visual inspection confirms the incomplete phrase أن يتناهى في in the printed edition itself. The translation renders the evident argumentative connection and does not claim to establish every missing word. This locus remains for comparison with another textual witness.
T094-U2: Technical discussions of readings, grammatical permissibility, legal acknowledgment, and the authors' sectarian polemic are translated as their historical claims. The closing merit report and other transmitted reports have not been independently authenticated.

al-Tīn · source and review notes
TEXTUAL NOTES
K095-U1: The derivative forms ammān and karrām are retained as morphological examples; the unvowelled electronic source alone does not establish their full vocalization. The chapter’s argument is about the city’s trustworthiness and security.
T095-U1: XVI:505 prints عند الغذاء, “as food,” correcting the electronic الغداء, “the midday meal.” The source’s historical therapeutic claims are translated as quoted claims, not endorsed as medical guidance.
K095-U2: The verse rendering gives an intelligible English question; K095.06 and T095.06 preserve the author’s more specific grammatical construal, “what makes you a liar on account of denying requital,” and its alternative address to the Prophet.

al-Qadr · source and review notes
TEXTUAL NOTES
K097-U1: The first interpretative statement makes God the exclusive agent of revelation; Ṭībī’s explicit grammatical explanation confirms this. The Turkish comparator’s wording appears to make the Qurʾān itself the agent; it has not been followed.
K097-U2: The DKA text supplies the two distinct explanations of the thousand months. The embedded base in the Ṭībī transcription telescopes part of their wording; the complete DKA passage is retained.
T097-U1: Ṣila and mawṣūl in the discussion of the verbal noun concern the relation of a governing expression to its dependent material; the intervening hiya is the difficulty. The English renders the grammatical relation rather than implying an ordinary relative pronoun.

al-Zalzalah · source and review notes
TEXTUAL NOTES
K099-U1: The electronic DKA text has بالنسيان, “by forgetfulness,” where the embedded Kashshāf text in Ṭībī has باللسان, “by the tongue.” The latter is required by the discussion of literal speech and is followed here; the Turkish comparator supports it.
T099-U1: Printed XVI:536 confirms the nine-verse heading, as against DKA’s eight; no verse text is missing from this translation. The same page prints تطلع in the lexical gloss for jizʿāl. “Limps” is a provisional contextual correction to تظلع, explicitly distinguished from the printed reading. The word’s vocalization and gloss require a further lexical witness.
T099-U2: Printed XVI:539 confirms that the grammar discussion changes its illustrative name from ʿAmr to Zayd between examples. The translation preserves that change; its grammatical argument is not silently rewritten.
T099-U3: Printed XVI:545 reads بنصف القرآن, “as half the Qurʾān,” correcting the transcription’s بنف القرآن. This differs from the Kashshāf’s preceding four-recitation report; both authorial statements are retained.
T099-U4: The claims about unbelievers, major sinners, expiation, and mitigation of punishment are the positions quoted and debated by the authors. The quotation from al-Intiṣāf and Ṭībī’s description of al-Zamakhsharī’s intention remain separately attributed.

al-Qāriʿah · source and review notes
TEXTUAL NOTES
T101-U1: The Ṭībī chapter heading says ten verses, while the DKA heading says eleven. This difference has not been silently regularized.
K101-U1: The poetic imprecation is rendered literally in the base text, with its actual force of astonished praise explained in Ṭībī’s layer. The embedded base has yathūb, “returns,” where DKA has yaʾūb; the sense is the same.
T101-U2: Printed XVI:556 confirms khuzyā, “disgraced,” against the electronic transcription’s defective خِزْيا form. The season explanation and the author’s preference for the first interpretation are retained without editorial reconciliation.

al-Takāthur · source and review notes
SOURCE AND REVIEW NOTES
K102-U1: The difficult poetic citation involving ḍimād, a shared attachment, is translated together with Ṭībī's complete lexical explanation and alternate reading. Printed p. 560 was inspected and confirms the wording of that explanation; the compressed verse still requires comparison with further witnesses. Bracketed wording makes the supplied sense visible. The Turkish version construes the participants differently; its interpretation has not replaced Ṭībī's explicit gloss khalīl = husband.
K102-U2: K-DKA's transcription has a damaged phrase concerning conversion of wāw to hamza. The translation follows the grammatical explanation preserved in Ṭībī's quoted base and his citation from al-Zajjāj.
T102-U1: The image “the stain of clay” and “the herald of union” belongs to the quoted mystical definition in al-ʿAwārif; it is retained rather than replaced by an explanatory summary.
T102-U2: Ṭībī first notes evidence for a general questioning and then argues for the address's specific context. Both stages of his argument are preserved. The closing merit report and other reports await independent hadith review.

al-ʿAṣr · source and review notes
TEXTUAL NOTE
T103-U1: XVI:566 reads أرض واصية, “land whose vegetation is continuous”; the electronic text’s أوض is corrected from the scan. This is a transcription correction, not a translation of the modern editor’s apparatus.

al-Humazah · source and review notes
TEXTUAL NOTES
K104-U1: The third variant of “will be cast” is printed as an active verb with an object pronoun; the agent is not explained in the Arabic passage. The English preserves that indeterminacy rather than importing the Turkish comparator’s supplied subject, “wealth.”
T104-U1: The discussion of wa-ʿadadahu moves between a verbal analysis and an accusative nominal analysis with an omitted verb. The cold-water example illustrates an understood “gave to drink”; the compact source wording and both analyses are retained.
T104-U2: XVI:574 prints وأقر بدء إحراق كل أحد على قدر استحقاقه. “The commencement of each person’s burning is fixed according to what he deserves” is a provisional construal of this difficult phrase. The scan confirms the wording; further witnesses are needed.
T104-U3: The main text at XVI:576 reads يلزم, “would follow,” in its final sentence; the printed apparatus records a witness with لا يلزم, “would not follow.” This reverses the implication. The translation follows the printed main text and retains this unresolved variant explicitly; it does not silently attribute a settled doctrine of everlasting punishment for every slanderer to Ṭībī.

al-Fīl · source and review notes
TEXTUAL AND REVIEW NOTES
K105-U1: The conflicting elephant counts and the clause “and it was alone” occur in the printed base at XVI:578 as well as the transcription. The English marks the alternative rather than inventing a single consistent count.
T105-U1: The electronic wording says “with dotted ghayn” when explaining عرض, which begins with ʿayn. The intelligible word is rendered ʿurḍ; the letter-name needs scan/manuscript collation before finalisation.
K105-U2: The DKA poem reads عدوا where Ṭībī’s gloss explicitly discusses غدوا, “soon/tomorrow”; the latter is followed and the difference recorded.

Quraysh · source and review notes
TEXTUAL AND REVIEW NOTES
K106-U1 / T106-U1: The camel verse has zahw in the printed Dubai base and rahw in the DKA transcription. Both are preserved and explained. The difficult printed phrase الزهو غير الإدراك appears in the transcription as well; it is not securely established as a transcription error. The bracketed reconstruction of the cited verse and its relation to the adjacent lexical explanations require further fine collation before final publication. The provisional account above retains all the lexical alternatives rather than suppressing the difficulty.
T106-U2: The reading “al-Zubayr, from al-Zajjāj” occurs in the print; the modern apparatus notes a witness omission. It remains attributed as printed, without asserting a resolved identification.

al-Kawthar · source and review notes
TEXTUAL AND REVIEW NOTES
K108-U1: The first electronic Kashshāf witness prints أعطيناك despite immediately saying “with nūn.” The embedded Kashshāf in Ṭībī has أنطيناك, which supplies the intended dialectal form.
T108-U1: The electronic text drops the oath and the Prophet’s reply in the al-Rubayyiʿ report. The full words above are restored from printed XVI:603, visually inspected.
T108-U2: The printed phrase وأن المعطى لم يكن عظيما، إلا أن المعطي عظيم remains syntactically difficult. The provisional concessive rendering is retained with an explicit flag; do not silently rewrite it as an unqualified claim about the gift’s size. The following sentence expressly describes both gift and Giver as great.
T108-U3: The phrase ليدمج معنى معطى قطع النفس in the scan/transcription is also awkward. “So as to incorporate the meaning of cutting the soul off” renders the evident relation of sacrifice to self-denial, but the extra معطى needs further textual collation.
K108-U2: Jamʿ is Muzdalifa in the Arabic. The Turkish comparison’s phrasing places the dawn prayer at Minā; the English follows the Arabic distinction between Jamʿ and Minā.

al-Kāfirūn · source and review notes
SOURCE AND REVIEW NOTES
T109-U1: The working transcription drops “before” in the sentence on p. 608 asserting worship according to a revealed law before the mission, and contains minor damaged forms in the surrounding quotations. The scan was inspected: قبل المبعث is present in print and confirms the restoration. The complete closing pages 611–612 were also visually checked.
T109-U2: Ṭībī both preserves and criticizes al-Zamakhsharī's assertion about worship before the mission. The assertion has not been silently rewritten to agree with the criticism. Similarly, both positions on abrogation and all six explanations of the apparent repetition are retained.
T109-U3: Punctuation distinguishing al-Rāzī's quoted argument from Ṭībī's interventions is editorial in this translation. The interventions explicitly introduced as “I say” remain attributed to Ṭībī.
